PGE · PGE-PL · the new Memo conveyor's card of 1 November 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote behind each of the eleven ratings: PGE

The Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions and its 13 group judgements, 51,150 words, set out under the eleven ratings: each rating, then each of its lettered sub-items, then the answers beneath.

Word for word. Every answer is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written; a script checked that every word is on this page, in order. What is changed is layout only: answers are grouped by rating rather than by layer; each answer is titled with the question as it was set (the Investment Analyst's own heading appears beneath only where it adds words); an answer that belongs under more than one sub-item is shown in full once, at its first place, and as a one-line link everywhere else.

Which answer sits where comes from the same table the ratings map uses. Each answer carries its layer and group, because "positive" means something different in each layer. Each layer's own material (what it was asked, what its scores mean, the Investment Analyst's notes and appendices) is at the end, so nothing is left out.

The ratings alone: the ratings map.

How to read the scores and letters

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning (listed just below).

Where the Rater has scored this stock (every memo from 3 November 2026), the number in each pill is the Rater's, set after the memo was written from the Investment Analyst's judgements against a key per group of questions; the Investment Analyst's own score is kept in the pill's tooltip. The letter beside each score is read from the one canonical source for ratings, the same file the eleven ratings page and the memo scorecards read; nothing is graded on this page. The letter follows the item's rule: for most items this stock's rank among the stocks scored on the same scale on that same question or group judgement, re-cut each night (Richard's ruling of 4-Nov-26: every letter by rank; in the file's words: A above 90%; B+ above 82.5% to 90%; B above 75% to 82.5%; C+ above 66.67% to 75%; C above 58.33% to 66.67%; C- above 50% to 58.33%; D above 25% to 50%; F up to 25%); on the External change forces and Internal change forces items the number carries a sign (50 = neither positive nor negative) and the letter is the rank, so read the two together. “37th of 56” is the stock's place among them; a dashed outline marks a provisional grade (fewer than 30 stocks scored).

What the scores mean in each layer

Layer 1

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 2

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

Inputs

1

External headwinds/tailwinds

–not yet rated

Memo topic 1, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

External change forces

Layer 1 · External change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

PGE's external environment is at its best point of the cycle now and gets worse with time.

  • Now. In 2026 three things coincide: an energy shock lifts spreads on existing plants, capacity payments are locked to 2028, and grid demand accelerates. That explains the 21% rise in Q2 recurring EBITDA and the sharp upgrades to the brokers' estimates.
  • The asymmetry. The positive forces are cyclical or already priced. The grid tailwind is real but slow and formula-bound. The negative forces are dated and unpriced:
  • the end of coal capacity payments after 2028;
  • the state's appetite for revenue in an election year with a 7%-of-GDP deficit;
  • capital-goods inflation that erodes returns on new projects.
  • What drives it. Policy is the hinge variable: it sets the household tariff, the grid return, coal support and the levies.
  • Instruction to the Consumer. Build the model on two clocks: a regulated and contracted engine growing about 5% a year, and a merchant and conventional engine peaking in FY2026-27 and falling to a 2029 trough. Make policy, not the economy, the main scenario axis.
1a

External headwinds/threats

6 answers
C-0240C-0542C-0745C-0952C-3545C-3940
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

D4019th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: competitive intensity is rising wherever PGE is exposed to markets and is absent where it is regulated.

  • Generation: rising sharply and structurally. Solar, prosumers, storage and offshore entrants push coal down the merit order and cut daytime prices.
  • Retail: rising moderately for business customers; nil for households under the regulated tariff.
  • Distribution: nil (concession monopoly).
  • Capacity auctions: benign. PGE wins the most capacity.

Net: a moderate negative. The rising intensity hits the segments that are shrinking anyway (coal, merchant renewables, business supply) and spares the segment that is growing (networks).

Reasoning and evidence

  • Generation.
  • Coal fell from 95% of Polish output in 2005 to 61% in 2025 and 52.6% by late 2025.
  • Solar capacity rose from 0.6 GW in 2018 to 25.6 GW in February 2026, of which 13.3 GW is rooftop prosumer installations.
  • Poland had more than 450 hours of negative prices in 2025 and curtailed more than 1 TWh of renewable output.
  • Offshore entrants arrived: Orlen and Northland's 1.2 GW Baltic Power delivered first power in July 2026; Equinor and Polenergia have Bałtyk.
  • New owners: Public Power Corporation (277 MW from EDP), Calik (255 MW), Engie (250 MW of storage).
  • About 5 GW of batteries have won availability auctions in three years.
  • Retail.
  • PGE's share of retail supply has slipped to about 23% (early 2026) from about 25%.
  • Business volumes are falling (C-01). Third-party-access switching is rising.
  • Households are locked to the default supplier by the regulated G tariff, so price competition there is nil.
  • Capacity market. PGE won 1,859 MW in the supplementary auction for 2027 against Enea 1,595 MW, Tauron 1,410 MW and Energa 506 MW. Its 2024 five-year contracts for old coal units run to 2028, while most of Tauron's and Enea's capacity revenue ends after 2025.

Dimensions

  • Persistence: structural. Renewable build follows EU targets and the falling cost of solar; it is not a price war that can end.
  • Feedback loop: a reinforcing loop against coal. More solar raises balancing costs and cuts the hours coal runs. Fewer hours raise unit costs, which pushes earlier retirement.
  • Balancing loop that protects PGE: solar cannibalises its own daytime price, which slows merchant solar entry. Grid congestion blocks connections. Both slow new entry and protect PGE's dispatchable fleet in evening peaks.

For the model

  • Do not assume merchant renewable margins recover. Management cut its 2030 renewables-and-storage EBITDA target to PLN 3.8bn from PLN 5.3bn, and Bernstein forecasts PLN 2.6bn.
  • Model coal running hours down after 2027.
  • Model business supply margins thin; management's PLN 950m-a-year retail margin target is aspiration, not evidence.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

D4217th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: a mild headwind to operating costs and a material headwind to the cost and timing of the investment programme.

  • Operating costs: CO2 creeping up, gas spiking, but hard coal cheaper and largely passed through or hedged.
  • Investment programme: turbine, transformer and cable makers hold pricing power and long lead times, and a weaker zloty inflates the 35% of capital spending bought in foreign currency.

The operating effect largely washes through wholesale prices. The capital effect does not, because regulated and contracted returns are fixed once a project is approved.

Reasoning and evidence

  • CO2.
  • The weighted average allowance price paid rose to EUR 74.76 per tonne in 2025 from EUR 66.22. Prices averaged EUR 77 in January to May 2026.
  • Morgan Stanley sees EUR 60 to 90. PGE's own planning assumes +4% in 2026, +3% in 2027, then +12% a year to 2031 as reserve rules tighten.
  • CO2 cost PLN 15.7bn to 16.2bn in 2025.
  • Hedging is all-in-one: power, fuel, CO2 and currency are contracted together.
  • For the model, CO2 matters through the clean dark spread and the uncovered household tariff, not as a gross cost line.
  • Gas.
  • European gas rose 34% in H1 2026 on Middle East disruption.
  • Gas-fired generation still made record results because power prices rose more. District heating faces cost pressure unless tariffs adjust.
  • PGE now buys gas on the Polish exchange. It committed about PLN 2.8bn to LNG regasification slots for 15 years from 2030.
  • Coal.
  • Hard coal purchases cost PLN 2,185m in 2025, down 32%, on volumes down 16%. Utility coal prices fell to PLN 15.20 per GJ.
  • Polska Grupa Górnicza supplies over 70% of PGE's hard coal under a take-or-pay contract worth about PLN 13.5bn over 2023-2028.
  • Lignite is 100% self-supplied from Bełchatów and Turów (35.85m tonnes in 2025). That is a fixed-cost operation whose unit cost rises as volumes fall.
  • Russia blocked a rail route for Kazakh coal (62% of Polish coal imports, October 2026), a supply risk at the margin.
  • Capital goods.
  • Siemens Gamesa and Vestas raised turbine prices 40% to 45% since 2020.
  • Gas turbines are sold out to 2029-2030. Transformer lead times are about 200 weeks, with prices +81% since 2021.
  • Cable slots are the offshore bottleneck. Siemens Gamesa is the sole turbine supplier for Baltica 2 (107 turbines of 14 MW).
  • PGE signed the Rybnik and Gryfino gas peaker contracts with Siemens Energy and Polimex in March 2026 (about EUR 530m in total), which secures those slots.
  • Labour. Wage agreements were signed at over 85% of group employers. Parent payroll rose to PLN 443m from PLN 405m.

Dimensions

  • Trend: capital-goods inflation is persistent while orders exceed factory capacity.
  • Feedback loop: a balancing loop. High equipment prices delay projects (Baltica 3 was redesigned after missing return targets), which slows future capacity growth and keeps old capacity running longer.
  • Peers: Energa's Ostrołęka CCGT cost rose 14% to PLN 2.85bn and broke its loan timetable. PGE's Dolna Odra and Gryfino came in, which shows PGE executes better on gas.

For the model

  • Add a capital-cost contingency of 5% to 15% to projects not yet contracted.
  • Hold CO2 at EUR 75 to 90 with full forward hedging over 12 to 18 months.
  • Model district heating margins exposed to gas for one tariff cycle.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

C+456th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: macro forces act at three levels and bite hardest at the company and country level, through Poland's fiscal position.

  • Industry: global bond yields (the US ten-year above 5%) and European rates set the discount rate for every utility.
  • Sector: the gas-price shock re-rates owners of existing generation.
  • Company and country: Poland's fiscal stress is the force that matters most for PGE, through four channels:
  • Valuation: the risk premium on Polish state assets.
  • Politics: inflation turns into pressure for household price caps and windfall levies.
  • Cost of debt: borrowing for a PLN 117bn programme.
  • Regulated returns, with a lag: the allowed return is indexed to Polish ten-year bond yields averaged over 18 months. This is the one channel where fiscal stress helps PGE.

Reasoning and evidence

  • Poland.
  • The general government deficit is about 7.1% of GDP in 2026 and 2027, the highest in the EU, and the domestic bond market has sold off.
  • The central bank holds 3.75% until about September 2027; a hike is possible. Its 2027 inflation forecast was raised 145 basis points to 4.2%.
  • EUR/PLN is near 4.40, with Morgan Stanley's bear target at 4.47. GDP is +3.3% in 2026 and below 3% in 2027.
  • Mitigants at PGE.
  • Fixed-rate debt rose to 77% of the total in 2025 from 58%. Over 95% of debt is in zloty.
  • About PLN 14bn of loans carry 0.5% interest.
  • Baltica 2 revenue is indexed to the euro, which hedges its euro-denominated project debt.
  • Regulated-return channel.
  • In 2026 the distribution WACC was lower (bond yields fell after 175 basis points of rate cuts in 2025). Tauron's distribution return fell 1.34 points; PGE's distribution "benefited from a higher RAB despite a lower WACC".
  • The 2026 bond sell-off should lift the formula return for 2028 onward.
  • Sector and industry. The Fitness for Fighting digest is risk-off, with defensives leading and leveraged defensives selling; Utilities integrated is Overweight on the European power-price reading. Rate-sensitive utilities lagged as the US ten-year rose back above 5%.

Dimensions

  • Where it bites hardest: in the share price (country risk premium and positioning) and in policy (affordability politics before the 2027 election). The income statement is cushioned by fixed and cheap debt.
  • Interplay: a weaker zloty raises CO2 and imported capital-equipment costs in zloty but also raises zloty-indexed Baltica 2 revenue.

For the model

  • Use a marginal cost of new commercial debt of about 6% to 7%, against an average of about 4% to 5% on the existing book, with the 0.5% loans carried separately.
  • Hold the distribution WACC flat in 2027 and rising slightly from 2028; ask for research to confirm.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

–52

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: four other external dynamics matter, two positive and two negative. The net is slightly positive over six months and slightly negative over 18.

  1. Positive and cyclical: the gas-price shock. - The European gas shock of 2026 is being read by the market as a windfall for owners of existing generation and regulated networks (Industry Digest, 9 October 2026). - PGE was up 50% relative over 30 days and entered the stock pool on that reading. - It lifts 2026-2027 spreads but invites political clawback.
  2. Positive and structural: PGE is now the liquid way to own Polish grids. - Energa is now 95% owned by Orlen, after a capital increase to 95.24% on 1 July 2026 and a buyout offer at PLN 18.87, so it is no longer a listed way to own the story. - PGE is Poland's largest listed power company and a primary target for passive and regional funds, which supports flows.
  3. Negative and cyclical: rising bond yields and risk-off breadth. - The Fitness for Fighting digest reads risk-off beneath an index at its high, with leveraged defensives de-rating first and Fragility at Double Underweight. - A capital-intensive utility with rising debt is exposed.
  4. Negative and structural: the European Union's 2040 target. - A 90% emissions cut by 2040, ETS2 from 2028 (households and transport fuels) and shrinking free allowances for district heating tighten the path for Turów (to about 2045) and Bełchatów (mid-2030s). - This raises the probability of early closures without compensation.

Also relevant

  • The EU recovery fund deadline (2026): about EUR 28bn of Poland's EUR 55bn remained to be paid in early 2026. Cheap funding is front-loaded and will not be repeated after 2026.
  • Equipment bottlenecks moving from capital to grid to heavy equipment.

Dimensions: the windfall and the fund-flow effects are already priced (cohort at the 95th percentile). The yield and EU-target effects are not dated and could arrive suddenly.

C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

D4517th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: New entrants are entering fast in generation, flexibility and business supply. They are absent from distribution and household supply.

Entrant type Threat to PGE Who and what Strength
Adjacent High in offshore and large-scale generation Orlen (fuel major turned power major), foreign utilities (Public Power Corporation, EDP Renewables with a 14 GW European pipeline, Engie), developers (Northland, Calik) Bring capital and compete for sites, contracts for difference and supply-chain slots
Standard High in daytime wholesale; nil in grid Private solar and wind developers, prosumers Already took about 10 points of generation share; cut daytime prices
Orthogonal or innovative Medium now, high later for business retail Industrials self-supplying (on-site generation, small reactors, power purchase agreements), data-centre operators wanting their own supply, battery operators and aggregators They take PGE's most valuable business customers

The overall threat is concentrated in the two segments already in structural decline: coal output and business retail. The growth segment, distribution, gains from entrants because each one needs connecting.

C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

F4021st of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Suppliers are a source of risk on capital projects and a manageable risk on fuel.

Capital-goods suppliers hold pricing power and long lead times

  • Turbines +40% to 45% since 2020; gas turbines sold out to 2029-2030; transformers about 200 weeks and +81% since 2021; cable slots scarce.
  • Siemens Gamesa is the sole turbine supplier for Baltica 2. Original-equipment makers have shifted contract risk to developers.

PGE has secured its near-term slots:

  • Baltica 2: turbines, towers from Baltic Towers in Gdańsk, foundations by Van Oord, installation by Cadeler and Fred. Olsen, onshore substation by GE Vernova and Polimex.
  • Rybnik and Gryfino peakers: Siemens Energy and Polimex.
  • Żarnowiec battery: LG Energy Solution.

The risk applies to the next wave: Baltica 3 and 9, more storage, and grid equipment.

Fuel suppliers

  • Polska Grupa Górnicza supplies over 70% of hard coal under take-or-pay to 2028 (PLN 13.5bn over 2023-2028). Pricing is politically negotiated, and volume shortfalls trigger charges as coal burn falls.
  • Gas moved to the exchange in 2026.
  • LNG slots are committed from 2030 (PLN 2.8bn).

Local supply chain: improving (Smulders platforms, Baltic Towers), which reduces logistics risk.

For the model: capital costs for uncontracted projects with a 5% to 15% contingency. Take-or-pay coal costs carried as fixed to 2028.

1b

External tailwinds

7 answers
C-0160C-0345C-0438C-0866C-3252C-3738C-4355
C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

D6014th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: demand that matters for PGE's earnings is changing in two different ways.

  • Grid demand is growing structurally and is highly visible. This means connections, network capacity and the regulated asset base. It runs through 2030 and beyond.
  • Energy volumes are growing only cyclically. Power sold and generated rose in 2026 on weather, industrial strength and the energy shock, with under a year of visibility.
  • Business volumes in PGE's own retail book are shrinking structurally.

The structural part sits in the regulated half, which is about 40% of EBITDA. That makes the demand picture good for the regulated half and neutral to negative for the rest.

Reasoning and evidence

  • Grid demand: structural, and visible for two or more years.
  • In H1 2026 PGE signed more than 28,400 connection agreements for 766 MW of capacity.
  • It has 2 GW of data-centre connection requests. The Polish grid operator holds 13 GW of applications, though conversion is uncertain.
  • The strategy needs PLN 75bn of distribution investment to add 11 GW of renewable connection capacity.
  • Poland's power use is projected to reach 240 to 267 TWh by 2040, from about 170 TWh.

In a regulated network, demand for connections becomes asset-base growth. Asset-base growth becomes allowed return. So this demand converts into earnings with a regulatory lag of one to two years and high predictability.

  • Energy volume: cyclical.
Period Change Cause
2025 Gross national demand -0.9% (GDP +3.6%) Growth now less energy-intensive
Q1 2026 National demand +5.3% Cold weather (2.2°C colder)
Q2 2026 National demand +4% (+1.5 TWh); PGE distribution volumes +4% to 9.82 TWh; PGE generation +18% to 13.46 TWh Industry and exports; gas-price shock

The generation jump reflects the gas-price shock making coal competitive again, not underlying demand.

  • Business retail demand: structurally negative.
  • Q1 2026: Tariff A sales -25%, Tariff B sales -15%, total sales to end customers -6%. Q2 2026: retail supply volumes -5%.
  • Industrial clients are moving to self-generation and corporate power purchase agreements.
  • Management concedes that one large customer leaving can remove up to 1,000 GWh.

Dimensions

  • Skew over 12 months: negative for volumes. Industrial output was +4.3% in August against +6.1% expected. GDP slows below 3% in 2027 as EU funds fall away.
  • Feedback loop: a balancing loop works against the retail book. High network prices drive self-generation, which lowers grid-sourced volumes, which raises unit network costs. In distribution, volume loss is partly protected by tariff resets.

Peers

  • Enea showed the same mix in Q1 2026: household sales +33.6%, business sales -3.4%.
  • Tauron and Enea carry the same grid-demand tailwind.

For the model

  • Grow distribution EBITDA from the RAB (see C-08 and C-67), not from volumes.
  • Hold group generation volumes flat to down after 2026, with coal volumes falling as spreads normalise and units retire. Dolna Odra's last coal units go in 2026; Rybnik's in 2027-2028.
  • Model retail business volumes down 5% to 10% a year. Household volumes follow weather only.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

F4521st of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: the main disruption force is strengthening and acts on PGE in two directions at once.

  • The force: cheap distributed solar plus batteries plus flexible demand.
  • Against PGE: it destroys the value of the coal fleet and of unhedged daytime solar.
  • For PGE: it raises the value of the grid that must connect and balance it, and of dispatchable and storage capacity.

Net over 18 months: roughly neutral. Grid gains are regulated and slow; coal losses are protected by capacity payments to 2028. Over four years: negative before it turns positive. Coal loses its capacity payments in 2029, while the storage and grid gains build gradually.

Reasoning and evidence

  • Against PGE.
  • Rooftop solar produced 45% of Poland's 20 TWh of solar output in 2025. That cut daytime grid demand and displaced base-load coal.
  • PGE impaired PLN 248m of renewable assets in Q2 2026 because negative-price hours lower solar values.
  • Carbon Tracker estimates new solar or onshore wind is now cheaper than running PGE's existing coal plants.
  • For PGE.
  • Connection queues and congestion make the network the bottleneck. The grid is the scarce asset, earning a regulated return of up to 12.96% including reinvestment premiums.
  • Storage revenue in management's plan rises from PLN 0.2bn in 2027 to PLN 1.2bn by 2035. Żarnowiec battery (262 MW) is due in Q2 2027; Gryfino battery (400 MW / 800 MWh) in 2029.
  • A second-order threat to PGE's gas plans. The Sector Digest's rule is that batteries collapse capacity prices wherever they bid into the same auction. Poland's capacity market is open to storage. The value of new gas peakers after 2028 is therefore exposed. Management has already made new gas conditional on capacity support beyond 2028 and cut its 2030 gas EBITDA target to PLN 2.6bn from PLN 4.0bn.
  • Data-centre demand is an upside disruption with weak foundations.
  • PGE has 2 GW of requests and plans PLN 6bn of investment, with a claimed 23% return on invested capital.
  • Bernstein warns Polish power is too expensive and too carbon-intensive for hyperscalers. The Sector Digest calls the request queue a possible "artificial bubble".

Dimensions

  • Trend: strengthening; storage and prosumer additions are accelerating.
  • Peers: Energa (93% to 98% smart meters, 9.6 GW of renewables connected to its grid) is better placed to earn from flexibility. PGE's metering lag (35% to 47%) delays demand-side products.
  • Cone of outcomes: widest in 2029-2032, when coal capacity payments end and storage revenues are still small.

For the model

  • Hold renewable EBITDA near the brokers' level: PLN 1.8bn to 1.9bn in FY2026-27, rising with Baltica 2.
  • Do not add data-centre earnings before 2029.
  • Treat the value of gas capacity after 2028 as conditional.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

F3825th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: political and regulatory change is the dominant force on PGE, it is under way on several fronts at once, and its net effect is negative to mixed.

  • Supportive: the grid formula, the offshore contracts for difference, capacity payments to 2028, and cheap state-linked funding.
  • Adverse:
  • the state reversed the coal carve-out, which leaves the coal liability permanently on PGE's balance sheet;
  • the household tariff is set below cost;
  • support for coal after 2028 is unfunded;
  • the controlling shareholder now wants cash dividends during peak investment;
  • a windfall clawback and an election-year price intervention are live risks.

Reasoning and evidence

  • The coal carve-out reversal.
  • The Ministry of State Assets abandoned the plan to move coal plants into a national agency (NABE) on 9 July 2025, citing a PLN 53.8bn funding gap to 2040. A change of minister (Wojciech Balczun) followed.
  • Consequences: the coal impairment of PLN 8.65bn to 8.97bn, the PLN 12,050m loan impairment at parent level, and a 2030 plan with coal EBITDA at minus PLN 3.3bn.
  • The CFO says support for coal after 2028 has "unresolved issues".
  • The Researcher's verdict (24 October 2026): the clean-up has been "reversed rather than delayed".
  • The household tariff.
  • The regulator approved an average household tariff of PLN 495.16 per MWh that does not cover costs. Management expects a PLN 560m loss in 2026.
  • PGE Obrót held a PLN 327m onerous-contract provision at March 2026. Supply recurring EBITDA fell 50% in Q1 2026.
  • High wholesale costs point to a significant tariff revision in January 2027, but the government has hinted at price caps.
  • Capacity market.
  • The European Commission has allowed capacity payments for coal units above 550 g CO2/kWh only to 2028. The 2027 supplementary auction was a temporary lifeline.
  • Fitch: capacity payments are likely to be "insufficient to keep coal-fired generation" profitable after 2028.
  • Supportive measures.
  • Distribution returns are set by formula on the regulated asset base.
  • Offshore contracts for difference (Baltica 2 indexed from PLN 319.6 to 443.49 per MWh, 2021-2025; Baltica 9 at PLN 489) and an amended Offshore Wind Act.
  • About PLN 14bn of state-linked loans at 0.5% interest; PLN 21bn from the national recovery plan; PLN 1.8bn of non-repayable grid subsidies in 2026.
  • The state's fiscal appetite.
  • The State Assets minister expects "significantly higher dividend income" from state companies (3 September 2026). PGE's dividend resumption fits that agenda.
  • The state also taxes some 2026 fuel revenues more than 20% above average at 60%. It forced generators to pay into the Price Difference Fund in 2022-2024 (PGE booked a PLN 605m provision for those settlements).

Dimensions

  • Persistence: structural. Five of six earnings drivers are set by a regulator or the state.
  • Skew: negative in 12 months. Dated events: the 2027 household tariff (December 2026); the run-up to the autumn 2027 parliamentary election, which raises the pull towards household price caps and windfall levies; continued silence on post-2028 coal.
  • Positive tail: a negotiated coal separation would remove a liability Bernstein values at minus PLN 12.3bn (minus PLN 20bn with decommissioning and pensions). It has no date.

Peers

  • The same applies to Tauron and Enea, which are more exposed.
  • Public Power Corporation shows the alternative: a state below 34% ownership, a coal exit by 2026 and a re-rating to 14.6 times earnings.

For the model

  • Make policy the scenario axis:
  • a household tariff loss of PLN 0.3bn to 0.6bn a year in 2027-2028;
  • a windfall-levy scenario taking PLN 0.5bn to 1.5bn of 2027 coal and gas EBITDA;
  • post-2028 coal EBITDA with and without replacement support.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

C+668th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: yes, one sector-level driver is clearly accelerating demand for PGE's most valuable business.

  • The driver: grid build-out with regulators allowing high or rising returns, pushed by electrification, renewable connections and data-centre requests.
  • Where it helps: it accelerates growth in PGE's distribution asset base.
  • The limit: this acceleration is visible to the market and already in the price. The Sector Digest says the grid half of the state-utility cohort is valued as grid owners, at the 95th percentile.

Reasoning and evidence

  • Volume of investment.
  • Combined grid spending at the four Polish utilities rose from PLN 3.3bn to PLN 14bn (Bernstein: PLN 11.8bn in FY2024).
  • PGE plans more than PLN 4bn a year to 2030 (PLN 37bn in FY2025-FY2030; networks are 31% of capital spending to 2030 and 33% to 2035). It targets a doubled asset base by 2035.
  • Returns.
  • The base allowed distribution return is 7.48% pre-tax real.
  • With reinvestment premiums, PGE expects its return on the asset base to reach up to 12.96% in FY2025.
  • The Sector Digest gives an 8.3% reference.
  • Cohort context.
  • Tauron's asset base is PLN 26.6bn at a 9.5% allowed return; Enea borrowed PLN 9.13bn from the state development bank for grids.
  • Distribution EBITDA growth is about 5.6% a year to 2030 in Bernstein's reading of PGE's plan.
  • Proof in the numbers. Distribution recurring EBITDA +12% in Q1 2026 (PLN 1,477m). FY2025 distribution EBITDA PLN 5,314m, 41% of group recurring EBITDA.

Dimensions

  • Persistence: about ten years of visibility from the network plan.
  • Risk: the allowed return is formula-set and fell in 2026. A regulator lowering the WACC before new assets earn is the early-warning signal in the Business Foundations report.
  • Constraints: equipment lead times and skilled labour, not demand, cap the pace.

For the model

  • Distribution EBITDA of about PLN 5.4bn to 5.6bn in FY2026, rising 4% to 6% a year to FY2030 (about PLN 6.5bn to 7.0bn by 2030).
  • The brokers' flat PLN 5.2bn to 5.3bn looks too low unless the allowed return falls further.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

F5219th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Customer demand supports the regulated thesis and undermines the retail thesis.

Customer group Behaviour Fragility
Distribution customers Connections rising (28,400 agreements, 766 MW in H1 2026; 2 GW of data-centre requests); distribution volumes +4% in Q2 2026 Low
Households (5.36 million, 91.5% of distribution customers) Captive and stable but served below cost; 11% more Tariff G volume in Q1 2026 on cold weather Politically fragile: affordability is an election issue
Business customers Leaving: Tariff A -25%, B -15% in Q1 2026; total sales to end customers -6% Structural, not cyclical: self-generation and power purchase agreements are cheaper than grid supply at high Polish network prices
Heat customers Stable but face falling demand from efficiency rules and demographics (management monitors "the risk of overestimating future heat demand") Moderate

Interplay: the business migration has a second-order benefit. Customers who self-generate still need grid connection and backup, which keeps the regulated asset base and connection fees growing. It also has a cost: it raises network tariffs per kWh for remaining customers, which accelerates migration. That is a reinforcing loop on the retail side.

For the model:

  • Retail supply EBITDA stays weak: PLN 0.2bn to 0.4bn in FY2026-27, against PLN 0.21bn and 0.32bn from the brokers.
  • It improves only if the 2027 household tariff covers costs.
  • Distribution is unaffected by retail migration.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

F3825th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Regulators and politicians are doing four things at once. In net they hurt, and the position is fragile.

Helping

  1. Funding and protecting the grid. - WACC-based tariffs with reinvestment premiums. - Recovery-plan loans at 0.5%. - PLN 1.8bn of grid subsidies in 2026. - The Grid Act tightening speculative connection queues, which helps genuine projects.
  2. Supporting offshore and dispatchable capacity. - The offshore three-tier price system (PLN 485.71 to 512.32/MWh). - An amended Offshore Wind Act compensating redispatch. - Capacity auctions with 17-year contracts for new gas. - Relaxed coal-stock rules for retiring units (June 2026).

Hurting

  1. Shielding households at utilities' expense. - A below-cost G tariff: PLN 495.16/MWh, and an expected PLN 560m loss. - Historical freezes and fund contributions. - The government hinting at price caps for 2027.
  2. Leaving coal on utilities' balance sheets. - The coal agency abandoned. - Post-2028 support unresolved; the EU derogation runs only to 2028. - EU climate policy (2040 target, ETS2, Turów's long life) raises the cost of keeping it.

Fragility: high. Everything above can change by decree or tariff decision. The 2027 tariff decision and the 2027 election are the dated tests. National regulators can also force utilities to keep loss-making units running "in the public interest". Compensation is legally due, but slow and uncertain.

C-43

How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

F5522nd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: The addressable market is large relative to PGE, but the growth it allows is set by regulated investment, not by market share.

How fast each market grows

  • Power demand: about 170 TWh now, 240 to 267 TWh by 2040, about 2% to 3% a year.
  • Grid investment: about PLN 14bn a year across the four main operators.
  • Storage: 5 GW won availability auctions in three years.
  • Data-centre power: 173 MW (2023) rising to over 500 MW (2030).
  • Offshore: about 3.4 GW of Phase II contracts for difference awarded in December 2025, on top of Phase I.

What it allows PGE

  • Regulated asset base: 7% to 9% a year (doubling by 2035).
  • Regulated and contracted EBITDA: 5% to 8% a year to 2030. Distribution about 5.6% a year in Bernstein's reading.
  • Group EBITDA: about 3% a year to 2030. Management's PLN 15bn from PLN 12.9bn means +3.1% a year, because coal falls by about PLN 5bn.
  • Long run: about 7% a year to 2035 on management's ambition (PLN 30bn or more), or about 2% on Bernstein's PLN 16bn.

The binding constraint is capital and regulatory approval, not demand. PGE can deploy capital as fast as the regulator recognises it, the supply chain delivers it and the balance sheet funds it.

For the model: group EBITDA growth of about 0% to 2% a year in FY2026-2029 (the coal decline offsets regulated growth). Faster after 2030 if coal is resolved.

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

F5220th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Judgement: PGE is near the top of its earnings cycle.

  • Merchant and conventional earnings are at or near a cyclical peak in 2026-2027 and should turn down in 2028-2029.
  • Regulated and contracted earnings are early in a long upswing that runs to 2035.

The turn in the conventional cycle is dated, not guessed: the 2027 supplementary capacity contract lapses after 2027 and the five-year coal contracts end in 2028.

Reasoning and evidence

  • Signs of the peak.
  • Q2 2026 recurring EBITDA +21% to PLN 3,963m, with record district heating and gas-fired results.
  • Generation +18% and conventional units running more. Group revenue +10.6% in Q2 2026 after -32.8% in 2024 and -4.7% in 2025.
  • The brokers expect coal EBITDA of about PLN 2.1bn in both FY2026 and FY2027, falling to PLN 1.08bn in FY2028. Management's plan has minus PLN 3.3bn in 2030.
  • Fitch expects conventional generation performance "to normalise" from its high levels.
  • The rising cycle.
  • Distribution capital spending above PLN 4bn a year, with the asset base doubling by 2035.
  • Baltica 2 first power in 2027 (all 111 foundations installed on schedule by August 2026).
  • Rybnik CCGT capacity payments from 2027; district heating EBITDA target raised to PLN 3.4bn for 2030.

Dimensions

  • Cone of outcomes: narrow for FY2026 (H1 is banked at PLN 8.1bn). Wider for FY2027, which depends on spreads and policy. Widest for FY2029-2030 (coal support).
  • Skew: negative after FY2026.

For the model

  • FY2026 recurring EBITDA of about PLN 13.3bn to 13.8bn.
  • FY2027 flat to slightly down: about PLN 12.8bn to 13.8bn.
  • FY2028 supported by Baltica 2: about PLN 13.0bn to 14.5bn.
  • FY2029 the trough risk: about PLN 12.0bn to 14.0bn, depending on coal support.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

D4817th of 25

Layer 1 · External change forces

Each thematic driver in turn (Sector and Industry Digests)

Driver Direction for PGE Strength now Notes Timing
Grid asset growth with rising allowed returns Tailwind Strong Weakened slightly by the lower 2026 WACC Already priced
Long contracts for power available at any hour Tailwind Strong to end-2028 Becomes a headwind in 2029 unless replaced (2027 supplementary 1,859 MW; Rybnik CCGT 17-year; gas peakers PLN 0.5bn a year from 2030); exposed to batteries in open auctions Cliff in 2029
Eastern state utilities valued as grid owners Tailwind Spent Next leg needs coal separation, which has no date Already priced
Governments taking generators' profits Headwind Moderate and rising Poland's 2022-2024 Price Difference Fund precedent, a 60% levy on some 2026 fuel revenues, a 7%-of-GDP deficit and an election in 2027 Dated to 2026-2027
Batteries entering flexibility markets Headwind (gas peaker value) / opportunity (PGE storage) Moderate Storage target 18 GWh by 2035 2028 onward
Building beyond the industry's cash flow Headwind Moderate Capital spending exceeds operating cash flow through 2027; supplier pricing power raises costs Through 2027
Bond yields Headwind Moderate Valuation, not income: debt is fixed and cheap Ongoing
Energy-shock windfall for existing assets Tailwind Strong now Cyclical; fades as gas normalises 2026-2027

At each level of the value chain

  • Suppliers are strong; that is a headwind.
  • Customers: households are captive but regulated; business customers are leaving. Mixed.
  • Industry: competition is rising in generation; a negative.
  • Regulators and politicians: supportive for grids and offshore, hostile for coal and household prices. Net negative.

Overall judgement

  • Next six to 12 months: the external picture is moderately positive. The cyclical windfall, the locked capacity payments and regulated growth outweigh the headwinds. This is why the earnings estimates rose.
  • Over 18 months to four years: it turns negative-to-neutral. The windfall fades. The coal capacity payments end after 2028. Political clawback risk peaks around the 2027 election. Rising capital costs compress returns on new projects.
  • The tailwinds that remain after 2028 (grid, offshore, storage) are real but regulated, slow and largely priced.
  • The headwinds after 2028 (the coal cliff, policy) are large and not dated in the market.

Internal change forces

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group. The rating above stands for it, so it carries no number of its own.

The internal story is positive in direction and limited in reach.

What has improved

  • Under a new chief executive (January 2026), with a steady finance chief and a minister who wants cash and discipline, PGE has:
  • cut and focused its capital plan;
  • made new gas conditional on support;
  • rebased its 2030 target to a credible level;
  • resumed dividends;
  • kept its main projects on schedule.

What has not changed

  • Ownership, governance and the coal liability are the things that set PGE's valuation discount and its post-2028 earnings risk. None has changed.
  • Leadership and strategy reset whenever the minister changes, and the next dated risk is the autumn 2027 election.

For the model

  • Assume a dividend in 2027.
  • Treat post-2027 payout and strategy as policy-dependent.
  • Carry a recurring allowance for "one-off" charges.

Internal risks

Group judgement

Risks

F3523rd of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

PGE's risks are not about a slowing core. The core is cyclically strong. They are about three things:

  1. A dated cyclical turn: spreads normalise in 2027, the supplementary capacity revenue lapses after 2027, and coal capacity payments end in 2028.
  2. Policy shocks: a windfall levy, household tariff caps, coal support denied, state-directed capital. The energy shock, a 7%-of-GDP deficit and the 2027 election make these more likely in the next 18 months than usual.
  3. Sentiment: an extended share price at the upper-middle of its own range, in a crowded cohort at the 95th percentile, in a risk-off market. Rating changes already lag estimate upgrades.

Downside to a fair trough is about 30% to 45%. The dividend and the grid's standalone value cushion it.

For the model: run a "policy downside" case alongside the base case:

  • a levy of PLN 1bn a year in 2027-2028;
  • a household tariff gap of PLN 0.5bn a year;
  • coal EBITDA without support from 2029.
C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

F4525th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: modest improvement in capital and project discipline and in disclosure, little improvement in operational standards and cost discipline, and no improvement in governance.

Improving

  • Project execution.
  • Baltica 2's 111 foundations were installed on schedule (May to August 2026).
  • The 1.36 GW Dolna Odra CCGT and the 1.34 GW Gryfino CCGT were delivered; Dolna Odra contributed PLN 470m of EBITDA in 2025.
  • The Turów Unit 7 dispute was settled (a PLN 135m provision release).
  • Rybnik CCGT is "progressing well".
  • Capital selectivity: spending to 2035 cut by 10.6%; gas made conditional.
  • Financial risk management: fixed-rate debt raised to 77%; an all-in-one hedging framework (power, fuel, CO2, currency at a 1:1 ratio); economic net debt reported.
  • Grid quality targets: SAIDI (outage minutes) to fall 30% against the 2019-2024 average; 100% remote meters by 2030.
  • Digital: a new CRM for 1.2 million customers; over 70% of new contracts electronic.

Not improving

  • Smart meters: 35% to 47% at end-2025 against Energa's 93% to 98%; 304,000 installed in H1 2026.
  • Collections: receivables days rose to 42 in 2025 from 40, against Tauron's 24.
  • Costs: overheads rose PLN 107m in Q1 2025 on inflation. Employee turnover rose to 10.2% from 8%.
  • Labour: 156 unions cover about 28,000 employees; union density 67%; collective agreements cover 82%.
  • Reverse factoring of PLN 1,574m keeps a debt-like item outside reported net debt.

Governance (unchanged or worse)

  • The state holds 60.86% and appoints a supervisory board member directly.
  • A 10% voting cap applies to every shareholder except the state.
  • Board members have been dismissed at the state's request.
  • Executives hold no shares and receive no equity pay.
  • The Remuneration Committee met three times in 2025.

For the model: assume no structural cost savings beyond natural retirement in the coal division. Assume operating costs grow with wage inflation (4% to 6%). Assume project delivery on schedule for contracted projects (Baltica 2, Rybnik, Żarnowiec), with a 6 to 12 month delay probability on uncontracted ones.

C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

F2825th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the negatives are larger and more certain than the positives in C-20.

Present

  1. Coal stays on the balance sheet with a dated cliff. It falls from about PLN 2.1bn of EBITDA in 2026-2027 (brokers) to minus PLN 3.3bn in 2030 (management), and post-2028 support is unresolved.
  2. Retail is loss-making on household tariffs. A PLN 560m loss is expected in 2026, and business customers are leaving to self-generation.
  3. Leadership churn and a culture that cannot hold talent. Three presidents in 23 months; staff turnover 10.2%; pay caps; no equity alignment.
  4. The dividend begins while free cash flow is negative. The likely driver is the state's budget, not surplus cash.
  5. Write-downs keep recurring. - 2015: PLN 8.8bn coal impairment. - 2023: PLN 761m onerous-contract provision. - 2025: PLN 8.65bn to 8.97bn coal impairment, a PLN 766m onerous provision, a PLN 605m Price Difference Fund provision, a PLN 316m retail onerous provision. - 2026: a PLN 248m renewables impairment in Q2.
  6. Capital commitments are swelling. Future commitments rose to PLN 21.4bn at March 2026 from PLN 15.1bn; gas commitments to PLN 6.5bn from PLN 1.8bn; plus about PLN 2.8bn of LNG slots from 2030.

Plausible

  • State-directed support for coal mines. A medium-low probability on precedent: the 2015-2016 support for Kompania Węglowa and PGG cost PGE value.
  • Nuclear cash calls under sole ownership.
  • A windfall levy or revenue cap on 2026-2027 generation profits.
  • A new government in late 2027 rewriting strategy and payout.
  • Further impairments of solar and onshore assets as negative-price hours rise.
  • Capital-cost overruns on uncontracted projects.
  • Easement claims on distribution land after a Constitutional Tribunal ruling.
  • Turów's lignite life to about 2045 clashing with the EU's 2040 framework.

For the model: carry a recurring "one-off" allowance of PLN 0.3bn to 0.8bn a year below recurring EBITDA. That is the historical norm.

C-59

How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?

F2825th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system?

Judgement: The culture is not congruent. Goals are set by the state (energy security, social peace, affordability, investment). Strategy aims at earnings quality and dividends. Incentives reward stability and policy delivery. Execution runs through a bureaucratic, union-dense organisation.

  • The organisation: 156 unions; 67% density; collective agreements at 82%; tripartite teams for coal regions; decisions centralised and multi-layered.
  • Labour reduction: relies on natural retirement.
  • Visible friction:
  • staff turnover rose to 10.2%;
  • no employee engagement score is published (Enea publishes 77);
  • three presidents in 23 months.
  • Strengths: labour peace and supply continuity, so no strikes; a clean compliance record (28 investigations, no confirmed corruption); safety focus.

Interplay: the misalignment shows when commercial and political goals diverge (household tariffs, coal closures, mine support). Then the culture follows the state.

D-03

In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?

F447th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: highly concentrated. Two root causes generate nearly all of the difference: the run-off of one segment (coal), and one capital programme whose costs (depreciation, interest, cash) arrive before its returns. A third, smaller cause is the state taking more (tax, a possible levy, the dividend). Inside the 18-month window the drivers are more numerous and smaller, so the concentration bites after the window, not within it.

The difference to be explained

  • FY2020 to FY2025: EBITDA more than doubled (FactSet operating PLN 5.1bn in FY2021 to PLN 11.4bn; recurring PLN 12.9bn in FY2025). Free cash flow averaged about zero. There was no dividend. Net debt stayed near 0.3 times EBITDA.
  • FY2026 to FY2030 (my figures): EBITDA flat, then lower (PLN 13.6bn to PLN 12.3bn to 12.8bn). EPS halves (PLN 2.76 to PLN 1.31). Free cash flow is about minus PLN 23bn in total. A dividend is paid. Net debt rises about eightfold.

The EPS bridge, FY2026 to FY2029 (PLN per share)

Step Effect Root cause
FY2026 adjusted EPS 2.76
Lower EBITDA (minus PLN 1.32bn), after tax -0.44 Coal
Higher depreciation (plus PLN 1.5bn), after tax -0.50 Capital programme
Higher net financial items (plus PLN 1.1bn), after tax -0.37 Capital programme and dividend
Tax rate 23% to 25.5% -0.08 The state
FY2029 adjusted EPS 1.37

Inside the EBITDA line the concentration is extreme

  • Coal falls by PLN 3.25bn (plus 1.85bn to minus 1.40bn).
  • Everything else adds PLN 1.93bn: offshore plus 1.10bn; distribution plus 0.40bn; supply plus 0.35bn; onshore renewables and storage plus 0.25bn; other plus 0.13bn; heat minus 0.05bn; gas minus 0.25bn.
  • A segment that is 14% of FY2026 EBITDA produces two and a half times the net decline in group EBITDA.

Against history, the drivers are the same two hands, now withdrawing

  • The rise in EBITDA from 2021 to 2025 was also concentrated. Distribution rose from about PLN 3.5bn to 5.3bn as the regulator's return and premium rose in 2022 to 2025. The capacity market paid PLN 2.2bn to 2.7bn a year to the coal fleet.
  • The forecast differs from history mainly because those two state-granted supports reverse (the premium normalises; the capacity payments end), and because the bill for the capital programme arrives.
  • So the concentration is not only in few drivers. It is in few decision-makers: the regulator, the State Treasury and the European Commission.

Inside the 18-month window the picture is diffuse

  • FY2027 EBITDA against FY2026 (a net fall of about PLN 0.15bn to 0.2bn): coal minus 0.40bn, gas minus 0.25bn, heat minus 0.25bn and railway minus 0.03bn, against offshore plus 0.35bn, supply plus 0.15bn, renewables plus 0.10bn, distribution plus 0.10bn and other plus 0.10bn. No single move exceeds PLN 0.4bn.
  • FY2027 EPS against FY2026 (minus PLN 0.41): about 80% comes from depreciation, interest and tax, which are scheduled, not uncertain.

What concentration means for predictability

  • It helps monitoring. Few things need watching, and most have dates.
  • It hurts robustness. After 2028 one decision (support for coal) moves EPS by 25% to 40%. A diffuse set of drivers would average out; this cannot.
  • It makes the errors correlated. A government that grants a low premium is likely to be the government that caps the household tariff and levies generators. The downside drivers share one cause, affordability politics before the autumn 2027 election.

(Continued in part 2.)

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

F4223rd of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: leadership has changed a great deal, and the change signals that the controlling shareholder, not the board or management, sets the direction.

  • In 23 months PGE has had three presidents (to March 2024, Marzec, Lubera), a dismissed vice-president, and supervisory board members dismissed at the state's request.
  • The finance chief has stayed throughout. That continuity anchors capital discipline.
  • Read the current team as competent but politically mediated. Expect it to change again after the autumn 2027 parliamentary election if the government changes.

Reasoning and evidence

  • Timeline.
Date Change
March 2024 Marzec appointed, after the government changed
8 December 2025 Marzec and a vice-president (Górski) removed. Supervisory board member Dariusz Lubera delegated to act as President, then appointed 15 January 2026
19 January 2026 Katarzyna Rozenfeld named Vice-President for Operations (more than 30 years at PwC, Vattenfall, EDF, Tauron)
19 May 2026 Vice-President Robert Kowalski dismissed
8 June 2026 Piotr Dziubałtowski joins as Vice-President
4 December 2025 Two supervisory board members dismissed at the state's request (Extraordinary General Meeting)
January 2026 Two supervisory board members appointed (Krężel, Wróbel)
June 2026 The Minister of State Assets appoints Andrzej Kozyra to the supervisory board
  • Continuity. CFO Przemysław Jastrzębski (since July 2024; former investment banker at Pekao and Santander) and regulatory Vice-President Marcin Laskowski (since March 2024) remain.
  • The driver was the ministry, not the electoral cycle.
  • The December 2025 reshuffle came under the same coalition government. It followed a change of State Assets minister in mid-2025 (Wojciech Balczun), the abandonment of the coal agency in July 2025, and the minister's later call for higher dividends.
  • The pattern: a minister changes, then the strategy changes, then management changes.
  • Lubera's profile.
  • He ran Tauron from 2008 to 2015, through its 2010 listing, so he is an industry insider. He moved directly from PGE's supervisory board into the executive role, which blurs oversight.
  • His first ten months produced the October 2026 strategy: lower capital spending, a lower 2030 target, a dividend, and selective gas investment.

Dimensions

  • Persistence: a structural feature of state ownership. Average tenures are short; succession plans are not visible; the pay cap is about PLN 0.75m a year with no equity.
  • What it signals for the investment case:
  • Positive near term: the current minister wants cash returns and capital discipline, and the board has delivered both.
  • Negative over four years: strategic continuity cannot be assumed past the 2027 election, and each reset has historically arrived with impairments or a new capital plan.

For the model: use a policy-break scenario in late 2027 to 2028. A new government could rewrite dividend and capital spending plans. This applies to the dividend from FY2027 earnings onward.

C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

D6017th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the priorities changed materially in October 2026, from volume growth to earnings quality, and the change is clearly stated and well funded.

  • Stated: segment-level EBITDA targets for 2030, a split of capital spending, and a leverage ceiling.
  • Funded: cash of PLN 14.2bn, preferential loans, project finance for Baltica 2, and leverage near 1 times.
  • But the strategy cannot resolve the coal problem, which is the largest value item. Management controls the regulated growth half and does not control the coal half.

Reasoning and evidence: what changed (October 2026 against June-July 2025)

Item June-July 2025 October 2026
Capital spending to 2035 PLN 235bn PLN 210bn
Capital spending to 2030 over PLN 120bn PLN 117bn
2030 group EBITDA target PLN 17bn PLN 15bn (Bloomberg consensus PLN 15.2bn, Bernstein PLN 14.6bn)
2030 gas EBITDA PLN 4.0bn PLN 2.6bn, and new gas conditional on capacity support beyond 2028
2030 renewables and storage EBITDA PLN 5.3bn PLN 3.8bn
2030 district heating EBITDA PLN 2.7bn PLN 3.4bn
2035 EBITDA ambition over PLN 30bn unchanged

Also new in October 2026:

  • Networks become the largest investment area (31% of spending to 2030, 33% to 2035), aiming to double the regulated asset base by 2035.
  • More than 70% of capital spending is to be backed by regulated returns or contracts for difference. About 63% of 2030 EBITDA is to be regulated or contracted.
  • Dividends resume from FY2026 earnings, targeting a yield of up to 5%. Net debt stays below 3.5 times EBITDA.
  • New lines: PLN 6bn for data centres and industrial zones by 2035; 18% of capital spending to M&A and options, including PLN 3bn for district heating network purchases.

Quality of the change

  • Choices are explicit and conditional. Gas only with post-2028 support; Baltica 3 redesigned rather than forced through. That is good strategy design under uncertainty. Bernstein calls the envelope "realistic and logical".
  • Funding is in place. Baltica 2 has PLN 11.1bn of non-recourse finance plus a PLN 3.9bn state development bank loan. Other funding: the PLN 3.89bn Rybnik term loan (November 2026), a PLN 2.25bn EIB loan, and about PLN 14bn at 0.5%.
  • Limits.
  • The 2035 target (PLN 30bn or more) is still roughly double Bernstein's PLN 16bn and depends on unproven nuclear, small reactors and offshore options.
  • The 2030 target assumes coal EBITDA of minus PLN 3.3bn, so the rest of the group must grow from about PLN 11.5bn (FY2026, excluding coal) to about PLN 18bn (+9% a year).
  • The strategy was rewritten 16 months after the last one, which says plans follow ministers.

For the model: use the October 2026 segment targets as the upper bound for 2030. Use Bernstein's figures (group PLN 14.6bn; gas PLN 1.6bn; renewables PLN 2.6bn; district heating PLN 2.5bn) as the lower bound. Model the ex-coal engine at 6% to 8% a year rather than 9%.

C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

C-5510th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: mid-term targets were cut, not raised, but the cut makes them credible, and one new commitment was added.

  • Cut: the 2030 EBITDA target fell to PLN 15bn from PLN 17bn, and the renewables and gas targets fell.
  • Raised: district heating went to PLN 3.4bn from PLN 2.7bn.
  • Added: a dividend with a target yield of up to 5%.

In "choices" terms this is a rebase to realism plus a cash return. It is positive for credibility and neutral for the earnings path.

Reasoning and evidence

  • No annual guidance. PGE never guides a single year. The new 2030 figure sits on top of the Bloomberg consensus (PLN 15.2bn), so it de-risks the medium term rather than setting a beat-and-raise path.
  • The 2035 ambition is unchanged at over PLN 30bn. It is the stretch figure and has no bearing on the 18-month model.
  • The dividend is the one raised commitment, and it surprised the market. Bernstein and Bloomberg consensus had assumed no dividend. The conditions differ between the two strategies:
  • June 2025: a dividend only with recurring net profit, two years of projected positive free cash flow and an investment-grade rating.
  • October 2026: "subject to leverage and coal market conditions".

The brokers' free cash flow is negative for FY2026-FY2027, so the conditions have effectively been loosened, consistent with the state's fiscal need. Dividends are to be calculated on net profit excluding non-cash impairments.

For the model

  • No near-term guidance to beat.
  • Dividend per share of about PLN 0.55 to 0.80, paid in 2027 from FY2026 earnings (the brokers have PLN 0.55, then PLN 0.45 paid in 2028).
  • Payout of roughly 25% to 30% of adjusted net profit, against roughly 3.5% to 5% of market value.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

F2525th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: very little. The biggest intended exit, moving the coal fleet to a state agency, was cancelled in July 2025, so the perimeter is not shrinking.

  • The group is shedding coal only by gradual retirement of individual units.
  • It is pruning projects (Baltica 3's original design, part of the gas pipeline) rather than businesses.
  • Meanwhile it is adding new activities: offshore, storage, nuclear preparation, data centres, heat-network purchases.

The net perimeter is widening, not narrowing.

Reasoning and evidence

  • Unit retirements. Dolna Odra's last two coal units go in 2026. Rybnik units 5 and 8 go in 2026 and units 6 and 7 by end-2027, though Bernstein reports Rybnik's operation extended to 2028 for system security.
  • Coal remains at the core. Bełchatów phases out by the mid-2030s, but Turów's newer 450 MW unit runs towards 2045.
  • Organisational simplification: the Conventional Generation division was renamed Coal Energy (2025); the nuclear company became PGE Energia Jądrowa (February 2026); the internal Group Code was revised for faster decisions.
  • Talks without a structure or date. Talks with the state on carving out lignite and hard coal after 2028 continue (CFO, January 2026). The Key Question report finds no agreed terms since July 2025. The government took "almost three years" to adopt its last energy strategy.

Interplay: without a carve-out, the low-return unit cannot be exited. It can only be run for cash while capacity payments last and then closed. Closure is constrained by union and regional politics (tripartite teams for Bełchatów and Turów). This is the central structural weakness that separates PGE from Public Power Corporation, which exits lignite by end-2026.

For the model: keep coal fully consolidated through 2030. Include decommissioning and reclamation cash costs (provisions rose PLN 435m in 2025) and the take-or-pay coal contract to 2028.

F5520th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: PGE is entering several new product markets, all in Poland. None is geographic. The sense of each move varies.

Offshore wind Sensible Close to the core, regulated by contracts for difference. Baltica 2 1.5 GW (50% with Ørsted); Baltica 9 975 MW at PLN 489/MWh; 350 MW F.E.W. For the model:

  • Include offshore (Baltica 2 from 2027) and storage (Żarnowiec Q2 2027, Gryfino 2029).
  • Exclude data centres and nuclear from EBITDA before 2029.
  • Include nuclear preparation spending of about PLN 0.1bn to 0.2bn a year.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

F2524th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible?

Judgement: PGE is not raising prices in real terms in any business it controls. Its "prices" are set by regulators, auctions or contracts. Increases are nominal at best and in households negative.

The one real gain is regulatory: the reinvestment premium that lifts the return on the asset base above the base WACC, from 7.48% real to up to 12.96%. It rewards investment, not pricing.

Management's PLN 950m-a-year retail margin target for 2030 is latent, not demonstrated.

Reasoning and evidence by business

Households Below cost G tariff PLN 495.16/MWh; PLN 560m expected loss in 2026; supply EBITDA -50% in Q1 2026. Political pressure: 79% of Poles expect the transition to bring stable or lower prices; 40% say prices have cut purchasing power

Interplay: the absence of real pricing means margin expansion must come from mix (more regulated and contracted assets) and volume (more connections and asset base), not price. That is why the strategy is built on capital spending.

C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement: M&A is stepping up modestly and sensibly. The deals are small, close to the core and in renewables, offshore and nuclear; the larger heat-network plan is still to come. There is no evidence yet of large or undisciplined acquisitions. Integration risk is low because deals are mostly projects, not operating companies.

Reasoning and evidence

  • Deals so far.
Date Deal
October 2025 ZE PAK's 50% of the nuclear company

  • the joint bid for EDF's Polish assets (2016-2017);
  • the Opole coal expansion (2013).

For the model: include small bolt-ons inside capital spending. Exclude large acquisitions. Run a sensitivity on PLN 3bn of heat-network purchases from 2027-2028 at about 6 to 8 times EBITDA (adding PLN 0.4bn to 0.5bn of EBITDA).

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

B625th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: yes, decisively, on both counts. PGE is moving from a cash-hoarding, no-dividend balance sheet (no dividends for FY2016-FY2018 or for FY2019-FY2024; net debt about 0.3 times recurring EBITDA at end-2025) to one that:

  • funds roughly PLN 15bn a year of capital spending;
  • pays a dividend from FY2026 earnings, targeting up to 5%;
  • lets leverage rise toward, but below, 3.5 times.

There will be no buybacks: the state would not allow them and the free float is 39%.

Reasoning and evidence

  • Path.
  • Capital spending rises from PLN 11.5bn in FY2025 to about PLN 15bn in FY2026 (brokers).
  • Free cash flow per the brokers is minus PLN 5.1bn in FY2026 and minus PLN 3.1bn in FY2027.
  • The dividend cash cost is about PLN 1.2bn to 1.8bn a year: at PLN 0.55 to 0.79 per share on 2,243.7m shares.
  • Economic net debt therefore rises from about PLN 15bn (1.1 times) to about PLN 25bn to 30bn by end-2028, roughly 1.8 to 2.2 times EBITDA.
  • Credit. Fitch downgraded PGE to BBB in January 2025. Its downgrade trigger is 3.7 times.
  • Dilution precedent. Shares rose from 1,869.8m to 2,243.7m in 2022, when a state-backed equity raise funded the transition. With a PLN 210bn programme, a further equity raise is possible later in the decade if leverage nears 3.5 times. Not before 2028.

Interplay: the dividend serves the controlling shareholder's fiscal need. It is likely to be paid while the minister's agenda holds, but it is borrowed money during negative free cash flow. It is a candidate to be cut if leverage, coal losses or a new government change priorities. The 2016 precedent: the state used its vote to cut the proposed dividend from PLN 0.92 to 0.25.

For the model:

  • Dividend PLN 0.55 to 0.80 a share in 2027, flat to lower in 2028.
  • Net debt rising about PLN 5bn to 7bn a year in FY2026-FY2027.
  • Share count constant at 2,243.7m.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

–60

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: four further positives are present and one is plausible.

  1. Cheap, secure funding. - About PLN 14bn of recovery-plan loans at 0.5% to the distribution company; PLN 21bn of recovery-plan funding in total. - PLN 1.8bn of non-repayable grid subsidies in 2026; the PLN 3.89bn Rybnik loan (November 2026); Baltica 2 project finance. - This lowers the cost of capital on regulated assets that earn formula returns. The spread between allowed return and funding cost is unusually wide for the funded tranche: about PLN 0.7bn to 0.8bn a year of interest saved against market rates.
  2. Contract coverage. - Rybnik CCGT (882 MW) has a 17-year inflation-indexed capacity contract worth about PLN 300m a year from 2027. - The two gas peakers (600 MW at Rybnik and Gryfino, completion about 2030) have PLN 0.5bn a year of 17-year indexed payments. - Baltica 2 has a 25-year contract for difference. - Regulated and contracted EBITDA is planned to be about 63% of the 2030 total, against 38% in 2020.
  3. Operational recovery in heat and gas. - Record district heating (Q1 2026 recurring EBITDA +25% to PLN 1,122m) and gas (Q1 EBITDA doubled to PLN 179m; record Q2). - The 2030 heat target was raised; Gdynia's 50 MWe gas engines arrive in 2026; Kraków's PLN 886m gas CHP is under construction.
  4. Better reporting. Recurring EBITDA, economic net debt, segment disclosure and preliminary results let the market see the regulated core. This removes one source of the discount.

Plausible, the largest: a negotiated coal solution with the state after 2028. Options include a strategic-reserve mechanism, state compensation for units kept open in the public interest (already legally required) or a new carve-out vehicle. This would remove the negative-value business. It is possible because the state needs dispatchable capacity, the law obliges it to compensate forced operation, and the EU is pressing. The probability within 18 months is low (about 10% to 20%) and there is no clock.

C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

F4224th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: the only controlling actor is the Polish state (60.86%), and it has signalled change in four directions.

Signals so far

  1. It will not take the coal plants. The coal agency was abandoned on 9 July 2025, leaving the liability with the utilities.
  2. It wants more cash from state companies. The State Assets minister expects significantly higher dividends (3 September 2026).
  3. It wants energy security and investment. It supports offshore, nuclear (the second plant agreement, October 2026) and grids, and backed PGE's management change.
  4. It is still discussing coal solutions beyond 2028, with no structure announced.

There are no activists. The 10% voting cap that excludes the state makes activism impossible. CVC-style private investors (as at Public Power Corporation) are absent.

Interplay

  • Scope for change is high; its direction is unpredictable. Three of the four signals help minorities in the near term (dividends, investment, a possible coal solution). One hurts permanently (no agency).
  • The state's fiscal stress cuts both ways. It explains the dividend. It also raises the risk that the state uses PGE as a tax or funding vehicle: windfall levies, mine support, consolidation of weaker peers.
  • The 2027 election is the dated point at which the controlling actor's preferences could reset.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

F5224th of 25

Layer 1 · Internal change forces

Judgement: moderately strong, reasonably broad, and well resourced financially, but shallow in two places that matter.

Strong and well resourced

  • The October 2026 strategy is coherent: earnings quality, regulated focus, conditional gas, capital discipline.
  • The dividend is genuine and novel.
  • Funding is secured and cheap.
  • Key projects are on schedule.

Shallow

  1. The largest problem is outside management's power. The coal liability depends on the state and the EU.
  2. The human and governance resources are weak. Leadership churn, pay caps, no equity alignment and staff turnover make delivery dependent on political continuity.

The changes improve the regulated and contracted half of the group, which is already valued by the market. They leave the coal half, the source of the discount and of the 2029 earnings risk, untouched. This is an "improving operator inside an unchanged ownership and asset problem" story.
