Foundations

3

Value chain dynamics and industry structure

D4819th of 25

The Investment Analyst's group judgement: Industry and Value Chain

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

Industry and Value Chain

D4819th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

PGE leads a concentrated, state-owned industry. Concentration buys positional stability, not pricing power, because every price PGE charges is set by a regulator, an auction or the exchange.

Two value chains

  • The network chain: monopoly, formula-priced, growing with every renewable connection and data-centre request. Disruption strengthens PGE here.
  • The generation and retail chain: new entrants, cheap renewables and batteries, self-supplying industry and political household pricing erode PGE here structurally. Only capacity payments protect it, and they end in 2028.

Competitors and suppliers

  • Competitors are benign and weaker than PGE.
  • Suppliers hold the power on capital goods.

The real "shark" is the common owner and regulator. It has shown reactive, situation-dependent behaviour: freezes, levies, the coal agency reversal. It faces an election in 2027 and a 7%-of-GDP deficit.

History says rallies built on spreads and policy hope mean-revert. Lasting re-ratings (Public Power Corporation, RWE and E.ON) required ownership or asset separation that PGE does not have.

For the model: a regulated network valued on growth in its asset base, a generation business run for cash into a 2028 cliff, a loss-making household book, and policy as the main source of variance.

3a

Value chain dynamics

6 answers
C-3252C-3345C-3460C-3738C-3845C-3940
C-32How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?F5219th of 25full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

F4524th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: PGE owns most of its route to market:

  • default supplier to 5.73 million customers;
  • its own distribution network;
  • exchange sales;
  • Kotsovolos-style channels are absent, but PGE is building corporate power purchase agreements and "Energy-as-a-Service".

The intermediaries that matter are the regulator, the grid operator (PSE) and the power exchange (TGE). All three are gatekeepers that currently hinder more than help.

The three gatekeepers

  • PSE (the transmission operator) controls connections and balancing.
  • Transmission is "the biggest bottleneck"; connection queues are clogged with speculative projects.
  • PICASSO balancing integration since July 2025 sent balancing prices between minus PLN 45 thousand and plus PLN 48 thousand per MWh.
  • A new "Alpha" penalty for forecasting errors is coming, and MARI integration is due in Q4 2026.
  • These raise balancing costs for PGE's renewables and reward its flexible plants.
  • The regulator sets the household tariff (a hindrance) and the distribution tariff (a help).
  • The exchange gives transparent pricing, and PGE moved its gas purchases there in 2026.

PGE's own channel weakness: smart meters at 35% to 47% against Energa's 93% to 98%. This limits dynamic tariffs, flexibility products and demand forecasting. It is being fixed, with 100% targeted by 2030.

Fragility: moderate. Regulatory gatekeepers can change rules quickly; the Grid Act brought stricter connection rules and shorter validity.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

B604th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: On balance, existing competitors' behaviour helps PGE now. It is not fragile within 18 months. It becomes more contested after 2028.

Rivals are financially constrained or distracted

  • Tauron: leverage rising from 1.9 times (2025) to 3.9 times by 2030; capacity revenue cliff after 2025; 1,410 MW of supplementary capacity for 2027.
  • Enea: highest coal and mining exposure; leverage limit 3.0 times; distribution WACC falling from 9.35% to 7.5% by 2028.
  • Energa: now an Orlen subsidiary. Its CCGTs at Ostrołęka and Grudziądz are delayed, and it broke the Ostrołęka loan timetable.

Where rivals are active

  • Tauron: 582 MW of batteries under construction; renewables target 6.1 GW by 2035.
  • Orlen: offshore (Baltic Power live; Baltic East 900 MW at PLN 476.88/MWh).
  • Polenergia and Equinor: Bałtyk at PLN 492.32/MWh.

These raise competition for supply-chain slots and dilute future arbitrage margins for storage. They do not threaten PGE's core earnings.

Cooperation

  • Joint project structures with Tauron for auctions.
  • Shared LNG terminal slots with Orlen, Enea and Unimot (August 2026).
  • These show coordinated, not hostile, behaviour.

Fragility after 2028: gas peakers and storage from all four groups will compete in the same capacity and flexibility auctions. Batteries in particular depress clearing prices.

C-37What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?F3825th of 25full answer under 1b External tailwinds
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

F4520th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Disruption acts on every actor in PGE's value chain. It leaves PGE's network position robust and its generation and retail positions fragile.

Actor How disruption acts on it Effect on PGE
Demand (households, industry) Prosumers and self-generation shrink grid-supplied volumes but raise connection and backup needs Negative for retail, positive for grid
Competition Zero-marginal-cost renewables and batteries lower prices and spreads; coal is pushed out of the merit order Negative for coal and merchant solar
Regulators and politicians EU climate law forces coal exit and raises CO2 cost; national politics protects households; regulators must fund grids Mixed
Suppliers Global demand for turbines, transformers, cables and gas turbines gives makers pricing power and long lead times Negative for capital-spending returns
The system operator Rising balancing needs and new penalties Negative for renewables, positive for flexible assets (gas, storage)

Fragility: the regulated network, about 40% of EBITDA, is strengthened by disruption. Generation and retail, about 35% to 40%, are weakened. The heat and railway businesses, about 25%, are broadly neutral. The disruption increases the importance of the one business whose return is set by politicians, which makes PGE more dependent on regulators over time.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

B725th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: PGE is first in Polish generation and in retail supply, and second in distribution.

Activity PGE share Rank Nearest rivals
Generation 31% to 33% of national output; 19.1 GW (18.8 GW in another source) 1st Enea 11% to 12% (6.3 to 6.4 GW); Tauron 6% to 8% (5.3 GW); Energa 2%
Retail supply 23% to 25% (5.73 million customers) 1st Tauron 19% to 21%; Enea 18% to 19%; Energa 13% to 15%
Distribution (by volume) 29% (5.86 million customers over 129,938 km², about 40% of Poland's territory) 2nd Tauron 36% to 37% (6.05 million customers); Energa 16%; Enea 14%

Its generation lead over Enea is about 2.5 to 3 times. Its retail lead is small. In distribution, the gap to Tauron is set by fixed concessions, so it cannot be closed.

What the ranking means for the model

  • Generation leadership is a shrinking asset. It is mostly coal and lignite, and the share is falling as private renewables grow.
  • Retail leadership is a liability in households. The regulated tariff is below cost, so a bigger household book means a bigger loss.
  • The second place in distribution is the valuable position. It earns the regulated return and grows with connections.
  • Scale matters most in the capacity market. PGE won 1,859 MW in the 2027 supplementary auction against peers at 506 to 1,595 MW. It matters also in funding capacity (lowest leverage of the peer group) and in system importance to the state.

Peers: Tauron has the larger grid but higher leverage, heading to 3.9 times by 2030. Orlen, through Energa, is the challenger, with capital and offshore ambition.

C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

C-6210th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: All three markets are highly concentrated and steep.

Market Concentration
Generation Top four state groups (PGE, Enea, Tauron, Energa) hold 52%. Adding Orlen's own gas plants and ZE PAK puts the top five at about 55% to 60%
Distribution Four state operators plus Stoen (E.ON) in Warsaw hold over 95%. Shares fixed by concession
Retail Top four hold about 75% to 80%

The next five generators are each well under 3%, so the top five hold far more than five times the share of numbers six to ten.

The important qualification: concentration here does not produce market power over price.

  • Distribution and household tariffs are set by the Energy Regulatory Office.
  • Wholesale prices are set on the exchange by the marginal plant. PGE's scale lets it influence availability, but it cannot set the price.
  • Capacity payments are set by auction.

So concentration supports stability of position rather than margin. The owner of all four leaders is the same state, so the oligopoly behaves more as an arm of policy than as a profit-seeking cartel.

Trend: generation concentration is falling. Coal's share fell to 52.6% of output in late 2025. Prosumer and private solar now produce about 12% of generation (20 TWh of solar in 2025). Distribution and retail concentration are stable.

C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

F4819th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Stability differs sharply by activity.

  • Distribution: perfectly stable (concessions).
  • Retail: fairly stable. PGE drifted from about 25% to 23%, mostly business customers leaving through third-party access and self-supply.
  • Generation: unstable, with share moving structurally from the state groups to new entrants.

Reasoning and evidence: new entrants in generation, 2021-2026

Entrant type Evidence
Solar Capacity grew from about 4 GW in 2021 to about 25 GW by end-2025. Prosumers produce 45% of solar output. Solar was about 12% of national generation in 2025, all from outside the state groups' core fleets
Offshore Orlen and Northland (Baltic Power, 1.2 GW, first power July 2026); Equinor and Polenergia (Bałtyk)
Foreign utilities and funds Public Power Corporation (277 MW), Calik (255 MW), Engie (250 MW batteries), EDP
Batteries About 5 GW won availability auctions in three years

New entrants took roughly 10 to 15 points of generation share from coal-based incumbents in five years. PGE's share fell from about 37.9% of capacity (an earlier ING figure) to 31% to 33% of output.

Dimensions

  • Persistence: structural and continuing. The 2030 plan has renewables at over 50% of Polish output. Poland plans to cut hard-coal capacity from 18.5 GW to 6 to 7 GW by 2030.
  • Balancing loop: grid congestion and negative prices slow new merchant entry, as noted under C-02.

For the model: PGE's generation volumes should fall gradually after the 2026 cyclical rebound. Distribution volumes grow with the economy and connections. Household volumes are stable; business volumes fall.

C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

D5816th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Historically, Polish power consolidated towards the state champions by political design:

  • the 2006-2007 creation of the four groups;
  • PGE buying EDF's Polish assets (2017) and PKP Energetyka (2023);
  • Orlen buying Energa (2020-2026).

In generation, share now flows away from the largest incumbents to private renewables. In capacity and contracted assets, it flows to whoever has the balance sheet and the auction skill.

Who are the advantaged now? Owners of grid, of contracted dispatchable capacity and of capital.

  • PGE has all three: second-largest grid, most capacity contracts, lowest leverage.
  • Orlen has capital, offshore ambition and Energa's grid.
  • Tauron and Enea are weaker (balance sheets and capacity cliffs).

So PGE is among the advantaged in the parts of the market that are consolidating. It is the most exposed incumbent in the part that is fragmenting (coal generation).

Evidence

  • PGE took 1,859 MW of the 5.8 GW supplementary 2027 capacity, the largest share. Its 2024 five-year contracts run to 2028, while Tauron and Enea lost most capacity revenue after 2025.
  • PGE won Baltica 9 in the December 2025 offshore auction and bought RWE's F.E.W. Baltic II.
  • Fitch: PGE has the lowest leverage among Polish peers and more debt capacity than Enea.

Interplay: a possible future consolidation, of Tauron or Enea assets into PGE by state direction, would be a negative for PGE minorities. The Deutsche Bank note on government consolidation plans gives the precedent. It would be positive only if priced fairly. There is no current proposal.

C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

D5015th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading What is competitors' attitude to competition: margin and return first, or volume first; benign or loss-tolerant; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: Domestic competitors are state-owned and policy-first, not profit-first. That makes them benign towards each other in retail and auctions but tolerant of losses where the state asks.

  • They accept below-cost household tariffs.
  • They ran loss-making coal plants for system security.
  • Energa built Ostrołęka C coal, cancelled at large cost.

Private and foreign entrants are return-driven (Ørsted's Baltica 3 hurdle of 150 to 300 basis points over WACC) but volume-focused in solar.

Orlen is the aggressive actor: capital-rich, expanding in offshore, gas and retail, and consolidating Energa.

Prices and volume: yes, industry prices fall when volume demand falls, mechanically. The exchange price is set by the marginal plant, and solar oversupply produced 450 or more negative-price hours in 2025. No incumbent can defend price by cutting output.

Evidence of a shift to margin discipline: the Business Foundations report finds utilities moving from volume-focused customer acquisition to protecting margins on high-value commercial accounts. Trading targets increasingly include profitability. Capacity auctions show rational bidding; PGE contracted old coal under five-year agreements at good prices.

For the model: no price war risk in retail or capacity from peers. The price risk comes from the exchange (renewable cannibalisation) and the regulator.

C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

F3822nd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

The Investment Analyst's heading How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over 18 months to three years: is there 'Michael O'Leary' instability, and would competitors launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: Competitors' attitude is stable. The instability sits with the common owner and the regulator, not with rivals.

The state's behaviour over three years is reactive and situation-dependent:

  • emergency household price freezes and revenue caps (December 2022 to 2025, PLN 500/MWh household cap);
  • forced contributions to the Price Difference Fund;
  • the coal agency announced, delayed and abandoned (July 2025);
  • management reshuffles;
  • a below-cost 2026 household tariff.

That is exactly the "hard to predict, situation-dependent" pattern the question describes.

The 'shark attack' on pricing comes from the state. It acts when it senses public pain from high prices, not when it senses a weak competitor. The 2026 energy shock and the 2027 election create precisely those conditions. Poland has already applied a 60% levy to some 2026 fuel revenues.

Peer behaviour: Tauron and Enea are financially weaker and less able to attack. Orlen could compete hard in business retail and offshore auctions but has not undercut household prices. Metlen in Greece shows the alternative pattern: an aggressive private challenger taking share with promotional pricing. No such challenger exists in Poland because household prices are regulated.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

D5216th of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: The structure is supportive in about 55% to 60% of PGE's earnings and unsupportive in the rest. The support is legally strong but politically fragile.

Part of the business Share of EBITDA Structure
Distribution about 40% Concession monopoly with formula return; supportive and durable
District heating about 18% Regulated, local monopoly networks; supportive
Railway energy about 9% Supportive
Generation about 25% to 30% Price-taker in a market losing scarcity to renewables; coal's support (capacity payments) ends in 2028
Household retail Loss Below cost by regulation

Fragility

  • The supportive half rests on regulatory formulas that the same state can alter: WACC cuts (Tauron -1.34 points), tariff caps, levies. Wisdom Library: "State-granted moats are absolute and fragile."
  • The 2026 WACC cut shows the formula can move against PGE within a year.
  • The protection is only as good as the government's need for grid investment. That need is strong for at least the next five years, which limits the fragility in practice.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

F4523rd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Substitutes are a rising threat to three PGE products and an opportunity for one.

Threats

  1. Grid-supplied electricity for households: rooftop solar plus home batteries (13.3 GW of prosumer solar).
  2. District heat: individual heat pumps, building efficiency rules and demographic decline. PGE is answering with heat-network integration, electrode boilers, heat pumps and 0.5 GWh of heat storage, and raised its 2030 heat target. Bernstein is less confident (PLN 2.5bn against management's PLN 3.4bn).
  3. Gas and coal peaking power: batteries.

Opportunity

  • The grid connection itself has no substitute. Every alternative still needs a connection and backup, which strengthens the regulated business.

Trend: accelerating for solar and batteries; slower for heat (municipal networks are hard to replace in dense cities).

For the model: district heating EBITDA of PLN 2.0bn to 2.4bn a year in FY2026-2028 (brokers PLN 2.4bn, 2.0bn, 2.2bn). The upside to PLN 3.4bn by 2030 is a management target, not an assumption.

4a

Business quality

D5219th of 258 answers
Judgement52C-4158C-4265C-4355C-4440C-4532C-4638C-4755
Group judgement

Company - Competitive advantage

D5219th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

PGE's competitive advantage is concentrated in one place: a state-granted distribution monopoly whose regulated return compounds as the energy transition forces grid investment.

  • Scale adds funding power, auction strength and system importance. These let PGE grow that monopoly faster than its peers and win contracted dispatchable capacity.
  • Elsewhere PGE has no pricing power. Its customer offer is not demonstrably superior (business customers are leaving and metering lags). Its historic production advantage (own lignite) is becoming a liability.
  • Mechanics favour PGE's mix every year. They also run the coal fleet into a dated cliff.

The quality of the advantage is high but entirely regulatory. Its durability depends on the state continuing to need grid investment more than it needs cheap household power.

C-41

Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

D5817th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: PGE has one strong and widening moat, two moderate moats, and two former advantages that are turning into liabilities.

Advantage Type Strength Direction Evidence
Distribution concession Regulatory Strong Widening A legally designated monopoly: 5.86 million customers over about 40% of Poland's territory. Its return is set by formula on invested capital, up to 12.96% with premiums. Every new connection, renewable plant and data centre adds to the asset base, which is planned to double by 2035. This is a "state-granted moat": absolute while the state values grid investment, fragile if the formula is cut (as in 2026)
Scale and system importance in dispatchable generation Scale and political Moderate Peaking 19.1 GW, the largest fleet and the one the grid needs on windless winter evenings. It earned the most capacity contracts (five-year contracts to 2028; 1,859 MW for 2027). It gives PGE first call on state support (17-year contracts for new gas, preferential loans). It narrows as coal retires and batteries enter
Balance sheet and funding access Scale and financial Moderate Narrowing slowly as it is spent Lowest leverage among Polish peers (Fitch). About PLN 14bn of 0.5% loans; PLN 11.1bn of Baltica 2 project finance. It funds the asset-base growth that is the real moat. It narrows as spending lifts leverage toward 3.5 times
Own lignite mines Production Former advantage Turning into a liability 100% of lignite needs, about 70% of conventional fuel, from Bełchatów and Turów. Once a cost advantage; now a fixed-cost, carbon-heavy liability with reclamation obligations, a Turów life to about 2045 and a falling load factor
Default-supplier status in households Customer Former advantage Captive but loss-making The lock-in holds customers whom the regulator makes PGE serve below cost

Culture and knowledge: an offshore capability is being built through Baltica 2 with Ørsted, a new PGE Baltica chief executive with 20 years' experience, and the F.E.W. Baltic II team. There is no culture-based moat, as Management shows.

Net: PGE's moat is narrowing to its regulated core and widening there. The model should treat the network as a compounding regulated asset and the rest as competitive or declining.

C-42

How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

C+658th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: PGE is the largest Polish utility by most measures.

Measure PGE Peers
Revenue (2025) PLN 61.4bn Tauron and Enea well below; Energa PLN 22.8bn (2024)
Recurring EBITDA PLN 12.9bn Tauron PLN 7.5bn (2025); Enea about PLN 6.8bn (2024); Energa about PLN 3.3bn (2024)
Generation capacity 19.1 GW Enea 6.3 to 6.4 GW; Tauron 5.3 GW
Employees 41,690 n/a
Market value about PLN 35bn Tauron about PLN 16bn; Enea about PLN 11bn

PGE is about 1.7 times Tauron by EBITDA. It is small against European majors (Iberdrola, Enel, RWE).

What scale confers

  • Funding capacity: it can carry PLN 117bn of spending to 2030 without equity. Peers cannot (Tauron to 3.9 times; Energa needed Orlen's equity).
  • Auction power: the largest capacity wins.
  • System and political importance: first call on state funding; "too important to fail".
  • Procurement weight with turbine and grid-equipment makers. It secured slots in 2025-2026.
  • Ability to run many programmes at once: offshore, storage, heat, nuclear.

What scale does not confer

  • Lower unit costs in competitive markets.
  • Pricing power: prices are regulated or set by the exchange.
  • The bounded-market condition: scale gives no cost edge against private renewables.

C-44

How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

F4024th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Inferiority cannot be disproved in the areas customers can compare. In the monopoly areas the question does not apply. Customer utility there is set by regulation and reliability.

Where PGE is demonstrably inferior or at risk

  • Business customers are leaving for self-generation and power purchase agreements because network and supply costs are high (Tariff A -25%, Tariff B -15%). That is revealed preference against PGE's offer.
  • Smart metering trails peers (35% to 47% against Energa's 93% to 98%), so PGE cannot offer the dynamic tariffs and flexibility products competitors sell. Enea already offers dynamic pricing; 55% of its customers use e-invoices.
  • Receivables days lengthened to 42 from 40, against Tauron's 24.

Where PGE is acceptable or improving

  • Grid reliability is improving (a 30% SAIDI reduction targeted).
  • 70% of new contracts are digital; a new CRM is in place.
  • PGE avoided the over-billing disputes Tauron faced under the price cap.
  • Rail traction supply contracts are renewed: PGE Energetyka Kolejowa will supply 2 TWh in 2026-2027, of which 602 GWh is renewable.

Households: they receive more than they pay for. The tariff is below cost, so the surplus is transferred to them by the regulator rather than earned by PGE.

For the model: no premium pricing. Business supply share keeps falling.

C-45

To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

F3223rd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility?

Judgement: PGE has almost no commercial pricing power. Its "pricing" is administered. The evidence of application is in bids and regulatory submissions, not in price lists.

  • The clearest skill is in capacity auctions: securing five-year coal contracts in 2024 and the largest 2027 supplementary award at PLN 325.56 to 372.04 per kW-year.
  • The second is in offshore auctions: Baltica 9 at PLN 489/MWh, near the PLN 492.32 maximum observed.
  • The third is in using the regulatory asset-base formula with reinvestment premiums.

Where pricing power is absent: household tariffs (below cost; the regulator rejected PGE's requested level) and business supply (customers leave).

Pricing power across conditions

  • Inflation: good for contracts for difference (CPI-indexed) and grid (nominal WACC). Bad for household tariffs (regulatory lag).
  • Falling wholesale prices: bad for merchant generation, but good for retail margins under a fixed tariff.
  • Political stress: bad, through caps and levies.
C-46

To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

F3823rd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto?

Judgement: PGE does not share scale economies as a strategy. "Scale economies shared" describes a voluntary choice to cut prices and build loyalty, and PGE's prices are not its own to set.

  • De facto, value flows to customers in two ways:
  • involuntarily, through below-cost household tariffs (a PLN 560m expected loss in 2026);
  • through the regulated tariff formula, which passes efficiency gains to customers at each tariff reset.
  • What PGE retains is the reinvestment premium on the asset base, not a scale saving.
  • Messaging and values focus on energy security and transition, with no customer-value promise.

There is no reinforcing customer loop here. The loop that does exist is political: lower prices, then voter approval, then more state pressure for lower prices.

C-47

To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

D5514th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Competitive advantage

Judgement: Mechanical forces are strong at PGE. They improve the regulated half and degrade the coal half with every period that passes. The net is positive for mix and quality, neutral to negative for total earnings until about 2030.

Positive mechanical forces (need no heroics)

  • The regulated asset base compounds with every zloty spent at a formula return: 7.5% to 9% a year.
  • Contracts for difference index revenue to inflation (Baltica 2 +39% in four years).
  • Capacity contracts fix revenue (Rybnik CCGT 17 years; gas peakers 17 years).
  • Retiring coal units remove fixed costs and carbon (Dolna Odra, Rybnik).
  • Assets enter service on contracted schedules (Baltica 2 in 2027, Żarnowiec in Q2 2027, Gryfino battery in 2029).

The regulated and contracted share of EBITDA rises mechanically: 38% in FY2020, 41% distribution alone in 2025, about 63% by 2030.

Negative mechanical forces

  • Coal capacity payments expire in 2028 (dated).
  • Lignite load factors fall, so unit costs rise.
  • Free CO2 allowances for heat shrink, and the EU cap tightens.
  • Depreciation rises as new assets enter service.
  • Interest rises as net debt grows.

Against peers: PGE's mechanical position is better than Tauron's or Enea's, whose capacity cliff came earlier and whose balance sheets cannot fund the same asset-base growth. It is worse than Public Power Corporation's, whose coal exit is complete by end-2026.

For the model: let the mechanical forces drive the forecast. Grid asset growth (asset base +7% to 9% a year; EBITDA +4% to 6%). Contracted additions on schedule. The coal cliff at end-2028. EPS falling slowly as depreciation and interest rise faster than EBITDA.

Company - Management

Management is competent, honest in its reporting and good at delivering contracted, state-backed projects.

  • Current direction: a sensible, conditional strategy built around the regulated core and a dividend.
  • Constraints:
  • a controlling shareholder that changes leaders and strategies with its ministers;
  • pay caps and no equity;
  • a bureaucratic, union-dense culture;
  • a long record of value-destroying, state-directed allocation.

For the model: management will deliver the contracted plan on schedule and keep leverage under 3.5 times. It will not deliver structural surprises on costs or coal. Its plans may be rewritten after the autumn 2027 election.

C-55

How big is the company's ambition or vision (a force: growth as a priority, outcompeting, innovating, adapting, pushing), particularly in relation to peers?

F4819th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: The stated ambition is large. The ambition is national, set by the state's energy-security and transition goals, rather than commercial.

  • Targets:
  • EBITDA above PLN 30bn by 2035, double 2025;
  • PLN 210bn of capital spending;
  • over 4 GW of offshore wind;

  • net zero by 2050.

  • the 2030 targets were cut to match consensus;
  • the bonus structure rewards stability and policy goals (grid security, state projects) rather than shareholder returns.

Interplay: a large ambition paired with weak incentives and political tenure is a recipe for capital deployment, not necessarily for value creation. Bernstein's PLN 16bn for 2035 (47% below the ambition) is the more useful anchor.

C-56

How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities (an enabler): widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and a dynamic strategy that can capitalise on a plausible range of environments?

D5815th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How great and smart is the strategy, with right and bold strategic priorities, widening the moat over the mid term, growth as a priority, and dynamic enough for a plausible range of environments?

Judgement: The strategy is smart in its choices and weak in boldness on the one issue that matters most.

Right priorities

  • It widens the real moat. Networks get the largest share of capital spending; the asset base doubles; more than 70% of spending is regulated or contracted.
  • It is dynamic in a useful way. Gas spending depends on post-2028 capacity support. Baltica 3 is being redesigned rather than forced. Storage and power-to-heat answer negative prices. The 2030 targets match realistic power prices.
  • It is focused on earnings quality and contracted cash flows that support a dividend.

Weaknesses

  • No credible path for the coal fleet, the largest value item and the main post-2028 risk. Only "negotiations" and a plan for coal as a reserve.
  • Too many simultaneous options (nuclear, data centres, offshore 4 GW, storage 18 GWh, heat purchases) for a team with political tenure and pay caps.
  • The 2035 target is not anchored in projects analysts can see.
  • It is rewritten every 12 to 16 months after ministerial change: June 2025, then October 2026.

Range of environments

  • It works well if regulators keep grid returns and state support for dispatchable capacity continues.
  • It works poorly if post-2028 coal support is denied, or if a levy and tariff caps squeeze the cash that funds the build.
C-57

How great is the chief executive, judged by actions rather than conjecture (an enabler, not an ossifier)?

F4822nd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: The evidence on Dariusz Lubera after ten months is limited but competent. He is an experienced industry insider with a credible first strategy. He is also a political appointee who moved from the supervisory board into the job. His independence and tenure are constrained by the minister's agenda and the 2027 election. He is an enabler of discipline, not yet shown to be an exceptional operator.

Actions, January to November 2026

  • Strategy: the October 2026 update (spending to 2035 cut 10.6%, gas made conditional, 2030 target rebased, dividend resumed).
  • Projects: Baltica 2 kept on schedule. Two gas peakers contracted (Siemens Energy and Polimex, March 2026). The F.E.W. Baltic II offshore deal completed.
  • Financing: the PLN 3.89bn Rybnik loan arranged.
  • Other: LNG regasification slots secured; a second nuclear plant agreement signed with the state; a vice-president replaced (May 2026).

Background: chief executive of Tauron in 2008-2015. Tauron listed in 2010 and grew distribution, but also invested heavily in coal. Its later write-downs and leverage reflect the era.

What is missing

  • No record yet of operating improvement (supply margins, metering, collections).
  • No resolution of coal.
  • No personal shareholding.

Predecessor context: the Leadership report's praise for capital discipline and the dividend belongs to Lubera's team, not to Marzec. Marzec's own delivery was the 2024 capacity contracts (the "outsmarting" of peers) and continued Baltica 2 progress. Both men show that PGE's chief executives execute the minister's agenda well when it is commercial.

C-57b

How high a priority is hiring well for the chief executive, and how great is the leadership team the chief executive has built (an enabler)?

F4523rd of 25

Judgement: The team is experienced and better than average for a Polish state utility, but it is not of top commercial quality.

  • What helps:
  • Lubera kept the CFO (Jastrzębski, investment banking, since July 2024) and the regulatory vice-president (Laskowski).

C-58

How well resourced and invested is the whole business (a buffer; an ant colony: built yesterday for resilience)?

D5516th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Financially well resourced now; historically under-invested in parts of the operating base.

Financial resources

  • PLN 14.2bn of cash at June 2026 (seasonal).
  • Undrawn and preferential funding.
  • Economic leverage about 1.1 times, the lowest among peers.
  • Capital spending stepping up from about PLN 11bn to about PLN 15bn a year.
  • Baltica 2 fully funded.

Under-investment, now being corrected

  • Distribution grids average over 35 years old, and connections were rejected for lack of capacity.
  • Smart meters lag peers: 35% to 47% against Energa's 93% to 98%.
  • Cabling: PLN 286m spent in H1 2026, targeting 30% of medium-voltage lines underground by 2030.
  • Storage arrived late (Żarnowiec 2027).
  • The six years without dividends (FY2019-FY2024) preserved cash, but much went to coal-era assets that were later written off.

Human resources: stretched. Turnover and pay caps limit capacity, as above.

The "ant colony" test (built yesterday for resilience): the balance sheet is the buffer. The assets are not resilient; coal is a fragility.

For the model: maintenance and grid spending at or above PLN 4bn a year in distribution (management "above PLN 4bn"). Total spending of PLN 14bn to 17bn a year in FY2026-2028, above the brokers' PLN 10.7bn for FY2028.

C-59How holistic and congruent is the culture, with alignment across the total system (as at Lifco, Avanza or Ryanair)? Such that the goals, strategy, culture, execution, decisions are congruent, holistic and symbiotic?F2825th of 25full answer under 2a Internal risks
C-60

How clear, straightforward and helpful is the company's communication, including on the strategy and the driver transmission mechanism?

C-6010th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: Communication is clear and fairly helpful for a utility, and better than the accounting.

Helpful

  • Segment recurring EBITDA every quarter, with one-offs itemised.
  • "Economic net debt", which captures CO2 obligations.
  • Preliminary results about two weeks before reports.
  • Explicit 2030 segment targets and a split of capital spending.
  • Sensitivity tables (CO2, currency, volume, margin, WACC on heat assets).
  • Plain statements of risk: the CFO on post-2028 coal support; management that the household tariff does not cover costs.

Weaknesses

  • No annual guidance.
  • Changing dividend conditions.
  • Heavy reliance on "recurring" adjustments that recur.
  • Complex contract-for-difference derivative accounting.
  • Frequent strategy rewrites.

Driver transmission: the mechanism from regulated asset base to distribution EBITDA, and from capacity contracts to coal EBITDA, is laid out. The mechanism from strategy to EPS is not, because depreciation, interest and the coal cliff are left to analysts.

C-61

How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?

D5516th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking?

Judgement: Mixed. It is good at delivering contracted physical projects and securing state-backed revenue. It is poor at structural change and at protecting minority value through cycles.

Recent delivery

  • Dolna Odra (1.36 GW) and Gryfino (1.34 GW) CCGTs commissioned.
  • Baltica 2 on schedule: financial close January 2025, foundations complete by August 2026.
  • Capacity contracts won: 2024 five-year; 2027 supplementary 1,859 MW.
  • Baltica 9 contract for difference won (December 2025).
  • Turów dispute settled; financing raised.

Delivery against consensus (Change Forces, approximate)

  • EBITDA beats in FY2022-FY2024 (+1.8%, +5.6%, +6.2%) and a miss in FY2025 (-8.6%).
  • Q1 2026 in line with preliminary figures.
  • Q2 2026 in line with estimates.

Misses and justifications

  • The coal carve-out (not management's decision).
  • Baltica 3 redesign and delay to about 2032.
  • Renewables impairments.
  • Strategy targets lowered twice (2030 EBITDA from PLN 17bn to 15bn; spending from PLN 235bn to 210bn).
  • Six years without dividends.

In tougher conditions (2022-2025 caps and levies), PGE delivered rising EBITDA. Much of that came from regulated distribution and price spikes, not from management action.

C-62

To what extent does the company hold outlier standards, driving higher, faster and better, with a record of out-executing competitors?

F3823rd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: No outlier standards, except in winning capacity and auctions.

  • Out-executes peers on:
  • capacity contracting (secured to 2028 while Tauron and Enea face earlier cliffs);
  • gas build (Dolna Odra and Gryfino delivered while Energa's Ostrołęka and Grudziądz slipped and broke loan terms);
  • balance-sheet strength.
  • Trails peers on:
  • smart metering (Energa near 98%);
  • collections (Tauron 24 days against PGE's 42);
  • staff turnover (Tauron about 7%, PGE 10.2%);
  • renewable build (Energa 46% green generation; Tauron above 1 GW and growing).
  • No evidence of a culture of "higher, faster, better".
C-63

Explain, with the evidence, the extent to which the company puts shareholders first, ahead of management, and how prudent its governance is (scaffolding put in place even where it was not needed): what does the evidence show, and why?

F2525th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

Judgement: PGE does not put minority shareholders first. It puts the state's policy first, the state as shareholder second, and minorities third. Management's own interest is not the problem, since pay is capped and there are no options. Governance has formal scaffolding (78% nominal independence, competent audit committee, clean audits) that offers little real protection.

Evidence that the state comes first

  • Control: 60.86% state voting. The state appoints one supervisory board member directly. A 10% voting cap applies to every other holder, so minorities cannot form coalitions.
  • Board dismissals at the state's request (December 2025).
  • The 2016 dividend: cut from a proposed PLN 0.92 to 0.25 at the state's vote, to fund coal mines and spending.
  • The 2016 capital restructuring: a PLN 110m tax charge benefiting the state as tax collector.
  • 2015-2016: pressure to support coal mines (Kompania Węglowa, PGG).
  • The coal agency: announced, then abandoned, leaving the liability with minorities.
  • Below-cost household tariffs imposed through the regulator.
  • Executive pay: fixed pay about PLN 0.75m; no equity; directors own zero shares; no insider dealing signal.

Evidence that minorities are now aligned with the state, for now

  • The dividend resumption serves both the state's budget and minorities.
  • Recovery-plan financing and capacity support benefit all shareholders.

Prudence: clean audits, no restatements, anti-corruption systems, a hedging policy that forbids speculation.

Why it matters: alignment is coincidental and temporary. It holds while the state needs cash and investment from PGE. It breaks when the state needs PGE to absorb a social cost.

C-64

To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, not just an operator or generator, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

F3023rd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Management

The Investment Analyst's heading To what extent is the company an enlightened capital allocator and a dynamic repurchaser of its shares, with a record of allocating capital at a high return on new invested capital?

Judgement: No. The long record is of capital allocated at or below the cost of capital, followed by large write-downs:

  • the Opole coal expansion (2013);
  • coal mine support (2015-2016);
  • a PLN 8.8bn coal impairment (2015);
  • the joint bid for EDF's Polish assets (2017);
  • a PLN 8.65bn to 8.97bn coal impairment (2025);
  • the renewables impairment (2026).

PGE has never bought back shares. It issued equity in 2022 (shares +20%).

Recent improvement

  • Selective gas.
  • Capital spending cut.
  • A dividend.
  • Shrewd capacity contracting.
  • Strong offshore contract terms.
  • Spending weighted to regulated returns.

These improve the quality of new capital but do not make PGE an "enlightened" allocator. Returns on new capital will be about the cost of capital (C-50 and C-51), and the allocator is ultimately the state.

4c

Financial profile

F4324th of 258 answers
Judgement43C-4932C-5045C-5145C-5230C-5350C-5430C-7662
Group judgement

Company - Economic strength

F4324th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

PGE's economics combine a strong regulated core with a weak and declining merchant periphery. The group earns middling returns on a base flattered by write-downs.

  • Earnings: high but cyclically lifted in FY2026. EBITDA is flat at about PLN 13bn to 14bn through FY2029 on the brokers' figures. EPS falls as depreciation and interest rise.
  • Cash: conversion is negative through 2027 because spending exceeds operating cash flow and the dividend restarts.
  • Earnings quality: poor, with recurring "one-offs", contract-for-difference derivative noise and four competing EPS bases.
  • Operating leverage to spreads is high in the merchant 35% to 40% of EBITDA.
  • Financial leverage is low but rising.
  • Costs are mostly not cuttable except by politically gated closures.
  • The big improvement levers are regulated growth (in management's hands) and a coal solution (in the state's hands).

For the model: regulated and contracted EBITDA compounding about 5% a year. Merchant EBITDA peaking FY2026-27 and falling into 2029. Depreciation and interest rising. A recurring one-off allowance. Negative free cash flow funded by debt. Leverage reaching about 2 times or more by 2029.

C-49

What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

F3224th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: PGE generates no sustained EPS growth. Its earnings swing with energy prices, write-downs and policy.

The brokers' figures

  • EPS rebounds to PLN 2.90 in FY2026, then falls to PLN 2.60 (FY2027) and PLN 2.47 (FY2028), about -8% a year.
  • Revenue grows +5%, +3% and 0% over the same years.

FY2026 is a cyclical peak, not the start of a growth path. Durability is low.

History

Year Revenue Revenue growth Reported basic EPS FactSet adjusted EPS
FY2019 PLN 37.6bn n/a -2.12 0.59

  • Bernstein's adjusted EPS for FY2025 is -1.56, after impairments; its FY2026 forecast is 2.19 and FY2027 is 1.89.
  • Revenue is a poor guide. It follows wholesale prices passed through, so it fell 33% in 2024 while EBITDA rose 8%.

Why EPS falls after FY2026 on the brokers' figures

  • EBITDA is flat at PLN 13.3bn to 14.1bn.
  • Depreciation rises from about PLN 4.8bn to 6.0bn (FY2026 to FY2028) as new assets enter service.
  • Net interest rises with net debt.
  • The coal supplementary capacity revenue lapses after 2027.

What the brokers have done recently: they raised FY2026 EPS by 72% over 12 months and FY2027 by 64% in three months. EBITDA rose only 13%, so most of the EPS lift came from below EBITDA. The likely sources: lower coal depreciation after the 2025 write-down, lower financial costs, and the move from a loss-making base. Upgrades driven by accounting are lower quality than operating upgrades.

For the model:

  • Adjusted EPS of about PLN 2.6 to 3.0 in FY2026.
  • PLN 2.2 to 2.8 in FY2027.
  • PLN 2.0 to 2.7 in FY2028.
  • A FY2029 trough risk around PLN 1.8 to 2.5.
  • Use PGE's own recurring definition: exclude impairments, onerous-contract changes and contract-for-difference derivative revaluations.
C-50

How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

F4521st of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The scope to deploy capital is very large, PLN 117bn to 2030 and PLN 210bn to 2035. The return on that capital is mostly regulated or contracted at about the cost of capital, not well above it. The plan builds size and earnings visibility, not much excess value. That supports a stable, utility-like multiple, not a growth multiple.

Returns by asset class

Asset Expected return Evidence
Distribution Base 7.48% pre-tax real; up to 12.96% with premiums; nominal about 8% to 10% pre-tax Post-tax real return about 6% to 8%
Offshore Low double-digit equity returns Baltica 2's contract terms are in the top fifth for developers; Ørsted required 150 to 300 basis points over WACC

Cost of capital: Polish ten-year yields of about 5.5% to 6% plus an equity risk premium give a cost of equity of 11% to 12%. Bernstein's discount rates: 8% renewables, 12% heat, 20% coal.

Mid- and long-term potential: earnings visibility rises (63% regulated and contracted EBITDA by 2030). Growth in earnings per share is slow, because rising depreciation and interest absorb much of the EBITDA growth until about 2030.

Fair multiple: a regulated network inside a coal group and a state-controlled balance sheet. Polish history supports about 5 to 8 times earnings. A network-only valuation could approach European regulated multiples (about 10 to 12 times EBITDA). Even 6.5 times EBITDA, Bernstein's multiple for PGE's distribution, would value the grid alone near today's market value. That remains locked until coal and governance change.

C-51

How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

D4519th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Returns on capital look moderate on the reported base and are flattered by write-downs. The return on new capital is likely below the return on the existing base.

Calculations

  • Capital employed: about PLN 55bn to 60bn (equity about PLN 45bn to 47bn plus economic net debt about PLN 15bn, less non-operating items).
  • Return on capital employed:
  • FY2025 adjusted EBIT PLN 7.3bn: pre-tax about 12% to 13%, post-tax about 10%.
  • FY2026 brokers' EBIT PLN 8.9bn: pre-tax about 14% to 15%, post-tax about 11% to 12%.
  • Tangible capital is close to capital employed. Intangibles are small; PKP Energetyka goodwill is the main item.

Why the base is flattered. The base shrank by about PLN 8.65bn to 8.97bn in 2025, when the coal fleet was written down to near zero (only Dolna Odra kept book value, about PLN 4bn). Coal earnings over 2026-2027 (about PLN 2.1bn a year of EBITDA from the brokers) are therefore earned on almost no book capital. That temporarily lifts group returns.

Return on new capital: regulated and contracted, so post-tax about 6% to 9%. As PLN 15bn a year of new capital is added at these returns and coal earnings fall, group returns on capital will decline toward about 8% post-tax by 2029-2030.

Peers: Public Power Corporation's ROIC is 7.5% (Enel 11%, Engie 9%, Iberdrola 7%), similar.

For the model: a return on capital employed falling from about 11% to 12% post-tax (FY2026) to about 8% (FY2029-2030).

C-52

How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

F3023rd of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Cash conversion is poor and becomes worse through 2027. Working capital swings by billions within the year because of CO2 settlement timing.

History (FactSet free cash flow)

Year Free cash flow Operating cash flow Capital spending
FY2022 +PLN 4.6bn n/a n/a
FY2023 -PLN 7.1bn n/a n/a
FY2024 -PLN 0.1bn n/a n/a
FY2025 +PLN 1.3bn PLN 12.8bn PLN 11.5bn

The brokers' forecast: free cash flow -PLN 5.1bn (FY2026), -PLN 3.1bn (FY2027), +PLN 1.6bn (FY2028). Peer-table free cash flow to net income: -0.8 times, -0.5 times, +0.3 times.

Working capital and similar items

  • CO2 timing. Allowances are bought forward and surrendered each September, so cash leaves in lumps. In Q1 2026 it cost PLN 1.9bn of operating cash flow (Q1 operating cash flow PLN 1,179m against PLN 4,738m), and Q2 reversed it (H1 operating cash flow PLN 8,877m). Cash peaks in June (PLN 14.2bn) and falls after the September surrender. PGE holds about 31.9m tonnes of allowances worth PLN 10.1bn in inventory.
  • Reverse factoring of PLN 1,574m (December 2025) is a debt-like item inside trade payables.
  • Receivables rose to 42 days.
  • Price Difference Fund settlements and state compensation create receivables and payables with the state. The fund provision was PLN 605m.
  • Net working capital is about 5% to 6% of revenue (FactSet), roughly stable.

For the model

  • Model cash flow annually, not quarterly.
  • Carry CO2 obligations explicitly in economic net debt.
  • Assume conversion of adjusted net income to free cash flow is negative in FY2026-27 and turns positive only when capital spending falls below operating cash flow, which brokers expect from FY2028.

C-54

How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

F3020th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: The earnings base is unclean. The gap between adjusted and reported earnings is large and recurring. "Non-recurring" items appear in most years. Several accounting features flatter or obscure earnings. The Consumer must model in layers and choose a basis explicitly.

Size of the gap

  • FY2025: reported net loss PLN 3.5bn; recurring EBITDA PLN 12.9bn; FactSet adjusted EPS +1.60; reported EPS -1.56; brokers'-basis actual EPS -3.10.
  • 2025 one-offs at EBITDA level totalled about PLN 1.47bn:
  • coal onerous contracts PLN 766m;
  • retail onerous contracts PLN 316m;
  • Price Difference Fund provision PLN 605m;
  • reclamation provisions +PLN 435m;
  • offset by other items.
  • Below EBITDA: impairments of PLN 8.65bn to 8.97bn, partly offset by PLN 1.39bn of deferred tax.

Recurring "one-offs"

  • Onerous-contract provisions: 2023 PLN 761m; 2025 PLN 766m plus PLN 316m; Q1 2026 minus PLN 244m in coal and plus PLN 109m in supply.
  • Impairments: 2015 PLN 8.8bn; 2025; Q2 2026 PLN 248m in renewables.
  • Price Difference Fund corrections every year 2023-2026.

Volatility from the Baltica 2 contract for difference. It contains embedded derivatives:

  • Q1 2026: minus PLN 249m in finance costs.
  • Q2 2026: plus PLN 267m in EBITDA and minus PLN 300m in finance costs.

These are non-cash, large and unpredictable, and will continue until 2027 and through operation.

Flattering features

  • Borrowing costs and decommissioning costs are capitalised into fixed assets.
  • Reverse factoring of PLN 1.57bn is kept out of net debt.
  • Coal earnings in 2026-2027 are earned on written-down assets with little depreciation.
  • Revenue for balancing settlements is estimated for up to 14 months.

Tax: 19% statutory. The effective rate is about 23% (Q1 2026: PLN 609m of tax). A proposal to raise corporate tax to 22% exists, and Poland raised bank tax to 30% for 2026.

Minorities: small (about PLN 1.24bn of minority equity).

For the model, in layers

  1. Recurring EBITDA by segment.
  2. A one-off allowance of minus PLN 0.3bn to 0.8bn a year.
  3. Depreciation.
  4. Financial result: cash interest, plus provision unwinding, plus contract-for-difference derivative noise (carried as zero in the base case).
  5. Tax at 23%.
  6. Minorities at about PLN 0.1bn to 0.2bn.
  7. Adjusted net income on that basis is the dividend base: management calculates the dividend excluding non-cash impairments.
C-76

How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

D6215th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Financial leverage is low today and comfortable. It amplifies EPS little now but will amplify it more each year as spending is debt-funded. It becomes material only if spending runs at the strategy's full pace (about PLN 20bn a year) or if coal losses arrive without support.

Position

  • Economic net debt at end-June 2026: about 1.06 times trailing recurring EBITDA (about PLN 14bn to 15bn). Reported net debt: PLN 1.5bn (FactSet June) to PLN 7.1bn (March).
  • Fixed-rate share: 77%. Debt in zloty: over 95%.
  • About PLN 14bn of 0.5% loans; project finance for Baltica 2.
  • Fitch BBB (downgrade threshold 3.7 times). Company ceiling 3.5 times.
  • Net interest about PLN 1.27bn (FY2025, FactSet), including provision unwinding.

Path

  • Brokers' free cash flow plus dividends add about PLN 6bn to 7bn of debt in FY2026 and PLN 4bn to 5bn in FY2027.
  • Economic leverage reaches about 1.6 to 1.9 times by end-2027 and 2.0 to 2.5 times by 2029.
  • At the strategy's full spending pace, leverage approaches 3 times by 2029-2030. Then dividend and credit-rating constraints bind.

EPS amplification: each PLN 5bn of new debt at 6% costs about PLN 0.3bn a year before tax, about PLN 0.11 of EPS (-4%). The 0.5% loans save about PLN 0.7bn to 0.8bn a year against market rates (about PLN 0.25 to 0.30 of EPS).

For the model

  • Net interest rising from about PLN 1.3bn to about PLN 1.8bn to 2.2bn by FY2028.
  • Capitalised interest on Baltica 2 during construction ends at completion, so the expense steps up in 2028.

Pricing power and operating leverage (and others)

5a

Pricing power

3 answers
