C-45To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?F3223rd of 25full answer under 4a Business quality
P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

F3516th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: price adds to revenue but almost nothing to margin, and the balance of price risk is negative through the autumn 2027 election.

  • Regulated and contracted part (about 60% of EBITDA): prices are indexed by formula: distribution return on the asset base, CPI-indexed CfDs, capacity contracts, and heat at cost-plus with a lag. They add about PLN 0.2bn to 0.4bn of EBITDA a year.
  • Merchant part (about 40%): PGE is a price-taker. The 2026 spread windfall unwinds: coal and gas lose about PLN 0.65bn in FY2027 and a further PLN 0.75bn in FY2028, including the lapse of the supplementary capacity payment.
  • Households: the price is administered below cost.
  • Upside is capped by clawback; downside is not protected. That asymmetry is the defining feature of PGE's pricing.

Price contribution by business

Business 2026 position 2027-2028 2029-2030 Effect on margin
Household G tariff PLN 495.16/MWh; expected loss PLN 560m Decision about December 2026. Forwards at PLN 590/MWh argue for a large rise, but the government is leaning to caps and compensation. Base: tariff raised but short of cost; supply EBITDA PLN 0.25bn in FY2027 against PLN 0.10bn in FY2026 Gap closes slowly; supply PLN 0.45bn to 0.50bn Small positive from 2027; election-year risk
Distribution Tariff +PLN 3.5/MWh; WACC lower (Tauron -1.34 points) The 18-month average of ten-year yields rose with the 2026 sell-off: return flat in 2027, rising in 2028 Return about 10% to 11% including premiums (Bernstein: 8.0%) +PLN 0.1bn to 0.25bn a year
Capacity market Five-year coal contracts; 2027 supplementary at PLN 325.56 to 372.04/kW-year Fixed; supplementary lapses after 2027 Coal contracts end 2028; new gas has 17-year indexed contracts Fixed, then a cliff
Offshore CfD (Baltica 2) PLN 319.6/MWh (2021) indexed to PLN 443.49 (2025) Indexed about 3% to 4% a year; first power 2027 About PLN 470 to 490/MWh Real price held flat
District heating Gas +34% in H1 2026; tariff lag of one to two years Tariffs catch up in 2027; if gas falls, the lag helps (2025: coal -17%, gas -21% widened margins) Cost-plus Neutral over a cycle; timing swings of PLN 0.2bn to 0.3bn
Wholesale power 2027 base forward PLN 590/MWh (mid-September 2026) Partly hedged earlier; PGE booked about PLN 0.7bn of losses on 2026-2027 conventional contract sales (Bernstein) Solar cannibalisation, batteries Price-taker; negative from 2027

Reasoning and evidence: why the asymmetry is real and current

  • Clawback is happening now:
  • the Energy Regulatory Office demanded an extra PLN 265m Price Difference Fund payment from PGE in July 2026;
  • Energa paid PLN 553m in March 2026;
  • Poland taxes some 2026 fuel revenues more than 20% above average at 60%, and Orlen books a PLN 2.4bn charge in Q4 2026;
  • the Ministry of Energy's draft household gas price cap for 2027 is financed by a 10% levy on gas traders' net revenue (6 November 2026);
  • Bill UD459 (October 2026) proposes temporarily higher corporate tax for large energy and fuel companies.
  • The regulator's power is asymmetric:
  • it can demand tariff cuts within a year if wholesale prices fall (Deutsche Bank, March 2024);
  • but in 2024 it capped household prices at PLN 412/MWh against a tariff of PLN 739, with compensation paid late.
  • Regional precedent:
  • CEZ: 60% windfall surcharge 2023-2025, then net profit +10% in H1 2026 once it expired;
  • Austria: a 95% levy above EUR 90/MWh extended to March 2030 (Verbund still models EUR 340m for 2027);
  • Romania: 100% above RON 450/MWh;
  • Slovakia: 90%.
  • Clawbacks last two to three years and then expire. Poland's fiscal position (deficit about 7% of GDP) and the 2027 election point to one in 2027.
  • Business customers prove there is no commercial pricing power. Tariff A sales fell 25% and Tariff B 15% in Q1 2026, while solar developers offered corporate power purchase agreements near EUR 21/MWh (Bernstein, late 2025).

Contribution to the lines (base case)

  • Revenue: price adds about +2% to 3% in FY2027 (tariff rise, higher forwards), subtracts about 2% to 3% in FY2028-FY2029 as wholesale prices normalise, and adds about +1% to 2% in FY2030 (indexation).
  • EBITDA:
Price component FY2027 FY2028 FY2029
Regulated and contracted indexation +0.3 +0.3 +0.3
Merchant price normalisation -0.65 -0.75 -0.2
Household tariff +0.15 +0.15 +0.05
Net about -0.2 about -0.3 about +0.15
  • Not in the base, as a scenario: a levy or revenue cap of PLN 0.5bn to 1.5bn of EBITDA a year in 2027-2028 (probability about 25% to 35%); a household tariff gap PLN 0.3bn to 0.5bn wider (probability about 30% to 40%).

For the model

  • Price is not a margin driver.
  • Index distribution, CfD and capacity by formula.
  • Let merchant spreads fall back from the 2026 level in 2027-2028.
  • Keep the policy scenarios separate from the base, because they are binary and dated (December 2026 tariff; 2027 budget; election run-up).
5b

Operating leverage

2 answers
C-7545P-0135
C-75

How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

F4518th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Operating leverage to revenue is low and misleading, because revenue mostly passes through fuel and CO2 costs. Operating leverage to spreads and volumes in merchant generation is high. It sits in about 35% to 40% of EBITDA (coal, gas, merchant renewables, supply); about 60% is regulated or contracted and has little operating leverage.

The company's own sensitivities (EPS impact)

  • Revenue ±1%: ±PLN 0.03 (±1.5%).
  • EBIT margin ±0.5 points: ±PLN 0.10 (±4.5%).
  • Volume ±2%: ±PLN 0.11 (±5%).
  • CO2 +10%: -PLN 0.15 (-6.85%).
  • EUR/PLN ±5%: ±PLN 0.07 (±3%).
  • A 10% move in wholesale power prices moves adjusted EPS by more than 20% (Morgan Stanley).

Fixed costs

  • Lignite mining and generation (own mines, about 41,700 staff group-wide, union agreements), plant upkeep, and grid operations (recovered through tariffs) are largely fixed.
  • CO2 and fuel are variable.
  • Every TWh of lost coal output therefore removes margin while most of the cost remains.

Range of outcomes for EBITDA, around the brokers' PLN 13.4bn

  • 12 months: about ±PLN 1.0bn to 1.5bn (±8% to 11%).
  • FY2027-2028: about ±PLN 2bn (±15%). Spreads, capacity support, household tariffs and levies drive it.
  • FY2029-2030: coal support alone is worth PLN 2bn to 5bn.

For the model: run sensitivities on clean dark and spark spreads (PLN/MWh), coal and gas volumes (TWh), the CO2 price, and the household tariff gap (PLN m), not on revenue.

P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

F3517th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: PGE carries two kinds of fixed-cost leverage, and over this window both work against profit.

  1. Classic operating leverage sits in coal. It is about 13% of FY2026 EBITDA but drives most of the EBITDA path. Lignite mining and plant costs are largely fixed, and the capacity payments act as fixed revenue. Volumes fall and the fixed revenue ends while the fixed costs stay. Coal EBITDA swings from PLN 1.85bn (FY2026) to minus PLN 1.40bn (FY2029) and minus PLN 2.00bn (FY2030): a PLN 3.3bn to 3.9bn fall, about 25% of group EBITDA, while group revenue barely moves.
  2. A newer capital-intensity leverage sits below EBITDA. Depreciation rises from PLN 4.7bn to 6.6bn (+40%) as the grid asset base, Baltica 2, Rybnik CCGT and storage enter service. The EBITDA margin stays at 19% to 21%, but the EBIT margin falls from 13.8% (FY2026) to about 10% (FY2029-FY2030).

Revenue is a poor guide. Revenue mostly passes through fuel, CO2 and wholesale power, so leverage from revenue to profit is close to zero and can run backwards. In 2024 revenue fell 33% while FactSet operating EBITDA rose 8%.

Against the last four to six years

  • The margin rise of 2024-2025 was mostly a denominator effect. FactSet operating EBITDA margin rose from 9.5% to 11.4% (FY2021-FY2023) to 18.4% to 18.5% (FY2024-FY2025), because revenue fell from the PLN 96.0bn peak to PLN 61.4bn.
  • The 2024-2026 EBIT margin peak (12% to 14%) is not sustainable. It was helped by the 2025 coal write-down: coal depreciation fell to PLN 60m in Q1 2026 from PLN 173m (Q2 2026: PLN 59m against PLN 171m). That adds about PLN 0.45bn a year to EBIT in 2026-2028 on assets now carried at PLN 3,966m, mostly Dolna Odra. It does not reverse, but it is a one-time step: from FY2027 new assets push depreciation up faster than EBITDA grows.
Margin FY2021 FY2023 FY2025 FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
EBITDA margin 9.7% (FactSet op.) 11.4% (FactSet op.) 18.5% FactSet op.; 21.0% recurring 21.0% 20.1% 21.2% 19.4% 20.1%
EBIT margin 4.6% 5.9% 11.9% 13.8% 12.7% 12.6% 9.9% 10.1%

Reasoning and evidence: where the fixed costs sit

  • Lignite is a fixed-cost business. Mine staff, overburden removal and plant upkeep do not fall with output (Bernstein, 8 May 2025). PGE self-supplies about 70% of its conventional fuel from Bełchatów and Turów. Lignite plants have minimum loads of 50% to 60% (Morgan Stanley, 13 January 2026). Each TWh of lost lignite output removes margin while most of the cost remains. Lignite output fell 11% in Q4 2025 to 7.03 TWh.
  • Precedent shows the fixed costs outlast the volume. ZE PAK (2025 annual report): "the scale of reduction in fixed costs associated with coal operations is proportionally smaller than the scale of the operational reduction"; Q1 2026 cost of sales rose 18% despite a smaller fleet. CEZ (June 2026): lignite cash extraction cost EUR 17/MWh against total marginal cost of EUR 95/MWh, of which CO2 is EUR 78/MWh. So CO2 is the variable cost, and the mine is the fixed cost.
  • Hard coal is more variable, but not until 2029. Hard-coal fuel is bought in, so it can fall with output, except that the Polska Grupa Górnicza take-or-pay contract (PLN 13.5bn over 2023-2028) fixes it to end-2028. From 2029 hard-coal fuel becomes variable. That softens the 2029 cliff slightly, by perhaps PLN 0.2bn to 0.4bn.
  • The leverage runs both ways within a year. Coal recurring EBITDA was PLN 281m in Q1 2026 (-31%) and PLN 763m in Q2 2026, when generation rose 18% on the gas-price shock. One quarter of better spreads and volume added about PLN 0.5bn.

Sensitivities to use, not revenue

  • A 10% move in power prices moves EBITDA by more than 10% and EPS by more than 20% (Morgan Stanley).
  • Company figures: a 2% move in volume moves EPS by about 5%; a 10% rise in CO2 lowers EPS by 6.85%.
  • Merchant share: about 35% to 40% of EBITDA (coal, gas, merchant renewables, supply) carries this leverage. The rest (distribution, heat, railway, contracted capacity, the Baltica 2 CfD) carries little.

How it shapes the lines

  • 18 months (FY2026 to mid-2027): the leverage is still positive. Capacity revenue is locked: five-year coal contracts plus the 2027 supplementary award of 1,859 MW, worth about PLN 600m, take contracted capacity to 11.1 GW for 2027. 2027 forwards are high: the base-load price rose to PLN 590/MWh in mid-September 2026 from PLN 500 (ING). Coal EBITDA falls only from PLN 1.85bn to 1.45bn.
  • FY2028: the supplementary PLN 600m lapses and spreads normalise further: coal PLN 0.70bn.
  • FY2029-FY2030: the five-year contracts end. The fixed cost base meets a revenue line without capacity support, and coal EBITDA goes negative. My base assumes about PLN 0.5bn a year of replacement support from 2029 (a strategic reserve, or the legally required compensation for forced operation). Management assumes minus PLN 3.3bn in 2030, Bernstein minus PLN 1.8bn; I use minus PLN 2.0bn.

Cone and skew

  • FY2027 coal: ±PLN 0.7bn (spreads, levy). FY2029 coal: minus PLN 0.4bn to minus PLN 2.9bn, set by the post-2028 support decision and by which capacity-revenue figure is right (Bernstein against Fitch, see the preface).
  • The skew is negative through 2028 and two-sided after it.

For the model

  • Model coal as capacity revenue plus spread times volume, less a fixed cost block. Hold the fixed costs flat in nominal terms, falling only at named closures (Dolna Odra coal units 2026; Rybnik units 2026-2028).
  • Do not let coal costs fall with volume.
  • Model depreciation by asset class (see P-08), because below-EBITDA leverage now matters more than revenue leverage.
5c

Financial leverage

2 answers
C-7662P-0238
C-76How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?D6215th of 25full answer under 4c Financial profile
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

F3817th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: financial leverage is the fastest-growing drag on EPS, and from 2029 it is the constraint that sets the dividend and capital spending.

  • Net financial items rise from about PLN 0.7bn (FY2026) to PLN 2.1bn (FY2030). That costs PLN 0.46 a share after tax, about 16% of FY2026 EPS.
  • FY2026 flatters the starting point. Cash peaked at PLN 14.2bn in June, and interest on Baltica 2 is capitalised. The FY2026 figure is not a run-rate.
  • Company net debt rises from PLN 4.2bn to about PLN 35bn by end-2030, and economic net debt from PLN 15.8bn to about PLN 43bn. Economic leverage goes from 1.23 times to about 3.2 times.
  • The leverage is low today but rises in the wrong year. EBITDA dips in 2029 with the coal cliff, so leverage jumps exactly when the dividend from FY2028 earnings is decided (mid-2029). The 3.5-times ceiling then binds, and the dividend is the first thing cut.

Path (PLN bn)

End-2025 End-2026 End-2027 End-2028 End-2029 End-2030
Company net debt 4.2 6.9 14.3 21.7 28.6 34.7
Economic net debt (CO2 obligations falling from 11.6 to 8.0 as coal burn falls) 15.8 18.5 25.3 31.7 37.1 42.7
Economic net debt / recurring EBITDA 1.23x 1.35x 1.86x 2.22x 2.93x 3.22x
Company net debt / recurring EBITDA 0.33x 0.50x 1.05x 1.52x 2.26x 2.61x
Net financial items -1.27 -0.70 -1.10 -1.50 -1.80 -2.10

Reasoning and evidence

  • What pushes debt up, per year 2027-2030:
  • FCF of minus PLN 4.5bn to 5.4bn;
  • dividends of PLN 1.0bn to 1.6bn;
  • acquisitions of PLN 0.5bn to 1.0bn (heat networks).
  • Mitigants in the cost of debt:
  • 77% of debt is fixed-rate and over 95% is in zloty;
  • about PLN 14bn of recovery-plan loans at 0.5%, which save about PLN 0.7bn to 0.8bn a year against market rates;
  • Baltica 2 has PLN 11.1bn of non-recourse finance plus a PLN 3.9bn state development bank loan;
  • the Rybnik term loan is PLN 3.89bn.
  • Aggravants:
  • new commercial debt costs about 6.5% to 7%, with the Polish ten-year yield at about 6.4% (October 2026);
  • capitalised interest (PLN 164m in 2024, a 26% capitalisation rate) stops on Baltica 2 at completion, so the expense steps up in FY2028;
  • provision unwinding (reclamation, employee benefits) adds a steady non-cash charge of about PLN 0.45bn a year inside net financial items.
  • How I built net interest: about 5% on average company net debt, plus about PLN 0.45bn of unwinding. That gives about PLN 0.7bn in FY2026, in line with the brokers' implied PLN 0.73bn (EBIT 8.92bn less pre-tax 8.19bn). It rises to PLN 2.1bn by FY2030. Rate sensitivity is modest: one point on the floating 23% of a gross debt of about PLN 40bn by 2029 is about PLN 0.1bn.
  • Bernstein (12 May 2026) has economic net debt peaking at PLN 27.5bn in FY2028. I have PLN 31.7bn, because I keep capital spending at PLN 15.5bn, where the brokers have PLN 10.7bn.

EPS amplification

  • Each PLN 5bn of new commercial debt at 6.5% costs about PLN 0.33bn before tax, or PLN 0.11 of EPS (about 4.5% of FY2027 EPS).
  • From FY2026 to FY2030 the rise in net financial items alone takes PLN 0.46 off EPS.

Balance sheet

  • Equity grows by retained earnings of about PLN 3.5bn to 4bn a year (FactSet consensus equity: PLN 47.0bn FY2026, PLN 53.0bn FY2028).
  • Net debt to equity rises from about 0.15 to about 0.6 by 2030.
  • The balance sheet stays investment-grade in the base case, but with no headroom for a policy shock in 2029-2030.

Equity issuance

  • Not in the base case.
  • Before 2028: low probability. Leverage stays under 2.3 times economic.
  • 2029-2030: material probability if post-2028 coal support is refused and capital spending is held near PLN 15bn. The precedent is the 2022 state-backed raise (shares +20%, from 1,869.8m to 2,243.7m). A 10% raise would dilute EPS by about 9%.
  • The more likely response is a dividend cut and capital deferral first. The state cut a proposed dividend in 2016 and suspended dividends for FY2016 and FY2019-FY2024.

Peers: Tauron's leverage rises to 3.9 times by 2030 (Fitch); Enea's ceiling is 3.0 times. PGE's starting point is the strongest of the three, so it can carry the dividend for longer, but not through the 2029 cliff at full capital spending.

For the model

  • Interest at about 5% on average company net debt plus PLN 0.45bn of unwinding.
  • Carry the CfD derivative revaluations at zero.
  • Test economic leverage against 3.5 times every year.
  • If the model shows more than 3.3 times in 2029-2030, cut the dividend before cutting capital spending, because that is the state's precedent.
5d

Cost-out scope NTM

2 answers
C-7735P-0335
C-77

How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

F3525th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Little of the cost base can be cut by management choice.

What can be cut

  • The main lever is closure: shutting coal units removes fixed costs. It is gated by politics (union tripartite teams, system security, capacity obligations to 2028). PGE plans it unit by unit (Dolna Odra 2026, Rybnik 2027-2028).
  • Workforce: headcount falls through natural retirement and voluntary leave programmes (41,690 at end-2025 against 42,898; a PLN 28m voluntary leave provision in Q1 2026). That is about 2% to 3% a year, mostly in coal. Union density (67%) and collective agreements (82%) prevent faster cuts. Wages rise under signed agreements.
  • Overheads and retail: digitalisation (CRM, e-contracts) can trim some percent of supply-segment costs; management targets PLN 950m a year of retail margin improvement by 2030, mostly from products, not cuts.
  • Capital spending (the most cuttable item): management cut PLN 25bn to 2035 in October 2026 and made gas conditional. More can be deferred if needed.

What cannot be cut

  • CO2 (about PLN 16bn a year) can only be reduced by burning less coal.
  • Fuel: take-or-pay coal to 2028 is fixed.
  • Grid operating costs are recovered through tariffs.

For the model: controllable operating costs grow at wage inflation minus 1 to 2 points. No discrete cost programme. Capital spending is the main flexibility, a cushion against downside of PLN 3bn to 5bn a year.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

F3517th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: little reaches the profit and loss account, and late.

  • 18-month window: nothing material. Wage rises under signed agreements outrun headcount attrition.
  • 2028-2030: net savings of about PLN 0.3bn to 0.5bn a year, almost all in coal, from unit closures and attrition. Restructuring and reclamation cash comes first: about PLN 0.5bn to 0.6bn a year in my operating cash flow.
  • The only large lever is capital spending, not operating cost. I assume PGE spends about PLN 83.5bn in 2025-2030 against the PLN 117bn plan (about 71%).
  • The Efficiency Improvement Programme in the October 2026 strategy reduces forecast EBITDA losses by 35% in 2030 and 53% in 2035 against previous forecasts. It most plausibly applies to the coal segment's losses. If so, it implies about PLN 1.8bn of loss reduction from a pre-programme figure near minus PLN 5.1bn to management's minus PLN 3.3bn in 2030. It is a plan to lose less, not a saving to add. I add nothing beyond management's figure. My coal figure (minus PLN 2.0bn in 2030) is better than management's because I assume partial replacement support, not more savings. The perimeter of the programme is not confirmed in the sources; it needs research.

Reasoning and evidence, line by line

  • Personnel (PLN 8.2bn in 2024):
  • headcount fell 2.8% in 2025 (42,898 to 41,690) by natural retirement and voluntary leave (a PLN 28m provision in Q1 2026);
  • but employee benefit costs rose 9.4% in 2024 (PLN 7,462m to 8,163m);
  • union density is 67% and collective agreements cover 82%;
  • wage agreements were signed at over 85% of group employers.
  • Net: personnel cost grows about 3% to 4% a year in nominal terms. It does not fall.
  • Coal fixed costs (closures):
  • Dolna Odra's last coal units close in 2026: restructuring provision PLN 228m in 2025, PLN 194m at Q2 2026.
  • Rybnik units 5 and 8 close in 2026 and units 6 and 7 by end-2027; Bernstein reports Rybnik extended to 2028 for system security.
  • Each closure saves perhaps PLN 0.1bn to 0.2bn a year of fixed cost, a year or more after severance and reclamation cash (reclamation provisions rose PLN 435m in 2025).
  • Closures are gated by tripartite union teams and by system security. The State Assets minister said on 5 November 2026 that coal plants "could stabilise Poland's power system for years to come".
  • Fuel: the hard-coal take-or-pay contract to 2028 blocks savings until 2029.
  • Grid operating costs: recovered through the tariff. Savings pass to customers at reset, so they do not raise EBITDA.
  • Retail and overheads: the new CRM for 1.2m customers and over 70% electronic contracts trim transaction costs. Management's PLN 950m-a-year retail margin target for 2030 is products and pricing, not cost, and it is unproven.
  • Capital spending: the real flexibility. PLN 25bn was cut to 2035 in October 2026, and new gas is now conditional on capacity support after 2028.

Peer precedents: material coal cost elimination in Europe has been state-funded

  • ZE PAK cut 504 posts in H1 2026, 307 with severance, using the state's Social Protection Act (paid leave at 80% of salary).
  • LEAG: up to EUR 1.75bn of approved state aid for fixed post-mining costs and social support.
  • RWE: EUR 2.6bn of lignite compensation.
  • Enea's 2014 fixed-cost plan (PLN 500m over three years) trimmed overheads but left fuel and CO2 untouched.
  • Tauron's average employment was flat in H1 2026 (18,867 against 18,889).
  • PGE has no state package for coal. Without one, closures are slow and costly.

How much and when (PLN bn, EBITDA effect against a no-action base)

FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Coal closures and attrition, net of wage drift ~0 +0.1 +0.2 +0.3 +0.4 to 0.5
Restructuring and reclamation cash (in operating cash flow) -0.5 -0.5 -0.5 -0.6 -0.6
Retail and digital ~0 ~0 +0.05 +0.1 +0.1

For the model

  • No discrete cost programme.
  • Controllable operating costs grow about 4% a year.
  • Coal fixed costs fall only with named closures.
  • Carry the restructuring and reclamation cash.
  • Treat capital spending as the flexible line, and expect management to use it when leverage approaches 3.5 times.
5e

Reinforcing feedback loops

2 answers
C-4755P-0538
C-47To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?D5514th of 25full answer under 4a Business quality
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

F3817th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

The Investment Analyst's heading After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement: in the next 18 months balancing loops dominate and hold EBITDA steady; over four years two reinforcing loops build against EPS and cash.

  • Balancing loops now: contracted capacity revenue, regulated true-ups, the gas shock's suspension of coal displacement, and lagged bond yields feeding the allowed return.
  • Reinforcing loops over four years: coal displacement, and the state's fiscal extraction (dividends, tax, levies).
  • The binding loop of 2029-2030: debt-funded dividends and capital spending meet the leverage ceiling just as coal turns negative.
  • Net: neutral for FY2026-FY2027 EBITDA; negative for EPS and FCF throughout; positive for earnings quality (regulated and contracted share rising from about 60% to about 70% or more).

The loops, ranked by effect on the lines

Loop Type and direction Operating now? (dated evidence) Effect on the lines When
1. Fiscal stress → the state takes more (dividend, tax, levies, regulator claims) Reinforcing, negative for minorities Yes, measurably. Corporate tax 19% → 22% passed by the Senate on 6 November 2026; State Assets minister wants higher dividends (3 September 2026); PLN 265m extra Price Difference Fund demand (July 2026); 60% fuel levy; UD459; deficit about 7% of GDP Tax: -3.2% EPS from FY2027 (in base). A PLN 1bn levy: -PLN 0.33 EPS (-14% of FY2027) (scenario). Dividend: PLN 1.0bn to 1.6bn a year of debt 2027 onward; election autumn 2027
2. Solar and batteries → fewer coal hours → higher unit cost → closures → dependence on capacity payments that end in 2028 Reinforcing, negative Structurally yes (solar 25.6 GW in February 2026; over 450 negative-price hours in 2025; lignite output -11% in Q4 2025). Suspended in 2026 by the gas shock (Q2 generation +18%) and by energy-security politics Coal EBITDA 1.85 → 1.45 → 0.70 → -1.40bn (FY2026-FY2029) Resumes 2027; bites 2029
3. Debt-funded dividend and capital spending → leverage → ceiling → dividend or capital cut Balancing, on the dividend Starting: dividend resumes while FCF is negative Caps DPS from 2029; caps capital spending at about PLN 14bn 2029-2030
4. Bond yields → allowed distribution return, with a lag Balancing, positive for EPS Yes. Ten-year yield about 6.4% (October 2026) feeds the 18-month average used in the 2027-2028 tariffs Each point of return on a PLN 35bn to 40bn asset base: +PLN 0.35bn to 0.4bn of EBITDA. The same yields raise the cost of PLN 15bn to 20bn of new debt by 2029 (+PLN 0.15bn to 0.2bn). Net about +PLN 0.2bn per point 2027-2030
5. Asset-base growth → regulated EBITDA → debt capacity → more grid spending Reinforcing, positive Yes: distribution recurring EBITDA +12% in Q1 2026; grid spending above PLN 4bn a year Distribution 5.70 → 6.70bn (FY2026-FY2030) Continuous; bounded by loop 3
6. Energy windfall → political clawback Reinforcing on political risk Yes: the 2026 shock and an election year; gas cap draft with a levy Caps merchant upside; scenario of PLN 0.5bn to 1.5bn 2027
7. Network charges → self-generation and power purchase agreements → lower grid volume → higher charges Reinforcing for supply; balanced in distribution Yes for supply (Tariff A -25%, B -15%). Distribution is protected by annual cost-recovery tariffs, and self-generators still need connection and backup Supply: -PLN 0.05bn to 0.1bn a year; distribution: nil to 2030 Continuous
8. Batteries → lower capacity and balancing prices Balancing, negative for gas peakers and late storage Building: about 5 GW won availability auctions in three years; UK and Nordic saturation precedents Little before 2029 (PGE's contracts are fixed); weighs on post-2028 gas and storage returns 2029 onward
9. Data-centre requests → connected load → grid revenue Weak and unproven 2 GW of requests; Bernstein warns of an "artificial bubble"; permitting takes four to five years None before 2029 After 2029

Reasoning: the interplay that matters

  • Loops 1 and 3 collide in 2029. The state's fiscal need (loop 1) argues for paying the dividend regardless. The leverage ceiling (loop 3) argues for cutting it once coal turns negative. The state has gone both ways before:
  • in 2016 it cut a proposed dividend from PLN 0.92 to PLN 0.25 to fund coal mines and spending;
  • in 2026 it wants cash.
  • This is the single most important behavioural uncertainty for DPS in 2029-2030.
  • Loop 2 is not dead, only paused. The 2026 gas shock lifted coal's running hours and profits. When gas normalises (peace, or the Strait of Hormuz reopening, both named as sentiment triggers in the first set), coal displacement resumes at full force. Six weeks of falling gas prices would change the FY2027 coal number more than any company action.
  • Loop 4 is the under-appreciated positive. The brokers' flat distribution line assumes the return falls as yields fall. The 2026 sell-off reverses that assumption for the 2027-2028 tariffs. It is the main reason my distribution EBITDA exceeds the brokers' by PLN 0.4bn to 0.8bn in FY2026-FY2028.

How the loops shape the lines

  • EBITDA: the loops net out. Flat at PLN 13.6bn to 14.25bn through FY2028, a dip to PLN 12.65bn in FY2029, recovery to PLN 13.3bn in FY2030.
  • EPS: loops 1 (tax) and 3 (interest) depress it every year from FY2027.
  • FCF and net debt: loops 1 and 5 drive debt up. Loop 3 is the brake, and it works by cutting the dividend.

For the model

  • Make loop 1 (levy and tax) and loop 2's post-2028 support decision the two scenario switches.
  • Make loop 4 a sensitivity on the distribution return (±1 point = ±PLN 0.35bn to 0.4bn EBITDA from 2028).
  • Make loop 3 a rule: if economic leverage exceeds 3.3 times, DPS falls by half.

Current guidance and SS management, simple case and no SHMLP risks (and comparable track record)

Memo topic 4, : the Rater's score

6a

Guidance conservatism

D4719th of 253 answers
Judgement47C-6850C-7140
Group judgement

Guidance

D4719th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

PGE gives no near-term guidance. It gives specific, block-built 2030 targets now set in line with consensus, and a 2035 ambition double what independent analysts model.

  • Its behaviour is an "honest tracker": it cuts targets when reality changes and publishes preliminary results. Beat-and-raise is not a return source.
  • The 2030 target looks modest headline but is stretched underneath. The ex-coal business must grow about 8% to 10% a year to offset a planned coal swing of about PLN 4bn to 5bn.
  • The dividend ceiling of 5% is above the brokers' forecasts and depends on a state vote.

For the model: use the brokers' figures as the anchor for 2026-2027 and the segment targets as the upper bound for 2030.

C-68

To what extent is company guidance above or below sell-side consensus for this financial year and next financial year, and why?

D5017th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: PGE gives no guidance for FY2026 or FY2027. There is therefore no gap for those years. The only quantified targets are for 2030 and 2035.

Item Company Brokers
2030 EBITDA PLN 15bn Bloomberg consensus PLN 15.2bn; Bernstein PLN 14.6bn. Set deliberately in line
2035 EBITDA above PLN 30bn Bernstein PLN 16bn. Far above
Dividend from FY2026 earnings "up to 5%" yield, about PLN 0.79 a share at today's price PLN 0.55 (FactSet, paid 2027), then PLN 0.45 (paid 2028). Brokers sit below the ceiling

Why

  • The 2030 target was rebased to consensus after the 2025 strategy proved too optimistic on gas and merchant renewables.
  • The 2035 figure keeps the long-term ambition the state wants: offshore, nuclear, data centres.
  • The dividend ceiling reflects the state's fiscal interest. The brokers' lower figure reflects negative free cash flow and doubt about payout in a spending peak.

For the model: brokers' FY2026 EBITDA of PLN 13.37bn and FY2027 of PLN 13.34bn are the only near-term anchors. Each rests on four brokers.

C-71

How conservative is current guidance for the next one to three years against the last one to five years, adjusted for the difference in input change forces versus the past?

F4023rd of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: There is no one-to-three-year guidance. The nearest proxy, the 2030 path implied by the October 2026 targets, is not conservative once the change in input forces is taken into account.

The arithmetic

  • Group recurring EBITDA goes from PLN 12.9bn (2025) to PLN 15bn (2030), only +3% a year, which looks conservative.
  • But the target already embeds coal EBITDA at minus PLN 3.3bn in 2030, against about plus PLN 0.4bn in 2025 (Bernstein's conventional figure) and about PLN 2.1bn expected for 2026-2027.
  • Ex-coal EBITDA therefore has to grow from about PLN 11.5bn to 12.5bn to about PLN 18.3bn: about 8% to 10% a year for five years.
  • The last five years: regulated distribution grew from about PLN 3.5bn to PLN 5.3bn (2020-2025, about 8% to 9% a year, helped by the WACC rise of 2022-2024); heat recovered after 2023.

Against the input forces

  • The WACC tailwind has reversed (2026 cut).
  • Renewables face price cannibalisation; the target was already cut 28%.
  • Gas depends on post-2028 support.
  • Heat demand is structurally declining.
  • The 2025 base included an exceptional year for heat and capacity payments.

So delivering 8% to 10% a year ex-coal is stretched: achievable in distribution and offshore, doubtful in renewables, gas and heat. Bernstein's PLN 14.6bn is the more conservative anchor.

The dividend "up to 5%" is not conservative relative to free cash flow (negative through 2027). It is affordable against leverage headroom.

C-73

What else is material or uncommon about the company's guidance or investor relations?

D4517th of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: Five uncommon features matter.

  1. Preliminary results are published about ten days before reports, removing surprise from the report day. For Q3 2026 (report on 24 November 2026), expect preliminary figures around mid-November.
  2. Non-standard metrics: "recurring EBITDA" and "economic net debt" are the metrics to model. Both are well explained, but "recurring" excludes items that recur.
  3. The dividend is decided by the state's vote at the AGM, about June 2027. The proposal can be overridden, as in 2016. Under the 2026 policy it is based on net profit excluding non-cash impairments, and "subject to leverage and coal market conditions".
  4. Strategy is updated roughly every 12 to 18 months, usually after a change of minister or management.
  5. Investor relations is accessible to a wide domestic analyst pool (BOŚ, IPOPEMA, Noble, Trigon, Pekao, PKO BP, Erste, Wood & Company, Citigroup, Patria, Santander) and Bernstein internationally. FactSet sees only three to five brokers' numbers, so "consensus" in FactSet is thin and can move sharply on one broker's update.
C-74

To what extent have sell-side estimates been cut deeply and fully across all analysts, and has company guidance recently been rebased lower, or more aggressively kitchen-sinked or sandbagged?

F3523rd of 25

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: The opposite of a cut. Estimates have been raised sharply and broadly in the last three to 12 months.

FY2027 revisions (FactSet)

Measure 1 month 3 months 6 months 12 months
EPS +13% +64% +49% +54%
EBITDA +5% +13% +18% n/a
Sales +10% +38% n/a n/a
Target price +23% +37% +42% n/a
  • FY2026 EPS: +44% over three months; +72% over 12 months.
  • Sell-side earnings momentum is graded A.

Kitchen sinks and rebasing

  • The 2025 write-downs were a kitchen sink by circumstance, not by design. Q2 2025 net loss PLN 9.6bn after the coal agency was abandoned; the coal fleet written to near zero; a PLN 12bn parent loan impairment; onerous provisions. This created a low base and lower future coal depreciation, which flatters FY2026-27 EPS growth.
  • The October 2026 targets were rebased lower, but to consensus, not below it. That is not sandbagging.

What this means

  • The estimate setup is "upgraded and extended", not "cut and washed out."
  • The breadth of conviction lags the numbers. Only 29% of brokers rate a buy, down 21 points in one month. The mean rating is Hold. Targets range from PLN 9.30 (Wood, Sell) to PLN 20.00 (BOS, Buy). The mean target of PLN 15.56 is below the price.
  • This is the Wisdom Library's "sell-side buy rating versus revisions divergence": the brokers raised numbers on cyclical conditions while declining to endorse the price.
  • The EPS upgrade is about five times the EBITDA upgrade. Part of the lift is below EBITDA (lower depreciation after the write-down, lower finance costs), a lower-quality source.
6c

Case simplicity

7 answers
Judgement47C-7948D-0260D-0344D-0440D-0562D-0645
Group judgement

Risks

F477th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The forecasts for the next 18 months are predictable in shape and direction, moderately predictable in EBITDA, and poorly predictable in the level of EPS and in cash. They are most predictable where the valuation will soon stop looking, and least predictable where it will soon start.

What supports predictability

  • The structure holds. Two independent sets and a third body of research agree on it: a regulated and contracted engine rising, coal running to a cliff dated December 2028, policy as the axis (D-01).
  • The 18-month forecasts rest on demonstrated strengths and signed contracts (D-02). About 60% of EBITDA is regulated or contracted, the coal capacity payments are locked to 2028, and the first half of 2026 is banked.
  • The EPS decline inside the window is about 80% scheduled: depreciation, interest and tax (D-03).
  • The monitoring calendar is unusually good. Most FY2027 swing inputs become hard numbers by the end of December 2026 (D-05).

What undermines it

  • Five numerical joints between the earlier sets needed correcting (D-01): distribution (input changed), the size of the coal cliff, the leverage definition, the tax rise treated as law, and the trough arithmetic. Three of the five are negative; two are slightly positive.
  • The drivers are concentrated in a few decision-makers whose choices are correlated through affordability politics (D-03).
  • The cone is negatively skewed (D-04): FY2027 EBITDA of minus 8% to plus 5%; EPS of minus 19% to plus 13%.
  • PGE gives no bridge from EBITDA to EPS (D-06), so the brokers' EPS is soft and the share price, which followed those estimates (D-07), rests on it.

The steer for the memo

  1. Separate the two horizons explicitly. FY2026 and FY2027 EBITDA are forecastable to within a few percent. FY2029 EPS is a policy scenario with a cone twice as wide. During 2027 the 24-month forward window moves from the first to the second.
  2. Carry three switches, not a single base case, until the end of December 2026: the tax signature, the reinvestment premium, and the funding of the household shield. If the memo is written before they resolve, it should say which way each moves the numbers. If it can wait seven weeks, half the FY2027 cone disappears.
  3. Compare with the brokers on EBITDA, value on EPS, and treat EPS comparisons as uncertain by about 5% either way.
  4. State the definition beside every regulatory or contractual figure. Each error found in the earlier sets was an error of definition.

C-79

How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

D4817th of 25

Layer 1 · Company - Economic strength

Judgement: Geographically narrow; by product, wide and widening.

  • Geography: one country, about 98% to 100% of revenue in Poland.
  • Segments (eight): coal energy, gas-fired generation, renewables, district heating, distribution, supply, railway energy services, other. Offshore is now reported separately in broker forecasts.
  • New lines being added: storage, data centres, nuclear, heat-network purchases.

What it means for predictability

  • Single country: all earnings share one regulator, one government, one currency and one sovereign risk premium. Predictability depends on Polish policy, so outcomes are correlated rather than diversified.
  • Many segments: they partly offset each other. The integrated hedge means high power prices help generation and hurt supply; low prices do the reverse. Weather lifts heat and distribution together. That diversification reduces volatility at group level within a year.
  • Widening product perimeter: it adds execution complexity and long-dated, uncertain cash flows (nuclear, data centres). It reduces predictability beyond 2030.

For the model: segment-level forecasting with a single policy scenario driving several segments at once (household tariff, levy, coal support, WACC). Correlation, not diversification, is the right assumption for downside cases.

D-02

To what extent does past and present fundamental delivery make the expected change plausible, and to what extent does the thesis rely only on strengths the business demonstrably has, rather than on strengths it would need to acquire?

D604th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: for the next 18 months the forecasts rest almost wholly on strengths PGE has demonstrated or on revenue already contracted, so the expected change is plausible. From 2029 they rest on three things PGE has not demonstrated: losing less in lignite than its own plan says, receiving coal support that has not been granted, and keeping a dividend through negative cash flow under a state vote. Plausibility is high where the 18-month question looks, and low where the valuation will be looking by the end of 2027.

Each forecast element, sorted by what it rests on

Forecast element Rests on Demonstrated? Evidence
Distribution EBITDA growing 2% to 5% a year Asset-base growth; the regulator's premium Yes for the asset base; the premium is the regulator's Asset base plus 9% in 2026; first-half 2026 EBITDA plus 5% despite a return cut of about 2.5 points; but a 2025 tariff rise of 0.4% against 3.5% to 4.1% for peers
Baltica 2 (PLN 0.35bn in FY2027, PLN 1.05bn in FY2028) Delivering a contracted project with Ørsted Yes for construction; operation is new 45% complete; all 111 monopiles installed by August 2026; budget unchanged at PLN 30bn; DNB Carnegie calls it the cleanest execution in Ørsted's portfolio
Coal EBITDA positive to 2028 Capacity contracts already won Yes Five-year contracts to 2028; 1,859 MW for 2027 at about PLN 326 per kW
Funding the programme Balance sheet and state-linked loans Yes Lowest leverage of Polish peers (Fitch); PLN 11.1bn of project finance; PLN 12.8bn of 0.5% loans undrawn
Dividend of PLN 0.70 in 2027 The state's wish for cash A coincidence of interest, not a strength The state forced a higher dividend in 2012 and cut one in 2016
Coal at minus PLN 1.4bn (FY2029) and minus PLN 2.0bn (FY2030) Removing cost from lignite; new support No See below
Supply recovering to PLN 0.4bn to 0.5bn The household tariff No Tariff below cost in 2026; management itself guides about PLN 0.4bn a year for 2026 to 2029
Gas at PLN 1.4bn in FY2030 A capacity framework after 2028 Conditional New gas is conditional in the strategy

The strengths PGE would need to acquire

  1. Taking cost out of a fixed-cost lignite business. - PGE's 2016 programme (PLN 500m of controllable cost by 2020) was offset by carbon costs and lost volume (Deutsche Bank, 2016). - ZE PAK's costs fell by less than its output. - RWE and LEAG closed lignite with EUR 2.6bn and EUR 1.75bn of state compensation. - PGE's programme has no published metrics, staffing plan or closure schedule, and Erste judges it high-risk because of union and political opposition. Nearly all PGE employees are represented by unions or works councils.
  2. Obtaining support after 2028. The grid operator confirmed talks with Brussels in April 2026. The Commission has approved strategic reserves for Estonia (EUR 750m, October 2025) and Sweden (EUR 300m, July 2025), so a legal template exists. Nothing has been proposed for Poland, and reserve units must stay out of the market, which pays fixed costs but no margin.
  3. Holding a dividend through negative free cash flow. There is no precedent at PGE, and its Polish peers' dividends have been suspended or put at risk under the same pressure.

What the precedents say

  • Of the state-controlled utilities with coal fleets that attempted this change, the ones that delivered had an exit or compensation for coal: Public Power Corporation (lignite exit by end-2026, the state below 34%) and RWE (compensation), with CEZ partly (accelerated depreciation, a dated exit). None delivered medium-term targets while carrying an unsupported lignite fleet.
  • PGE's own record: the 2014 to 2020 plan was missed, and the 2030 EBITDA target was cut from PLN 17bn to 15bn within 16 months.

The insight: the order in time

  • The demonstrated strengths are used first, in 2026 to 2028. The undemonstrated ones are called on from 2029. So the 18-month forecasts are more plausible than the four-year ones.
  • But the stock is valued on earnings 24 months forward. That window reaches 2029 during 2027. The plausible part of the forecast is the part the valuation leaves behind first.

One further reading of management's coal figure

Management published minus PLN 3.3bn while negotiating support with the state. It has an incentive to show a large hole. That argues the true unsupported loss may be smaller, which is the case for the second set's minus PLN 2.0bn. It is a reading of incentives, not evidence of a strength.

D-03In relation to the differences between the forecasts over the next 18 months to four years, vs. the last four to six years' financials, how diffuse or concentrated are the key drivers that generate the differences?F447th of 8full answer under 2a Internal risks
D-04

How wide is the cone of plausible outcomes over the next 18 months, how skewed is it, and so how predictable is the case?

F408th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the cone is narrow for FY2026 EBITDA, moderate for FY2027 EBITDA (about minus 8% to plus 5% around PLN 13.4bn) and wide for FY2027 EPS (about minus 19% to plus 13% around PLN 2.35). The skew is negative: the probability-weighted FY2027 EBITDA is about PLN 0.2bn to 0.3bn below the central figure. The case is predictable in shape and direction, moderately predictable in EBITDA, and poorly predictable in the level of EPS and in cash. About half of the width comes from state decisions due before the end of December 2026, so the cone should narrow sharply within seven weeks.

The window. Seven earnings events: the third quarter of 2026 (24 November 2026) to the first quarter of 2028 (about May 2028). The financial years in play are FY2026 and FY2027.

The cone, line by line

Measure Central (mine) Plausible range (about four chances in five) What sets the width
FY2026 EBITDA PLN 13.57bn 13.2bn to 13.9bn (plus or minus 2.5%) First half banked at PLN 8.10bn; the second half is PLN 5.47bn plus or minus 0.35bn
FY2027 EBITDA PLN 13.37bn 12.3bn to 14.1bn The table below
FY2027 adjusted EPS PLN 2.35 1.90 to 2.65 PLN 1bn of EBITDA is PLN 0.33 of EPS; the tax signature is PLN 0.08
FY2027 reported EPS about PLN 2.10 to 2.25 Wider still Recurring "one-offs" of PLN 0.10 to 0.27; Baltica 2 derivative revaluations of up to PLN 0.10 a share in a single quarter
FY2027 free cash flow about minus PLN 5.6bn minus 3.5bn to minus 7.5bn Capital spending of PLN 13bn to 17.5bn
Dividend paid in 2027 PLN 0.70 0.55 to 0.80 The state's vote in about June 2027

What makes the FY2027 EBITDA cone

Driver Effect on FY2027 EBITDA Probability When it resolves
A levy or revenue cap on generators minus PLN 0.5bn to 1.5bn 25% to 35% The 2027 shield package (November to December 2026); then any time before the autumn 2027 election
Household tariff further below cost than I assume, without compensation minus PLN 0.3bn about 30% The regulator's decision, about mid-December 2026
Household tariff covers cost plus PLN 0.2bn about 20% Same
Reinvestment premium one point above or below my assumption plus or minus PLN 0.25bn Symmetric Same
Gas and power prices fall back faster than assumed minus PLN 0.5bn about 25% Continuous
2027 forward prices hold near PLN 590/MWh for the unsold volume plus PLN 0.4bn about 30% Continuous; visible in the hedging through the first half of 2027
Baltica 2 first power slips by two quarters minus PLN 0.2bn about 15% First half of 2027
Tax rise vetoed plus PLN 0.08 of EPS; no EBITDA effect about one in three By about 27 November 2026
  • Probability-weighted: FY2027 EBITDA of about PLN 13.1bn and adjusted EPS of about PLN 2.29.
  • Joint downside (a PLN 1bn levy, a wider tariff gap and a lower premium together): about PLN 11.8bn of EBITDA and PLN 1.85 of EPS. I put it at about one chance in ten. It is more likely than its parts multiplied, because the parts share a cause (D-03).

Four judgements on the shape of the cone

1. The tariff cap and the levy are one risk seen twice.

  • In 2023 to 2025 household price caps came with compensation, and it was paid promptly. PGE booked PLN 7.66bn of compensation revenue in 2023, and its compensation receivable fell from PLN 1.02bn to PLN 0.47bn in the first nine months of 2025. Supply was made whole.
  • The money came partly from generators. PGE paid PLN 6.57bn into the Price Difference Fund in 2023. The regulator's audits then came back in 2026 for a further PLN 605m and PLN 265m.
  • So a cap is not itself the risk. The risk is who pays for it. For 2027 the draft gas cap is paid for by a 10% levy on the dominant gas trader. The cost of the electricity shield may exceed 0.5% of GDP (Deutsche Bank, 6 November 2026), and its funding is undecided. The State Assets minister said in October 2026 that windfall taxes remain an option.
  • The single thing to watch in the shield package is whether its funding reaches power generators. The first set's two separate risks (tariff, levy) should be carried in the memo as one.

2. The gas shock reaches 2027 through forward prices, not spot prices, and that is the main upside.

  • PGE sold much of its 2026 and 2027 coal output before the shock. The onerous-contract provisions at the coal subsidiary (PLN 0.7bn to 1.0bn during 2026) are the losses on those sales, already booked outside recurring EBITDA.
  • So 2027 coal earnings are less exposed to gas prices falling back than spot spreads suggest. The unsold part is being priced against a 2027 forward of PLN 590/MWh (mid-September 2026), where PGE's own planning assumes a 2% rise in 2027.
  • The second set's "normalisation" (minus PLN 0.65bn across coal and gas in FY2027) is therefore at the cautious end. That is where the upper half of the cone comes from.

3. The left tail is fatter than the right. The downside drivers are correlated through affordability politics before the election. The upside drivers (forward prices, a cost-covering tariff) invite the clawback that produces the downside. This is the asymmetry the second set described as "upside capped, downside unprotected", and it is what makes the skew negative.

4. The cone narrows quickly. The tax signature, the premium, the household tariff, the shield design and the December capacity auction all fall before the end of December 2026. Together they are about half of the width of the FY2027 cone. What remains after December is the levy risk through 2027 and wholesale prices.

Against the record

The cone is consistent with PGE's results against the brokers' expectations: beats of about 2% to 6% in FY2022 to FY2024 and a miss of about 9% in FY2025. A range of minus 8% to plus 5% is what history gives.

Beyond the window, for information

FY2029 adjusted EPS ranges from about PLN 1.1 (no coal support) to about PLN 2.0 (support of about PLN 1.5bn a year and a better distribution return), around a central PLN 1.37. That is roughly minus 20% to plus 45%: a cone about twice as wide as FY2027's, and two-sided.

D-05

How helpful is the company's disclosure on operating momentum and key drivers of the thesis to monitor the case over about the next six months?

C+623rd of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: helpful, and more helpful than the absence of guidance suggests. Over the next six months nearly every swing input for FY2027 will be published as a hard number on a known date. Most of those numbers come from third parties (the regulator, the President, the grid operator, Ørsted), not from PGE. PGE's own disclosure is good on outcomes and poor on leading indicators.

What PGE discloses well

  • Recurring EBITDA for eight segments every quarter, with one-off items itemised.
  • Preliminary figures about ten days before each report, so the news arrives early and the report day rarely surprises.
  • Economic net debt, which includes the CO2 obligations.
  • Sensitivities (CO2, currency, volume, margin, discount rate).
  • Capacity contract volumes and prices, and capital commitments by segment (PLN 22.3bn at June 2026).

What PGE does not disclose

  • Its household tariff application. Management declined to give it on the second-quarter call.
  • Its hedge book: the share of 2027 output sold and at what price, and the cost of power bought for 2027 household supply.
  • The distribution asset base and the total allowed return. Both earlier sets relied on Bernstein for these and had the base wrong by about PLN 5bn.
  • Capacity revenue by segment and year. That is why the earlier sets could not size the cliff.
  • The Efficiency Improvement Programme's measures, staffing and closures.
  • A payout ratio. "Up to 5%" is a ceiling on yield, not a formula.

The six-month calendar

Three points on using this

1. The onerous-contract provisions are PGE's best leading indicator, and neither earlier set used them as one.

  • They are management's own estimate of next period's loss, booked in advance, on household supply and on forward-sold coal output.
  • Supply: PLN 436m at end-2025, PLN 327m at March 2026, PLN 218m at June 2026. This is the 2026 household loss being used up at PLN 109m a quarter.
  • Coal: PLN 766m at end-2025, PLN 1,010m at March 2026, PLN 697m at June 2026.
  • Tauron and Energa announced their 2026 provisions (PLN 146m and PLN 123m) within days of the December 2025 tariff decision. PGE's announcement after the December 2026 decision will give the 2027 supply loss before 2027 begins.

2. Two tests for the third-quarter release.

  • Distribution. Second-half EBITDA of about PLN 2.7bn supports my PLN 5.50bn. About PLN 2.9bn would restore the second set's PLN 5.70bn.
  • Capital spending. The second set's PLN 13.0bn for FY2026 needs PLN 8.1bn to 8.7bn in the second half, against PLN 6.6bn in the second half of 2025, after a first half that was flat year on year (PLN 4.92bn against PLN 4.91bn on PGE's figures; FactSet shows PLN 4.29bn). A nine-month figure below about PLN 8bn would point to PLN 12bn or less for the year. The spending would be deferred into 2027, not saved. I have left the input alone and flag it as the next most likely change.

3. What cannot be monitored. The coal support talks have no clock. A levy can be announced on any day. The method for distribution returns after 2028 is unknown even to PGE. These are the items that matter most beyond the window, so disclosure is strong for the 18-month question and weak for the valuation question.

D-06

How clearly does what the company says it will achieve trace into specific operational drivers, onto identifiable revenue, cost and margin lines (the key fundamental milestones and measures), and through to earnings per share; and how clear is the external transmission from value-chain dynamics to earnings per share?

F458th of 8

It says nothing about depreciation, interest or tax, which is where most of the change in EPS arises. The internal trace, aim by aim

What PGE says Operating driver Line Clear as far as Where it breaks
"Double the regulated asset base by 2035"; PLN 36bn of grid spending to 2030 Asset base times (7.478% plus the premium), plus regulatory depreciation, plus a return on 35% of planned spending, times a quality factor of 0.9 to 1.1 Distribution EBITDA EBITDA The premium is set yearly and not known in advance; book depreciation and the funding cost of the new assets are not given
"Baltica 2 in full operation in the second half of 2027" About 3 TWh a year (PGE's share) at an indexed strike price (PLN 443.49/MWh in 2025) Offshore EBITDA of about PLN 1.1bn EBITDA See the worked example below
Coal EBITDA of minus PLN 3.3bn in 2030 Capacity contracts (volume and price disclosed), plus spread times volume, less fixed cost Coal EBITDA EPS Nothing breaks: the fleet is written down, so EBITDA falls to EPS almost one for one
Supply EBITDA of about PLN 0.4bn a year Household tariff against the cost of power bought Supply EBITDA, through the onerous provision EBITDA The inputs are not disclosed
Dividend "up to 5%" Net profit excluding non-cash impairments Dividend per share Not clear No payout ratio; the state votes
Net debt below 3.5 times EBITDA; 1.6 times on average in 2026 to 2029 Capital spending, project finance, grants Net debt on the bank definition Net debt The definition excludes project finance and CO2 obligations

The worked example: Baltica 2 adds about PLN 1.1bn to EBITDA and roughly nothing to EPS in its first full year (my estimate)

  • EBITDA: about PLN 1.05bn to 1.1bn.
  • Depreciation: about PLN 0.6bn a year on PGE's PLN 15bn share over 25 years.
  • Interest: capitalisation stops at commissioning. On PLN 5.5bn to 11.1bn of project debt (the sources differ on PGE's share) at 4.5% to 6.5%, interest is PLN 0.25bn to 0.7bn.
  • Pre-tax result: between plus PLN 0.25bn and minus PLN 0.2bn. EPS: between plus PLN 0.08 and minus PLN 0.07.
  • The largest single positive step in the EBITDA forecast leaves EPS about where it was. The coal step-down, by contrast, reaches EPS in full. This is the plainest way to show the Consumer why flat EBITDA produces halved EPS.

A caution on PGE's own leverage projection

PGE projects net debt of 1.6 times EBITDA on average in 2026 to 2029 and 2.8 times in 2030. That is on the bank definition and on PGE's own EBITDA targets and full capital plan. It cannot be set beside the second set's economic leverage of 2.9 to 3.2 times. Restated to the bank definition, our forecasts give about 2.1 to 2.5 times in 2030, below management's own figure, because we assume about 71% of the planned capital spending. The two views of the balance sheet are closer than they look.

The external transmission, link by link

From To Speed Filter
Gas, CO2 and power prices Coal and gas EBITDA One to two years Forward sales; CO2 fully hedged; losses on early sales appear first as onerous provisions
Wholesale power price Supply margin Once a year The regulator's household tariff, then the government's cap
Bond yields Distribution return Not before 2029 The base return is fixed for 2023 to 2028
Bond yields Interest cost Immediate on new borrowing 77% of existing debt is fixed; new commercial debt costs 6.5% to 7%
Equipment prices Capital spending, then depreciation Two to four years Contracts already signed for Baltica 2, Rybnik and the peakers
The state budget Tax, levies, the dividend Sudden None
  • Higher bond yields are, for 2027 and 2028, a pure cost to PGE. They raise the cost of new debt and do not raise the allowed return. The second set's "under-appreciated positive" (its loop 4) does not operate in the window.
  • The 2026 energy shock arrives in PGE's earnings in 2027 (through forward prices), together with its political cost (the shield and its funding). The benefit and the clawback land in the same year.

The consequence for the brokers' figures

Because PGE does not guide below EBITDA, each broker fills the gap alone, and they fill it differently. For FY2027, Trigon reaches EPS of PLN 2.60 from EBITDA of PLN 14.26bn; Erste reaches the same PLN 2.60 from PLN 12.20bn. The brokers' median EPS is therefore a soft figure (I-02).

C-82

What perception- or sentiment-driven risks are there that are sudden and high-magnitude, including events elsewhere in the sector, theme or market that could hit the share price whatever the company's own fundamentals?

F3021st of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: Sentiment risk is high because the shares and the cohort are extended.

Where the shares stand

  • +74% in the three months to mid-October; +40% over 12 months; relative strength at the 97th percentile.
  • Price 38% above the 200-day average; a peak of PLN 17.55, now PLN 15.755.
  • The cohort of central and south-east European state utilities is at the 95th percentile of its own valuation range.
  • The Fitness for Fighting digest is risk-off beneath an index at its high: Fragility at Double Underweight, Momentum at Underweight, leveraged defensives de-rating first.

Triggers that could hit the price regardless of PGE's results

  1. A Polish sovereign or currency event: a rating downgrade, a bond sell-off, or EUR/PLN beyond 4.47.
  2. US ten-year yields rising further (already above 5%), hitting utilities as bond proxies.
  3. Gas prices falling back (peace or Hormuz normalisation), which reverses the "windfall for existing assets" trade that drove the cohort.
  4. Cohort contagion: - Tauron or Enea news (Tauron at 7.0 times P/E at the 96th percentile; Enea at the 97th); - a coal or levy headline from Warsaw; - the Energa squeeze-out.
  5. The 2027 election campaign: utilities used as political tools.
  6. Broker downgrades on valuation: - the buy share fell 21 points in a month to 29%; - the mean target of PLN 15.56 is below the price; - Wood & Company has a Sell at PLN 9.30; Noble a Hold at PLN 8.95 (December 2025).
  7. Emerging-market risk-off and foreign outflows from Warsaw.

Interplay: the share price rise was driven by results, the capacity auction and the energy shock, "rather than concrete progress on coal spinoff negotiations" (Key Question report). Sentiment rests on cyclical drivers that can reverse quickly.

Track record (guidance raise/sell-side upgrades) ~L2-3Y

4 answers
C-6155C-7038C-6952P-0645
C-61How strong is the company's record of acting, not just talking: a recent record of huge delivery and action, and a mid-term track record of delivery, not misses or justifications, whatever the environment (outperforming in tougher ones is even better), beyond its records against guidance and estimates?D5516th of 25full answer under 4b Management quality
C-70

Beyond meeting or beating, what is the record, nature and behaviour of the company and its chief executive in upgrading guidance?

F3821st of 25

Layer 1 · Guidance

Judgement: PGE has no record of upgrading guidance. It has a record of revising long-term targets downward when reality changes, which Bernstein calls an "honest-tracker" archetype.

  • Strategy targets were revised in February 2012, the 2014-2020 plan, June 2025 and October 2026.
  • The latest revisions were downward: 2030 EBITDA from PLN 17bn to 15bn; capital spending from PLN 235bn to 210bn; gas and renewables targets cut.
  • Upward revisions: district heating to PLN 3.4bn from PLN 2.7bn, and the dividend.
  • The chief executive has been in place only ten months and has made one revision, mostly down.
  • Results management: PGE publishes preliminary quarterly figures, so reported results rarely surprise. That suits a tracker, not an upgrader.

For the investment case, "beat and raise" is not a source of returns here. Returns must come from estimate revisions driven by external conditions (spreads, policy) or from re-rating.

C-69

What is the company's record of missing, meeting or beating guidance and sell-side estimates over the last one, three and five years, including in comparable environmental conditions?

C-5212th of 25

Layer 1 · Sell-side estimates

Judgement: The record against sell-side EBITDA estimates is good in high-price, regulated-growth years and weak when write-downs or regulatory shocks hit. Against its own strategy targets, PGE has repeatedly fallen short.

Last year

  • FY2025 EBITDA missed by about 8.6% (Change Forces approximation), on onerous contracts and conventional weakness.
  • Q1 2026 was in line with preliminary figures. Recurring EBITDA was PLN 4.14bn, -5% year on year. Supply was -50%; distribution +12%; heat +25%.
  • Q2 2026 EBITDA of PLN 4.11bn was "fully in line with market estimates". Recurring was PLN 3.96bn, +21%.

Three years: beats in FY2023 and FY2024 (+5.6%, +6.2%), led by distribution and heat. FY2025 missed.

Five years: FY2022 beat (+1.8%). FY2021 was a record EBITDA year (PLN 9.5bn).

Strategy targets

  • The 2014-2020 plan missed.
  • The 2025 strategy's 2030 EBITDA (PLN 17bn) was cut to PLN 15bn within 16 months.
  • The coal carve-out was not delivered.
  • Baltica 3 was delayed.
  • Net income has been negative in four of the last seven years (2019, 2023, 2024, 2025) on write-downs.

Comparable environment: in the 2021-2022 energy shock, EBITDA beat. In the 2026 shock, it is meeting or beating. Expect FY2026 to meet or beat, and expect write-down risk to return as conditions normalise.

P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

D4512th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: weight falls steeply with time, and the lean changes sign.

Forecast Weight My base against the brokers Lean
FY2026 EBITDA High (about 80% confidence within ±4%) 13.75bn against 13.37bn Up
FY2027 EBITDA Medium (±8%) 13.60bn against 13.34bn Slightly up, with a negative policy skew
FY2027-FY2028 EPS Medium-low 2.43 and 2.26 against 2.60 and 2.47 Down about 7% to 8%
FY2029-FY2030 EBITDA and EPS Low (±15% to 20% on EBITDA) 12.65bn against the one visible broker's 14.09bn Down
Reported EPS, every year n/a n/a Below adjusted by PLN 0.10 to 0.27
FY2028 FCF n/a -4.9bn against the brokers' +1.6bn Down
DPS paid in 2027 n/a 0.70 against 0.55 Up
DPS paid in 2029-2030 n/a n/a Down: the leverage ceiling and the coal cliff

PGE's record is good at delivering what is contracted or regulated and poor at delivering targets that depend on markets, the state or structural change. So the forecasts deserve weight in proportion to the share of each year's earnings that is contracted or regulated. That share is high in FY2026-FY2027 and falls in FY2029, when coal capacity payments end.

Reasoning and evidence: the record

  • Against consensus (approximate; PGE gives no annual guidance):
  • EBITDA beats of about +1.8% (FY2022), +5.6% (FY2023) and +6.2% (FY2024) in high-price and regulated-growth years;
  • a miss of about -8.6% in FY2025, on onerous contracts and conventional weakness;
  • Q1 and Q2 2026 in line. Preliminary figures, published about ten days before each report, remove report-day surprise.
  • Against its own targets, repeatedly short:
  • the 2014-2020 plan missed;
  • the 2030 EBITDA target was cut from PLN 17bn to 15bn within 16 months;
  • capital spending to 2035 was cut from PLN 235bn to 210bn;
  • the coal carve-out was not delivered;
  • Baltica 3 was delayed to about 2032;
  • reported net income was negative in four of the last seven years (2019, 2023, 2024, 2025) on write-downs.
  • On physical projects, good:
  • Dolna Odra (1.36 GW) and Gryfino (1.34 GW) CCGTs were delivered, while Energa's Ostrołęka and Grudziądz slipped;
  • Baltica 2's 111 foundations were installed on schedule by August 2026; DNB Carnegie (August 2026) singles it out as flagging no schedule or cost concerns;
  • capacity auctions were won.
  • The brokers' estimates are thin and recent. Three to four brokers for FY2026-FY2028, one for FY2029. FY2027 EPS was raised 64% in three months. The visible brokers span a wide range:
  • FY2027 EPS: Trigon 2.60, Erste 2.60, PKO 2.08;
  • FY2028 EBITDA: Erste 12.7bn to BOS 15.4bn.
  • One broker's update moves "consensus" sharply.

Why each lean

  • FY2026 EBITDA up:
  • H1 is banked at PLN 8.10bn;
  • Q3 2026 carried the energy shock (2027 forwards rose to PLN 590/MWh in September) and locked capacity payments;
  • the brokers' figure implies a flat H2 after a +21% Q2.
  • FY2027-FY2028 EPS down:
  • the tax rise, worth about 3% of EPS, is not in estimates dated 16-21 October;
  • coal: my PLN 1.45bn and 0.70bn against the brokers' 2.14bn and 1.08bn;
  • interest on capital spending kept near PLN 15.5bn, against the brokers' PLN 14.0bn and 10.7bn.
  • My higher distribution and heat partly offset these.
  • FY2029 down: the single visible FY2029 forecast shows no coal cliff. Management itself plans coal at minus PLN 3.3bn in 2030.
  • Reported below adjusted: "non-recurring" items recur:
  • onerous-contract provisions in 2023, 2025 and Q1 2026;
  • Price Difference Fund corrections every year 2023-2026;
  • impairments in 2025 and Q2 2026 (PLN 248m, renewables).
  • Carry PLN 0.3bn to 0.8bn a year before tax.
  • DPS 2027 up:
  • the October 2026 policy is "up to 5%", or PLN 0.79 at today's price;
  • the state's fiscal need is explicit;
  • FY2026 adjusted EPS near PLN 2.8 makes PLN 0.70 a 25% payout.
  • The brokers' PLN 0.55 predates or discounts the policy.
  • Management's 2030 target should be read as at best met. PGE behaves as an "honest tracker": it cuts targets when reality changes and does not beat and raise. My FY2030 EBITDA is PLN 13.3bn against the PLN 15bn target and Bernstein's PLN 14.6bn.

For the next Analyst

  • Treat the FY2026-FY2027 EBITDA forecast as close to an anchor.
  • Treat the FY2027-FY2028 EPS gap to consensus as the most probable revision: mechanical, and dated to the next broker updates after the tax law and the Q3 2026 results.
  • Treat FY2029-FY2030 as a policy scenario, not a forecast.

Group judgement

Financial forecasts

F3417th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The financial forecast for PGE is a high plateau of EBITDA under a falling line of EPS and a rising line of debt.

The plateau is real and well supported for 18 months

  • H1 2026 is banked.
  • Capacity payments are locked to 2028.
  • 60% of EBITDA is regulated or contracted.
  • Baltica 2 is on schedule.
  • My FY2026-FY2027 EBITDA (PLN 13.75bn and 13.60bn) sits at or above the brokers'. The distribution return, supported by the 2026 bond sell-off, is the main reason.

EPS falls about 48% from FY2026 to FY2030, mostly for mechanical reasons

  • Depreciation on the new asset base.
  • Interest on a capital programme and a dividend funded by debt.
  • A corporate tax rise already passed by the Senate.
  • These are the most predictable parts of the forecast, and they are not yet fully in the brokers' numbers. The likely revision path for FY2027-FY2028 EPS is down about 7% to 8%, even if EBITDA is met.

Cash is negative every year

  • Leverage triples, to about 3 times economic net debt to EBITDA by 2029-2030.
  • The new dividend is affordable only against leverage headroom, not against cash.

The cliff is dated (end-2028) but its size is not

  • PLN 1.0bn to 2.5bn of lost coal support.
  • The state's post-2028 decision, which has no clock, sets it.
  • With the 2027 election and a deficit near 7% of GDP, the policy skew is negative over the next 12 months and two-sided after 2028.

For the third and fourth sets

  • The near-term EBITDA forecast is predictable.
  • The near-term EPS forecast is predictable in direction (down against consensus).
  • FY2029-FY2030 is a policy scenario.
  • The dividend is the variable where the state's two interests, cash now and solvency later, collide in 2029.

P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

F3017th of 17

Layer 2 · Financial forecasts

Judgement: the items below operating profit are the main reason EPS falls, and the most predictable part of the forecast.

  • From FY2026 to FY2029, EPS falls PLN 1.32 (2.82 to 1.50). Of that:
  • depreciation explains PLN 0.50;
  • net interest PLN 0.37;
  • the tax rise PLN 0.09;
  • lower EBITDA PLN 0.37.
  • So about 72% of the decline is below EBITDA and largely mechanical: assets entering service, debt already planned, a tax law already passed. Only 28% depends on the uncertain coal cliff.
  • No buybacks, no issuance in the base, so the share count is flat at 2,243.7m.
  • Against the last four to six years: net income was dominated by write-downs. Over the next four years, depreciation, interest and tax dominate. That makes earnings more predictable, but lower per share.

EPS bridge, FY2026 to FY2029 and FY2030 (PLN per share)

Step FY2026 → FY2029 FY2026 → FY2030
FY2026 adjusted EPS 2.82 2.82
EBITDA change (−1.10bn and −0.48bn), after tax −0.37 −0.16

Each item

  • Depreciation: PLN 4.05bn (FY2025) → 4.70bn → 5.05bn → 5.80bn → 6.20bn → 6.60bn.
  • Coal depreciation has fallen to about PLN 0.24bn a year after the write-down and stays low.
  • Increases come from four places: grid assets (about PLN 7bn a year of network spending to 2030 at 30-to-40-year lives); Rybnik CCGT (2027); Żarnowiec battery (Q2 2027); and Baltica 2 from end-2027 (PGE's 50% share of about PLN 30bn, consolidated as a joint operation under IFRS 11, so its EBITDA, debt and depreciation sit in PGE's accounts).
  • Brokers: PLN 4.82bn, 5.10bn, 5.95bn, close to mine.
  • Net financial items: see P-02 (-0.70bn → -2.10bn).
  • The CfD derivatives are carried at zero but will be noisy: minus PLN 249m in Q1 2026 finance costs; plus PLN 267m in EBITDA and minus PLN 300m in finance costs in Q2 2026.
  • At 30 June 2026 the inflation derivative was worth minus PLN 1,504m (PGE's share minus PLN 752m). A 10% rise in forecast inflation adds about PLN 236m of income.
  • The euro option was valued at PLN 2,563m at recognition and moves with EUR/PLN.
  • Expect swings of ±PLN 0.3bn a quarter in reported profit through 2027 and beyond. Brokers exclude them (Bernstein explicitly), and so does the dividend base.
  • Tax: effective 23.02% in Q1 2026 at a 19% statutory rate, so about four points of non-deductible leakage. With the statutory rate at 22% from 2027, I use 25.5%.
  • Risk to the downside: UD459 or a draft rate of up to 30% for energy companies (Garrigues, 28 October 2026). An extra five points would cost about 7% of EPS.
  • The deferred-tax remeasurement on the rate change is a one-off and non-cash.
  • One-offs: carry minus PLN 0.3bn to 0.8bn a year before tax below recurring EBITDA: onerous contracts, Price Difference Fund claims (PLN 265m demanded in July 2026), reclamation, restructuring (PLN 194m at Q2 2026). They hit reported EPS by PLN 0.10 to 0.27.
  • Minorities: about PLN 100m a year on the adjusted basis. Minority equity is PLN 1.24bn; the FY2025 minority share was a loss of PLN 103m, after impairments. Small.
  • Share count: flat. PGE has never bought back shares, and the state would not allow it with a 39% free float. Issuance is a 2029-2030 risk, not a base case (see P-02).
  • Dividends: paid PLN 1.57bn (2027), 1.46bn (2028), 1.23bn (2029), 1.01bn (2030). That is 24% to 30% of the prior year's adjusted earnings. They are calculated on net profit excluding non-cash impairments, and they are subject to "leverage and coal market conditions" and to the state's vote at the June AGM.

Reasoning: what changes against the past

  • 2019-2025: write-downs set reported earnings. PLN 8.65bn to 8.97bn of coal impairment in 2025, onerous provisions, and a reported net loss in four of seven years. Adjusted earnings rose with the 2022-2024 regulated and price upcycle.
  • 2026-2030: the depreciation and interest of a capital programme set adjusted earnings.
  • Reported earnings converge towards adjusted, because the coal fleet is already written down. Only Dolna Odra's book value (about PLN 4bn) remains at risk, plus renewables as negative-price hours rise.
  • FCF turns structurally negative: capital spending of PLN 13bn to 15.5bn against operating cash flow of PLN 9.6bn to 10.9bn.
  • Net interest is three to seven times the 2019-2021 level.

For the model

  • Keep CfD revaluations out of the adjusted line.

Peer precedents

C-80

To what extent has the core growth engine decelerated over the last twelve months: is the revenue growth rate running below the company's own normal rate, and is the input change force itself slowing?

C-6012th of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: No. Over the last 12 months the core engine has accelerated cyclically, not decelerated.

Evidence

  • Revenue growth turned positive (+1.7% in Q1 2026, +10.6% in Q2) after -33% (2024) and -5% (2025).
  • Recurring EBITDA +21% in Q2 2026.
  • Distribution recurring EBITDA +12% in Q1.
  • Generation volumes +18% in Q2.
  • Brokers raised FY2027 EPS by 54% over 12 months.

The input forces are mixed

  • Accelerating: the regulated engine (spending, connections).
  • At or near peak: the cyclical engine (gas-shock spreads, capacity payments, a cold winter), with Fitch expecting normalisation.
  • Weakening: the retail engine (business volumes falling).

Risk framing: the danger is not current deceleration but a dated deceleration in 2027-2029. It comes from spread normalisation, the lapse of the 2027 supplementary capacity contract, and the end of coal capacity payments in 2028. Comparisons against strong 2026 quarters will turn negative from about Q2 2027.

F3317th of 17

Judgement: the business gets better while the equity's per-share earnings and cash get worse.

  • Better business: more regulated and contracted, less coal. The regulated and contracted share of EBITDA rises from about 60% to about 70% or more, and above 100% of the total in 2029-2030, when coal is negative.
  • Worse per-share outcome:
  • EBITDA plateaus at PLN 12.65bn to 14.25bn, above the FY2023-FY2025 level (FactSet operating 11.0bn to 11.8bn; recurring 12.9bn in FY2025), so the plateau is high, not low;
  • adjusted EPS peaks in FY2026 at PLN 2.82 and falls about 48% to about PLN 1.5 by FY2029-FY2030;
  • FCF is negative every year, cumulatively about minus PLN 22bn;
  • company net debt rises about eightfold.
  • The brokers have the shape but not the depth:
  • FY2027-FY2028 EPS about 7% to 8% too high;
  • FY2029 EBITDA about 10% too high;
  • FY2028 FCF positive where I see minus PLN 4.9bn;
  • DPS in 2027 too low.

The net picture by line
