Reasoning: how the parts net out

  • The two clocks from the first set hold, but the crossing point is lower than it looks at EBITDA level.
  • At EBITDA, regulated growth nearly offsets the coal run-down, so the brokers' flat EBITDA line looks reasonable to FY2028.
  • At EPS, the regulated growth arrives with its own depreciation and interest, which absorb most of it. The coal decline arrives with almost no depreciation relief, because the fleet is already written down.
  • So the same EBITDA produces less EPS each year. That is the core insight for the Consumer.
  • Positive items that are real but bounded:
  • distribution above the brokers (+PLN 0.4bn to 0.8bn);
  • Baltica 2 on schedule (+PLN 1.05bn to 1.12bn from FY2028);
  • heat above the brokers (+PLN 0.1bn to 0.2bn);
  • FY2026 above the brokers (+PLN 0.4bn).
  • Negative items that are real and larger:
  • coal after 2028;
  • the tax rise;
  • interest;
  • the dividend's cost in debt;
  • the recurring one-offs.
  • Policy is the swing factor. The upside (coal support, higher allowed return) and the downside (levy, tariff caps, a higher energy tax) are each worth PLN 0.3 to 0.6 of EPS. Both are binary and dated.

Forecasts and upgrades

8

Probably upgrades this financial year to

–not yet rated

Memo topic 7, : the Rater's score

Behind the rating as a whole

Group judgement

The fourth set as a whole

F348th of 8

Layer 4 · The fourth set as a whole

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

Guidance, the brokers' forecasts and the narrative already hold the good news about PGE and do not hold the bad. The gap between our assessment and what is expected is unfavourable for an investment case on both horizons: slightly over the next six months, and strongly from month six to three years.

The three answers together

  • Guidance (I-01). There is none for the next two years, so nothing can be beaten or raised. The standing 2030 target is 15% above our figure. The dividend is the one commitment that can surprise positively, and it is already known.
  • The brokers (I-02). They are in line with us on EBITDA to FY2028. Our EPS is below theirs by 5% in FY2026, 10% in FY2027, 12% in FY2028 and 43% in FY2029. They are below us only on the dividend.
  • The narrative (I-03). It knows every event and has not added up their effect on EPS. Its best-known "hidden" upside is coal support.

The gap stated as a valuation

  • On the brokers' figures PGE trades at 6.4 to 6.6 times earnings 24 months forward (EPS of about PLN 2.40). That looks cheap: the 56th percentile of its own ten years, the lowest P/E in its sector.
  • On ours, the earnings that will sit in the 24-month forward window at the end of 2027 are those of FY2029: about PLN 1.4. Today's price is about 11 times that figure. The top of PGE's five-year range is 11.2 times.
  • The mechanism is the calendar, not an event. No announcement is needed. As 2027 passes, the forward window moves from years with capacity payments to the first year without them. Brokers add FY2029 to their models in spring 2027.
  • The Financial Analyst's model (update 1, before my change to the margin) gives a total return of minus 14.0% to 31 December 2027 at the guideline multiple of 8.64 times: minus 27% from earnings, plus 12.5% from the multiple and plus 4% from dividends. On my arithmetic the changed margin takes it to about minus 20%; the updated model will give the figure of record.
  • The return is negative across the plausible range. With coal support of about PLN 1.5bn a year (FY2029 EPS of about PLN 1.74 on the model basis), the same arithmetic gives about minus 4%. It needs support of roughly PLN 1.7bn a year or more, or a multiple above the guideline, to turn positive.
  • A remark on the guideline multiple, for the memo Analyst's own view. 8.64 times is above the level the dashboard labels "defendable" (8.1 times, the 75th percentile of ten years) and well above the five-year median of 5.3 times. A high multiple on depressed earnings is normal, so this may be right. But it means the model's negative return already assumes a re-rating.

How the gap unfolds in time

  • The next six months: small and two-sided. The third-quarter results, the dividend proposal and a slightly better FY2026 are mildly positive against the brokers. The tax signature, the tariff decisions and the shield funding are two-sided. Revision momentum fades mechanically from late January 2027.
  • Months 6 to 18: the gap opens. Three quarters of year-on-year decline are reported in May, September and November 2027. FY2029 enters the brokers' models. The election is in the autumn. The forward window reaches the cliff year.
  • 18 months to three years: the gap is widest. FY2029 EPS of PLN 1.37 against PLN 2.39; 2030 EBITDA of PLN 12.8bn against a target of PLN 15bn.

What would make the gap favourable

  1. Coal support of about PLN 2bn a year or more, legislated in Warsaw and cleared in Brussels, before the end of 2027. The first set put any framework within 18 months at 10% to 20%.
  2. The reinvestment premium held at its 2026 level through 2028 (plus PLN 0.25bn to 0.5bn of EBITDA).
  3. The 2027 forward price holding near PLN 590/MWh with no levy.
  4. A veto of the tax rise.

All four together would lift FY2027 EPS to about PLN 2.7 and FY2029 EPS to about PLN 2.1. That reaches the brokers' present figure for FY2027 and still falls short of their figure for FY2029. Our assessment has no path on which it ends up materially above what is already expected.

The steer for the memo

  • Topics on upgrades and momentum (#4, #7, #8). Current momentum is strong and dated. The upgrades have been made, the ratings have been cut, and the three-month revision figure leaves the window on about 21 January 2027.
  • Topic on the lifecycle (#10). The evidence fits a stock late in its upgrade phase: estimates and price have moved together and fast, the brokers have stopped endorsing the price, and the next revisions are more likely down than up.
  • Topic on the return (#12). Explain it as earnings falling by about a quarter in the forward window, partly offset by the multiple and the dividend. Show the coal-support sensitivity beside it, because that is the only variable large enough to change its sign.
  • Keep the honest positives. The EBITDA plateau is real and well supported to 2028. The dividend is likely and more durable than feared. The balance sheet has room. These make PGE a sound credit and a better business. They do not make the equity cheap on earnings that are about to roll into the forward window.

I-01

How likely is company guidance to be raised, met or reduced at the next few earnings events (all such events over the next 18 months), and what would drive each outcome? Secondarily, how likely is any guidance, mid-term target or ambition for 18 months to three years out to be raised, met or reduced?

F426th of 8

Layer 4 · Guidance

Judgement: PGE gives no annual guidance, so at its quarterly events there is nothing to raise, meet or cut. What stands in for guidance is a handful of standing commitments. Over the next 18 months the dividend is likely to be delivered and is the only commitment that can surprise positively; the 2030 EBITDA target is likely to be repeated until the next strategy rewrite, which is more likely than not to fall inside the window; and the capital plan is likely to be quietly under-delivered. Beyond 18 months, the PLN 15bn target for 2030 is more likely to be cut than met as published, unless coal support arrives, in which case it will be met in total and missed in composition.

The events. Third quarter 2026 (24 November 2026); FY2026 (March to April 2027); first quarter 2027 (May); first half 2027 (September); third quarter 2027 (November); FY2027 (March to April 2028); first quarter 2028 (May). Around them sit the dividend vote (about June 2027) and the parliamentary election (autumn 2027).

Three judgements on how the events will read

1. The nearest thing to guidance arrives on 24 November 2026.

  • PGE gave its qualitative view of 2026 by segment with its third-quarter 2025 results (26 November 2025): stable distribution, lower trading and renewables. Expect the equivalent for 2027 with the third-quarter 2026 results.
  • The likely content: distribution stable to slightly higher; conventional generation lower as 2026's conditions fade; heat lower on the gas-cost lag; supply dependent on the tariff; renewables higher with Baltica 2.
  • That is a segment-by-segment statement that 2027 is lower than 2026 outside the regulated and contracted lines. It would be consistent with our forecast and slightly below the brokers' coal figure (PLN 2.14bn for FY2027 against our PLN 1.45bn).

2. The quarterly prints in 2027 run against the positive calendar.

  • On my quarterly inputs, recurring EBITDA falls year on year for three quarters in a row: the first quarter of 2027 (about PLN 3.95bn against PLN 4.14bn), the second (about PLN 3.46bn against PLN 3.96bn) and the third (about PLN 2.78bn against about PLN 2.85bn). They are reported in May, September and November 2027. Growth returns only in the fourth quarter, reported in March 2028.
  • The positive steps in the first set's "staircase" (the dividend proposal, vote and payment; Baltica 2 first power) are not results events. The results events themselves are mildly negative through 2027. The first set's reading that the narrative "supports the shares for 12 to 18 months" should be qualified accordingly.

3. PGE will track, not lead. It publishes preliminary figures, cuts targets when reality changes and does not raise them. The read-across from peers is the same: Endesa repeated rather than raised its guidance after a strong nine months (October 2026). No part of the return can come from "beat and raise".

Secondarily: 18 months to three years

  • The 2030 target is hedged by its own composition. Management's coal figure (minus PLN 3.3bn) is more cautious than ours (minus PLN 2.0bn). Its figure for everything else (about PLN 18.3bn) is far above ours (about PLN 14.8bn). Erste notes that the target leans on PLN 4bn from an internal efficiency transformation that unions and politics could block.
  • If coal support of PLN 1.5bn to 2bn a year is granted, management can report PLN 15bn in total while the rest of the group falls about PLN 2bn short. If it is not, the shortfall shows and the target is cut.
  • My probabilities to the end of 2029: cut, about 55%; met in total with the help of coal support, about 35%; raised, under 10%.
  • The 2035 ambition (above PLN 30bn) is about twice Bernstein's PLN 16bn. It will be cut at some point. It has no bearing on the next three years.

The gap between our assessment and guidance. Where PGE has stated a figure, it is above ours (2030 EBITDA by about 15%), except for coal. The dividend is the one commitment where our figure is above the brokers', and it has been public since 19 October 2026. The gap is mildly unfavourable.

Dividend paid in 2027 PLN 0.55 0.70 +27% Raised: about 65% Becomes the actual
FY2028 capital spending; free cash flow PLN 10.7bn; plus 1.6bn 15.5bn; about minus 5.0bn Large Little change Cut: about 75%
FY2029 EBITDA; EPS (one broker) PLN 14.09bn; 2.39 12.25bn; 1.37 -13%; -43% Little change until brokers add the year Cut: about 70%

Why EBITDA holds while EPS falls

  • EBITDA: the differences offset. Our distribution is above the brokers' (by PLN 0.2bn to 0.5bn). Our coal is below (PLN 1.45bn against 2.14bn in FY2027; PLN 0.70bn against 1.08bn in FY2028). Supply and offshore are a little below. Our depreciation is close to theirs.
  • EPS: three things below EBITDA. The tax rise is "largely absent" from the brokers' models (AlphaSense) and their estimates are dated 16 to 21 October, before the Senate vote. Interest on a debt-funded programme and dividend is higher in ours. And see the next point.

Four qualifications, which make this softer than the second set said

1. The brokers' median EPS does not follow from the brokers' own lines.

  • For FY2027, the brokers' EBITDA (PLN 13.34bn) less their depreciation (PLN 5.10bn) is PLN 8.25bn, but their EBIT median is PLN 8.91bn. The medians come from different brokers.
  • Taking their own EBITDA, depreciation and financial result, EPS is about PLN 2.4 at a 23% tax rate and about PLN 2.3 to 2.35 at 25.5%. Line by line, the brokers' figures already produce our EPS.
  • The published PLN 2.60 is the middle of three brokers who reach similar EPS from EBITDA that differs by PLN 2bn (Trigon PLN 14.26bn; Erste PLN 12.20bn; PKO PLN 12.43bn for PLN 2.08).
  • So the gap will close when brokers refresh their models, not on any particular piece of news. The median moves only when Trigon or Erste moves. I can give the direction with some confidence and the date with little.

2. The tax rise may be vetoed (about one chance in three). Then the most mechanical cut does not happen.

3. There is no agreed shape after 2027. Bernstein, which is not in the FactSet median, has EPS of PLN 2.19 and PLN 1.89 for FY2026 and FY2027 on a lower EBITDA basis (PLN 12.1bn and 11.9bn), repeated on 9 November 2026. It then has EBITDA of PLN 14.83bn and EPS of PLN 2.59 in FY2028, above the median. The most detailed model on the stock is below the median for the next two years and above it for the third.

4. The upside case is live. The 2027 base-load forward was PLN 590/MWh in mid-September 2026. Morgan Stanley raised its EPS for CEZ by 7% on power prices on 23 October 2026. If the forward holds, no levy comes and the tariff covers cost, FY2027 EBITDA is raised. I give that about one chance in five.

The net breadth of revisions

  • Now: FY2027 EPS up 13% over one month, 64% over three and 49% over six. EBITDA up 5%, 13% and 18%. The dashboard's sell-side momentum rating is A.
  • The mechanics. The upgrades were made between 16 and 21 October 2026, around the strategy update. They leave the three-month window on about 21 January 2027 and the six-month window on about 21 April 2027. With no new upgrades, the three-month figure falls to about zero in late January. With the tax rise and any trimming of coal, it turns negative.
  • So the A rating should be expected to fade by February 2027 whatever PGE reports. The memo's reading of "current momentum" should be dated accordingly.
  • Ratings and targets have already turned. The share of buy ratings is 29%, down 21 points in a month. The mean target (PLN 15.56) is below the price. The October estimate upgrades came with Hold ratings (Trigon, target PLN 18.80; Erste, PLN 17.00). PKO's Buy carries a target (PLN 15.50) below the price. Target increases have run their course unless estimates rise again.

Secondarily: FY2029 and FY2030

  • What the brokers have. One broker for FY2029 (EBITDA PLN 14.09bn; EPS PLN 2.39), with no cliff in it. For 2030, a Bloomberg consensus of PLN 15.2bn and Bernstein at PLN 14.6bn, with coal at about minus PLN 1.1bn to 1.8bn.
  • Ours. PLN 12.25bn and 12.77bn; EPS of PLN 1.37 and 1.31.
  • The conclusion is robust to the policy outcome. Even with coal support of about PLN 1.5bn a year, our FY2029 EPS is about PLN 1.7, well below PLN 2.39. The brokers' figure is too high in every case except support of about PLN 3.5bn a year, which is more than the capacity payments being lost.
  • A date for it. After the FY2026 results (March to April 2027), brokers roll their models forward a year. FY2029 then gets three or four contributors instead of one. Spring 2027 is when the first broad view of the cliff year will form.
  • Read-across. Tauron shows what the year after a capacity cliff looks like: its generation EBITDA fell to PLN 272m in the first half of 2026 from PLN 577m.
  • Probability that the brokers' FY2029 to FY2030 figures are lower in 18 months than today: about 70%.

The gap between our assessment and the brokers. In line on EBITDA to FY2028; below on EPS in every year; far below for FY2029; above only on the dividend and, slightly, on FY2026 EBITDA. The gap is unfavourable, and it widens with the horizon.

11d

Current multiple

–not yet ratedMemo topic 11, : the Rater's score1 answer
D-0745
D-07

To what extent do the share price and the multiple move with sector and industry peers rather than idiosyncratically (on a rolling six-month basis), and so how far is the multiple company-driven rather than industry-driven?

C-454th of 8

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: PGE's share price does not move with the European utility sector. It moves day to day with the two other listed Polish state utilities, and over the last six months its own news has outweighed even that. The level of the multiple is company-driven and country-driven, not industry-driven.

The evidence (co-movement table, 13 November 2026)

  • Latest six-month window (20 May to 13 November 2026): correlation with an equal-weighted basket of 36 European integrated utilities is 0.38. The basket explains 15% of PGE's daily price variance; 85% is PGE's own. Beta is 1.73. PGE returned plus 56.1%; the basket plus 7.6%.
  • By peer: Tauron 0.80 (64% explained) and Enea 0.70 (50%). No other peer is above 0.28. CEZ is 0.09 and Public Power Corporation 0.12. Energa is minus 0.09, pinned by Orlen's buyout price.
  • Over 24 months: the basket's share has ranged from 10% to 39%. It fell from 39% in August 2026 to 15% now, as PGE rallied.

Three readings

1. There are two layers, and they point to different drivers.

  • Daily moves are Polish. Policy headlines, the zloty, Polish bond yields and Warsaw fund flows hit PGE, Tauron and Enea together.
  • The six-month drift is PGE's own. Over 12 months PGE is up 40% while Tauron is down 9% and Enea down 1%, despite daily correlations of 0.7 to 0.8. What separates PGE is specific and dated: capacity contracts that run to 2028 where theirs ended in 2025, the largest supplementary award, the dividend, and the estimate upgrades that followed.

2. The rally was led by estimates, not by the multiple.

  • Over three months the price rose 41% and the brokers' FY2027 EPS rose 64%. The 24-month forward P/E is 6.4 times against a two-year median of 5.6 times: about 15% of multiple expansion. The rest is earnings.
  • This corrects an impression in the first set. Tauron and Enea sit at the 96th and 97th percentiles of their own P/E histories because their earnings fell. PGE sits at the 56th percentile of its ten-year history (77th of five years) because its estimates rose. "The cohort at the 95th percentile" does not describe PGE.
  • So the valuation risk is in the earnings, not in the multiple. A share price that followed three brokers' estimates up will follow them down.

3. The multiple will be set by PGE's own estimate path and by Polish policy, and neither helped nor protected by the sector. A 1% move in the sector moves PGE by 1.7% on average, but explains little of what PGE does.

What this means for predictability

  • Helpful: there is little to analyse outside Poland. The outcome depends on the PGE-specific items already forecast.
  • Unhelpful: there is no sector anchor. The price carries its own thin estimates. And the Polish factor in the share price has the same source as the earnings risk (policy), so a bad policy outcome hits earnings and the multiple at once. The fundamental risk and the market risk do not diversify each other.

For the memo's view of the fair multiple. PGE's own history is the relevant yardstick, not the sector's. Its five-year band is 3.3 to 11.2 times (median 5.3); its ten-year median is 6.2.

Outside the eleven ratings

Answers not placed under any of the eleven ratings

The table the ratings map uses places these nowhere; each is shown with the reason the table records.

Why it is not placed It asks whether the stock fits the sector's historical patterns of share price and financial outperformance, a cross-check across many topics rather than one of the eleven.

C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

F4023rd of 25

Layer 1 · Industry and Value Chain

Judgement: Polish state utilities have outperformed in three kinds of episode and underperformed in three. PGE today fits an outperformance episode that history says tends to be cyclical and reversible. It does not yet fit the pattern that produced lasting re-ratings elsewhere.

How the group has outperformed

  1. Power-price and spread upcycles. PGE made record EBITDA of PLN 9.5bn in 2021 and cut net debt to PLN 4.2bn. The 2026 gas shock produced Q2 recurring EBITDA +21%.
  2. Policy hope. Shares rallied on the coal carve-out plans of 2021-2024 and fell when the plan was cancelled ("Polish energy stocks tank over fears government will ditch coal takeover strategy", December 2024).
  3. Capital returns. The October 2026 dividend surprise.

How it has underperformed

  1. State-directed capital allocation. - -21% in six months in 2013 (the Opole coal expansion). - -19% in late 2015 (coal mine rescue). - The 2016 dividend cut by the state's vote, from a proposed PLN 0.92 to 0.25. - The 2016 capital restructuring that created a PLN 110m tax charge. - The 2022 equity raise (shares +20%).
  2. CO2 and allocation shocks. -13% in January 2012 on less free allocation. The 2017-2018 CO2 doubling led to a suspended dividend.
  3. Price caps and levies (2022-2025). Revenue caps crimped merchant margins at record prices.

Rules of thumb that apply

  • Polish utilities' forward P/E mean-reverts in a band. The five-year range is 3.3 to 11.2 times, median 5.3 times, 10th to 90th percentile 4.1 to 7.7 times. PGE is at 6.4 times forward (77th percentile of five years; 84th of two). The dashboard's ten-year bands: 6.2 times "prudent" median, 8.1 times "defendable", 3.8 times "crash".
  • "Governance discount reassertion": operational beats do not re-rate a stock while governance is unresolved.
  • "A catalyst needs a price and a clock": the coal separation has no date. The Sector Digest says the cohort, at the 95th percentile, needs agreed coal terms, which have no date.
  • "Extended trend = banked upside" and "a crisp setup fit is often a late one": +74% in the three months to mid-October and +40% over 12 months.

European precedents of lasting re-rating, and why PGE does not fit them yet

Precedent What happened PGE's position
RWE and E.ON (2016) Split generation from networks Separation is not available to PGE
Public Power Corporation (Greece) Re-rated from a distressed state utility to 14.6 times P/E. It needed the state's stake cut to 33.4% with CVC holding 17%, a coal exit by 2026, a turnaround CEO in place since 2019 delivering every target, and a regional growth platform PGE has the grid and the balance sheet. It lacks the governance change, the coal exit and the management continuity
CEZ The Czech state buying 80% of the nuclear builder "de-risks CEZ's balance sheet" Shows a state can take a liability off a listed utility; Poland chose the opposite with the coal agency

For the model and memo: expect the shares to trade with spreads, policy news and dividends, within a P/E band of about 4 to 8 times. A move to 10 times or more needs coal separation or a governance change. PGE's present position near the upper-middle of its band, after an earnings and policy-hope rally, historically precedes mean reversion more often than further re-rating.

Why it is not placed The table holds this question in reserve for Riskiness sub-items that are not yet built, so it is not yet placed.

C-83

How far could the shares fall to a fair trough multiple?

F3020th of 25

Layer 1 · Risks

Judgement: To a fair trough multiple on trough-cycle earnings, the shares could fall about 30% to 45%, to about PLN 8.5 to 11.0. A severe policy case reaches about PLN 6.5 to 7.5 (-50% to -60%).

Basis

  • Valuation history (FactSet 24-month forward P/E):
  • five-year range 3.26 to 11.19 times;
  • median 5.31 times;
  • 25th percentile 4.59 times;
  • 10th percentile 4.06 times;
  • today 6.38 times.
  • Dashboard ten-year bands: "conservative" 5.30 times; "crash" 3.80 times.
  • Trough earnings: FY2028-2029 EPS of about PLN 2.0 to 2.3, once spreads normalise and the 2027 capacity revenue lapses. The coal cliff without support gives about PLN 1.6 to 1.9.

Scenarios

Case Multiple EPS Price Change
Fair trough 4.6 to 5.3 times PLN 2.0 to 2.3 about PLN 9.2 to 12.2 -20% to -42%
Central fair trough n/a n/a about PLN 10 -35%
Severe 4.0 times PLN 1.7 about PLN 6.8 -57%

Cross-checks

  • Wood & Company target PLN 9.30.
  • Noble's discounted cash flow PLN 7.98; sum of the parts PLN 8.28; peer multiples PLN 9.44 (all December 2025).
  • The 52-week low was PLN 8.21; the price was PLN 9.47 on 29 June 2026.
  • Bernstein's target PLN 14.20, and the mean target PLN 15.56, sit just below today's price.

Support: a 5% dividend yield at PLN 0.55 to 0.80 a share implies support at about PLN 11 to 16. The grid's standalone value is about PLN 34bn, roughly PLN 15 a share before coal liabilities and net debt. These argue against the severe case while the dividend policy holds.

Why it is not placed It rates the staircase of news the market can look forward to, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or the catalysts as such.

Group judgement

Stock market narrative

D5414th of 25

Layer 1 · Stock market narrative

Written last by the Investment Analyst for its group

The market can look forward to a clean, dated sequence through 2027: good third-quarter figures, the first dividend proposal in a decade, Baltica 2 first power and full operation, new batteries and gas units, and distribution growth.

  • Most steps are well founded.
  • Most are expected. The shares have risen 74% in three months on results and capacity awards, not on coal progress.
  • The truly re-rating step, a solution for coal after 2028, is undated and poorly founded.
  • The policy steps (household tariff, levies, the 2027 election) can intrude on the staircase at any point.

For the model and the later sets: the narrative supports the shares for 12 to 18 months if the dividend is confirmed at or above PLN 0.55 and Baltica 2 delivers. It becomes difficult from 2028, when the coal cliff and a possible new government enter the window.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the earlier answers agree with each other, which is a check on the analysis rather than on the company.

D-01

How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

–58

Layer 3 · Risks

Judgement: the two earlier sets agree on the structure of the case and on its direction, and that agreement is well founded. They jar, or are jointly wrong, at five quantitative joints. Three of the five corrections make the case less positive (distribution, the size of the coal cliff, the downside arithmetic). Two make it slightly better (the dividend is more secure than argued; the tax rise is not yet certain). Two of the five fall on lines the earlier sets called the most predictable. Congruence is good on structure and only fair on numbers.

Where the sets support each other

  1. Two clocks. The first set described a regulated and contracted engine rising and a coal engine running to a dated cliff. The second set put numbers on both: EBITDA excluding coal rising from PLN 11.9bn to 15.3bn, and coal falling from plus PLN 1.85bn to minus PLN 2.0bn. The independent research confirms every date the structure depends on: coal capacity payments end in December 2028 under the European derogation (Polish law of 14 February 2025); the supplementary award covers 2027 only; Baltica 2 was 45% complete at the third quarter of 2026.
  2. Policy as the scenario axis. Both sets reach it independently, the first from the value chain, the second from the pricing arithmetic.
  3. Earnings quality: the strongest join in the work. The first set (C-74) noticed that the brokers raised FY2027 EPS by 64% on an EBITDA rise of 13%, so most of the upgrade came from below EBITDA. The second set (P-08) found that 72% of the fall in EPS to FY2029 comes from below EBITDA. These are one observation seen from two ends. The lines that inflated the upgrade are low depreciation after the 2025 write-down and low interest while cash was high and Baltica 2 interest was capitalised. The same lines deflate EPS as new assets enter service and debt rises. This is why I trust the direction of EPS more than its level.
  4. Management delivers contracted projects and does not deliver structural change. The forecasts lean on management only for contracted items, which is consistent with the first set's verdict.

Where they jar, and my ruling on each

1. Distribution, the "main offset". Both sets are too high; I change the input.

  • The two positions. The first set called the brokers' flat distribution line "the largest identifiable gap". The second set explained the flat line (a falling allowed return) but still modelled PLN 5.70bn for FY2026, rising 4% a year, on the argument that the 2026 bond sell-off feeds the return formula for the 2027 and 2028 tariffs.
  • That argument does not hold. The base allowed return is fixed at 7.478% for 2023 to 2028 for operators that signed the regulator's transformation charter (Council of European Energy Regulators, February 2025 and January 2026). Bond yields therefore do not reach the tariff before 2029. PGE itself says the method after 2028 is unknown (strategy presentation, 20 October 2026). The averaging window for the risk-free rate is 36 months, not 18.
  • What moves is the reinvestment premium, which the regulator sets each year, and it is falling. Enea's premium fell from 4.28% to 1.87% for 2026, taking its total return from 11.76% to 9.35%. Bernstein reports PGE's return about 2.5 points lower in 2026 than in 2025 (12.96%). Forward views: Noble 7.5% by 2028; Fitch 8% on average in 2027 to 2030 for Tauron; Bernstein 8.0% by 2030, with PGE distribution EBITDA of PLN 6.22bn in that year.
  • The asset base in the second set is overstated. The PLN 30.5bn it used for 2025 is distribution plus railway (PLN 25.64bn plus PLN 4.78bn, Bernstein, May 2026). Distribution alone is PLN 25.6bn at end-2025 and PLN 28.0bn at end-2026, not about PLN 33bn.
  • The 2026 actuals do not support PLN 5.70bn. First-half 2026 distribution EBITDA was PLN 2,769m, up about 5%. The second quarter was PLN 1.29bn against PLN 1.31bn, a fall. PLN 5.70bn needs the second half to grow 9%. In November 2025 management said it expected stable distribution recurring EBITDA in 2026 (Polish Press Agency, 26 November 2025).
  • My line: PLN 5.50bn, 5.60bn, 5.75bn, 5.90bn and 6.20bn. It assumes the total return falls by a little under one point a year to about 8.5% by 2029, on an asset base growing 9% a year. Each point of return is worth about PLN 0.25bn of EBITDA (Erste, November 2025).
  • I stay above the brokers by PLN 0.2bn to 0.5bn, for three reasons. The asset base grows 9% a year. The regulatory formula pays a return on 35% of planned capital spending before assets are commissioned. And, as conjecture, smart meters are depreciated for regulatory purposes over one year (E.ON's regulatory fact sheet, November 2026): PGE has the most meters still to install of any Polish operator, so its meter programme returns cash through the tariff unusually fast.
  • This also settles the first set's source check 8. The asset base grows about 9% a year to 2030 (Bernstein) and about 7.7% a year over the decade (PGE: PLN 28.8bn in 2025 to PLN 60.3bn in 2035). Both figures are right for their horizons.

2. The size of the coal cliff. The second set's low end should be dropped; its central case stands.

  • The second set gave a range of PLN 1.0bn to 2.5bn for the capacity revenue lost after 2028. The low end rested on the figures that belong to Tauron.
  • PGE's evidence: PLN 2.25bn of conventional capacity revenue in FY2024 (Bernstein), and PLN 2.66bn for the Coal Energy segment in FY2025 (the 2025 annual report, as cited by AlphaSense). Unit closures (908 MW at Dolna Odra, 460 MW at Rybnik) reduce that, and the supplementary award adds about PLN 0.6bn in 2027 only.
  • So the capacity revenue that stops at the end of 2028 is about PLN 2.0bn to 2.5bn. The second set's central step-down of PLN 2.1bn, after PLN 0.5bn of assumed replacement support, is consistent with this. I do not change it.
  • But the Consumer should know what it leans on. Management's minus PLN 3.3bn for 2030 is already after its Efficiency Improvement Programme, whose perimeter is now confirmed as the coal subsidiary PGE GiEK (from about minus PLN 5.1bn before the programme). The second set's minus PLN 2.0bn is PLN 1.3bn better than management's own figure. Assumed support explains PLN 0.5bn of that. The other PLN 0.8bn rests on Bernstein's minus PLN 1.8bn, not on anything PGE has shown it can do (D-02).

3. The leverage ceiling and the dividend. The second set tested the wrong definition; the dividend inputs survive for a different reason.

  • The second set tested economic net debt, which includes CO2 obligations, against the 3.5-times ceiling. It concluded that the ceiling binds in 2029 to 2030 and halves the dividend.
  • The ceiling in PGE's bank agreements is defined on a net debt that excludes non-recourse project finance (Baltica 2), reverse factoring and CO2 obligations (strategy presentation, 20 October 2026; 2025 separate financial statements). On that definition PGE itself projects an average of 1.6 times in 2026 to 2029 and a peak of 2.8 times in 2030. The Rybnik loan sets 4.0 times, tightening to 3.5 times only if the investment-grade rating is lost.
  • On the bank definition the forecasts give roughly 1.6 to 2.5 times in 2029 to 2030. The range depends on how much of the PLN 11.1bn of Baltica 2 project finance is PGE's own share; the sources read it both ways. The covenant does not bind inside the forecast period. The constraints that can bind are Fitch's downgrade trigger of 3.7 times, on a broader definition, and the state's vote.
  • The dividend inputs (PLN 0.70, 0.65, 0.55, 0.45) still hold, because each is 24% to 30% of the prior year's adjusted earnings. Falling EPS drives the decline, not the ceiling.
  • Consequence: the dividend is more secure through 2029 than the second set argued, and its decline is a plain read-through from EPS. The "collision in 2029" between the state's need for cash and the ceiling is a decision about the credit rating, not a covenant event.

4. The tax rise. It is not yet law.

  • The second set called it "a tax law already passed" and one of "the most predictable parts of the forecast". The Senate passed it on 6 November 2026. The President has 21 days to sign, so until about 27 November 2026.
  • The evidence on a veto points both ways. He signed the 30% bank rate for 2026 and the fuel levy in October 2026. He also has a record number of vetoes on fiscal measures (Swedbank, 9 November 2026) and delayed the energy-profit bill in July 2026.
  • I keep 25.5% in the base and put the chance of a veto at about one in three. A veto adds about PLN 0.08 to FY2027 EPS. The line is predictable in size, not yet in fact.
  • A separate draft (UD459) would raise the rate to as much as 30% in 2027 for large fuel and energy companies. On the published draft, distribution by vertically integrated groups is excluded and coal-fired generation is not listed (Garrigues, 28 October 2026). Whether PGE's own lignite mining counts as "fossil fuel extraction" is not settled in the sources.

5. The downside arithmetic. The first set's "severe" earnings are now the base case.

  • The first set (C-83) put trough EPS at PLN 2.0 to 2.3 and "severe" EPS at PLN 1.7, and so a fall to a fair trough of 30% to 45%.
  • The second set's base for FY2029 is PLN 1.50, and mine is PLN 1.37. Both are below the first set's severe case.
  • A trough multiple on trough earnings double-counts, so the fair pairing is a median multiple on the cliff-year earnings: 5.3 to 6.2 times PLN 1.37 is PLN 7.3 to 8.5, a fall of 46% to 54% from PLN 15.755. C-83 should be read with these earnings, not the first set's.

Smaller points for the memo

  • EBITDA peaks in FY2028 and EPS in FY2026. The first set's "FY2026 is the cyclical high" is true for EPS and for merchant earnings only.
  • The 0.5% loans are mostly not yet drawn. PGE had drawn PLN 1.95bn at June 2026, with PLN 12.8bn available. The interest saving the first set described (PLN 0.7bn to 0.8bn a year) builds with drawdowns, which depend on recovery-plan milestones.
  • The "Dolna Odra" and "Gryfino" gas plants in the first set appear to be one plant (the 1.4 GW combined-cycle plant at Dolna Odra, near Gryfino), counted twice as evidence of delivery.
  • The second-quarter coal figure of PLN 763m that the second set used as recurring is described by AlphaSense as the reported segment figure, "heavily aided" by a PLN 970m year-on-year swing in onerous-contract provisions. The third-quarter release should be used to check the coal run-rate.

What the pattern of errors tells the Consumer

Every correction sits where an earlier Analyst turned a regulatory or contractual term into a number on one source: which perimeter, which debt definition, which averaging window, "passed" or "signed". In a company whose earnings are set by formulae, contracts and statutes, the definitions are the forecast. The memo should state the definition beside every such figure.

Why it is not placed It rates how well the sell-side and investors already articulate the case, and none of the eleven rates the narrative or how much of the case is known.

I-03

How comprehensively are these dynamics articulated in the current narrative of (i) sell-side commentary and (ii) investor commentary (for example on Twitter): how well known is each of them to investors who own or cover the company or its sector, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to three years out?

F338th of 8

Layer 4 · Stock market narrative

Judgement: the events of the case are well known; their effect on EPS is not. Commentary on PGE is conducted in EBITDA and business mix: the regulated share rising to 63%, the dividend back, capacity contracts to 2028, Baltica 2 on schedule. The dynamics that take EPS down are each known somewhere and are nowhere added up. The one large positive surprise still possible, support for coal after 2028, is the best-known "hidden" upside in the story. So what is under-appreciated is mostly negative, and what is positive is mostly known.

(i) Sell-side commentary, dynamic by dynamic

Dynamic How well known In the brokers' numbers? Our view against the narrative
Coal capacity payments end in 2028 Widely discussed; Bernstein since May 2025 The date, yes. The size, no: one broker forecasts FY2029 and shows no cliff Known as an event, absent as a number. Negative gap
A coal solution after 2028 Widely discussed as the swing factor; the grid operator's talks with Brussels reported in April 2026; Bernstein "would not be surprised" by support As hope, in ratings and targets Known. Relief would be modest if granted, because it is expected and we already assume PLN 0.5bn a year
The distribution return falling Well known: Noble, Fitch, Erste, Bernstein Yes: a flat distribution line We are above the brokers by PLN 0.2bn to 0.5bn. Small positive gap
Tax rise to 22%; an energy surcharge Macro notes only; "largely absent" from models of PGE No Negative gap of about 3% of EPS if signed; none if vetoed
A dividend while free cash flow is negative; the state's vote Widely discussed; a "positive surprise" (Bernstein and Erste, 19 October 2026) Partly: PLN 0.55 against a ceiling near PLN 0.80 Small positive gap on size. More durable than credit commentary implies
Net debt towards 3.5 times; an equity raise Credit analysts; an equity raise is a "shadow" topic No Neutral: headroom on the bank definition is ample to 2030
Depreciation and interest: EPS against EBITDA Visible in models, "less explicitly flagged"; Bernstein has EPS rising to PLN 2.59 in FY2028 Partly, and inconsistently The largest negative gap for the next 18 months. Nobody presents EPS halving by FY2029
The 2027 household tariff; election-year caps Macro houses (Morgan Stanley, Deutsche Bank, late October to early November 2026); no stress test of PGE's supply segment No Two-sided. Our supply is a little below the brokers'
Recurring "one-offs"; Baltica 2 derivative revaluations Poorly articulated; stripped out of adjusted figures Not applicable Minor; affects reported EPS and the perceived dividend base
Capital spending above the brokers' in FY2028; negative free cash flow to 2030 Bernstein notes negative free cash flow; the brokers have plus PLN 1.6bn for FY2028 No Negative gap
The 24-month forward window reaching FY2029 during 2027 Not discussed No The main unpriced mechanism (see the fourth set as a whole)

Three readings of the sell-side narrative

1. The narrative has the quality of EBITDA right and the quantity of EPS wrong.

  • The dominant frame is Bernstein's: "evolution, not revolution, and the dividend is back" (19 October 2026); a group moving to regulated and contracted earnings that deserves a higher valuation over time. That is a statement about EBITDA mix, and we agree with it.
  • The valuation most quoted, a P/E of about 6.5 times and the lowest in the European sector, is a statement about EPS. It rests on EPS near PLN 2.4 to 2.6 lasting. On our forecasts it does not.
  • D-06 gives the reason in one example: Baltica 2 adds PLN 1.1bn to EBITDA and about nothing to EPS, while the coal run-off reaches EPS in full.

2. The best-known item is the upside option. Every Bernstein note names coal support. The State Assets minister said on 5 November 2026 that coal plants "could stabilise Poland's power system for years to come". A decision to support coal would be read as confirmation, not surprise. Silence, as 2028 approaches, is what would disappoint. The asymmetry is unfavourable.

3. The disagreement among brokers is itself part of the narrative. Targets run from PLN 9.30 (Wood, Sell) to PLN 20.00 (BOS, Buy). Estimates rose 64% in three months while ratings were cut. The brokers have endorsed the numbers and declined to endorse the price.

(ii) Investor commentary

  • The research found almost no direct evidence of what investors are saying. Of 143 sources in the narrative report, 16 were news or investor items and none is quoted. Nothing from X (Twitter) or Polish investor forums was retrieved. I cannot assess this part from the files, and I have asked for it in the research requests.
  • What price and ratings imply (conjecture). The shares rose 56% in six months while the share of buy ratings fell to 29% and the mean target fell below the price. Buyers were not following recommendations. That fits buying led by momentum and fund flows: relative strength at the 97th percentile; PGE left as the liquid listed Polish utility after Orlen's buyout of Energa. It also fits a simple story: an energy-shock windfall, capacity contracts to 2028, a dividend after a decade without one, and a P/E of 6.
  • If that is the investor narrative, it is a narrative about FY2026 and FY2027. It is exposed to the same shift of horizon as the brokers' figures.

Primarily, the next 18 months. Priced: a strong FY2026, the dividend, Baltica 2 on schedule, capacity payments to 2028. Not priced: the depth of the EPS decline, the tax rise, three quarters of year-on-year decline in 2027, the loss of revision momentum from late January 2027.

Secondarily, 18 months to three years. The narrative holds 2030 EBITDA of PLN 15bn, "in line with consensus", and a gradual re-rating as an infrastructure group. Ours is PLN 12.8bn of EBITDA and EPS of about PLN 1.3, with free cash flow negative throughout.

Where we are more positive than the narrative. Distribution against the brokers; the size and durability of the dividend; the chance of a tax veto; the 2027 forward price. Each is worth 3% to 4% of EPS at most. None changes the picture.

Layer 1: Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

Written 10 November 2026, 13:52 to 14:12 UK, Opus, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
PGE Polska Grupa Energetyczna (PGE-PL): answers to the first set of questions

Written 2026-11-09 for the Investment Analyst who builds the operating and financial model (second set). Money in PLN; "bn" is billion, "m" is million. Price used: PLN 15.755 (close 6 November 2026); market capitalisation about PLN 35.3bn on 2,243.7m shares.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes

Preface: source checks, and the judgements that carry the model

Source checks (read these before using any figure in the files)

  1. The latest Earnings Review is current. It covers the second quarter to 30 June 2026 (results published 16 September 2026) and also the Strategy 2035 update of 19-20 October 2026. The earlier review covers the first quarter of 2026 (results 27 May 2026). The instruction's caution is resolved. Next results: third quarter, 24 November 2026; PGE normally publishes preliminary figures about ten days before.
  2. The Leadership Foundations report misattributes the October 2026 strategy. It credits the capital-expenditure cut, the lower 2030 target and the dividend resumption to Dariusz Marzec. Marzec was removed on 8 December 2025; Dariusz Lubera has been President since 15 January 2026, and the October 2026 update is his. All chief-executive judgements below are on Lubera.
  3. The Change Forces report's driver "impact percentages" and its FY2022-FY2025 "guidance versus actual" table cannot be traced to company guidance. PGE publishes no annual guidance. Treat its beat and miss figures (+1.8%, +5.6%, +6.2%, -8.6%) as approximate comparisons with consensus.
  4. Do not use the Risks report's currency sensitivity. It claims a 10% fall in the zloty adds PLN 1.62bn of CO2 cost and moves EPS by 38.87%. That ignores two facts: PGE hedges 100% of the nominal value of its allowance purchases forward, and Polish coal plants still set the wholesale price in many hours, so much of the CO2 cost passes into the power price. Use the company's own sensitivities. A 10% rise in the CO2 price cuts EPS by about 6.85% (PLN 0.15). Every PLN 14.2 per tonne costs about PLN 220m of EBITDA. A 5% move in EUR/PLN moves EPS by about 3%; a 2% move in volume, about 5%; a 0.5-point move in EBIT margin, about 4.5%.
  5. FactSet's FY2025 actuals on the brokers' basis are unusable. Their revenue is PLN 42.9bn against PLN 61.4bn reported. Never compute consensus growth from them. FY2026 to FY2028 consensus rests on three or four brokers and FY2029 on one, so write "the brokers expect", not "consensus expects".
  6. FY2025 EBITDA exists on four bases:
Basis FY2025 EBITDA (PLNm)
Reported 11,427
Company recurring 12,892
FactSet operating 11,385
Brokers' basis 12,410

Model the company's recurring EBITDA by segment and carry the one-off items on a separate line.

  1. Use economic net debt, not FactSet's net debt, for leverage.
Date Company net debt Economic net debt (adds forward CO2 obligations)
End-2025 PLN 4.2bn PLN 15.8bn (1.23x trailing recurring EBITDA)
End-March 2026 PLN 7.1bn PLN 15.3bn (1.20x)
End-June 2026 PLN 1.5bn (FactSet) about 1.06x

FactSet's June figure is low because cash peaks (PLN 14.2bn) before the September CO2 surrender.

  1. Some figures conflict between sources. - The 2025 coal impairment appears as PLN 7.5bn, PLN 8,650m (Coal Energy segment) and PLN 8,967m (conventional energy). The parent company separately impaired PLN 12,050m of loans to PGE GiEK and wrote its shares in it to zero. - Smart-meter coverage at end-2025 appears as both 35% and 47.4%. - The distribution RAB growth target appears as both a 7.5% and a ">9%" CAGR to FY2030.

The eight judgements that carry the model (each is argued in the answers)

  1. FY2026 is a cyclical high point built on conditions that will not last. Four things lift it: - a gas-price shock that widened coal and gas spreads (gas +34% in H1 2026; the Tauron deputy chief executive says coal economics "improved significantly" year on year); - a cold first quarter; - five-year coal capacity contracts paying about PLN 2.25bn a year to end-2028; - a one-year supplementary capacity contract for 2027 (1,859 MW, about PLN 600m).

Recurring EBITDA was PLN 8.10bn in H1 2026, against about PLN 7.61bn in H1 2025 (+6.5%).

  1. Two clocks run in opposite directions. - Regulated and contracted earnings rise mechanically: distribution RAB growth of 7.5% to 9% a year; Baltica 2 (1.5 GW, 50% share) from the second half of 2027; Rybnik CCGT capacity payments of about PLN 300m a year from 2027; two gas peakers with PLN 0.5bn a year of 17-year, inflation-indexed capacity payments from about 2030. - Coal runs down to a cliff when the capacity contracts end at end-2028. Management's own plan has coal EBITDA at minus PLN 3.3bn in 2030.

The 18-month window catches the top. The four-year window catches the hand-over trough, most likely in 2029.

  1. The brokers' forecasts already describe this shape. - EBITDA stays flat at PLN 13.37bn, 13.34bn, 14.05bn and 14.09bn for FY2026-FY2029, while EPS falls from 2.90 to 2.60 to 2.47 as depreciation and interest rise. - The brokers' distribution EBITDA is flat (PLN 5.33bn, 5.17bn, 5.27bn) although the asset base grows 7.5% to 9% a year. That is the largest identifiable gap in the forecasts.
  2. Capital spending will run below the strategy's pace but above the brokers' figures by 2028. - Strategy: about PLN 19.5bn a year (PLN 117bn over 2025-2030). - Brokers: PLN 15.1bn, 14.0bn and 10.7bn for FY2026-FY2028. - Actual H1 2026: PLN 4.3bn.

Free cash flow stays negative through 2027. Economic leverage rises from about 1.1 times to about 2 to 2.5 times by 2029, still inside the 3.5-times ceiling.

  1. The dividend from FY2026 earnings (paid mid-2027) is the most novel positive change. The state needs it: the deficit is about 7% of GDP, and the State Assets minister called for higher dividends from state companies on 3 September 2026. Expect it to be paid, but from borrowed money while free cash flow is negative. Model PLN 0.55 to 0.80 a share; "up to 5%" equals about PLN 0.79 at today's price.
  2. Policy, not management, sets most of the earnings. Four dated risks: - the 2027 household tariff decision (December 2026); - a windfall levy in answer to the 2026 energy shock; - the parliamentary election (autumn 2027); - European approval, or not, of coal support after 2028.
  3. Earnings quality is poor and must be modelled in layers. - Start from recurring EBITDA. - Then the items that "do not recur" but appear most years: onerous-contract provisions, reclamation provisions, Price Difference Fund corrections. - Then the non-cash swings on the Baltica 2 contract for difference: in H1 2026, plus PLN 267m in EBITDA, minus PLN 249m and minus PLN 300m in finance costs. - Then a tax rate of about 23%.
  4. The share price and the brokers' figures have run ahead of the brokers' conviction. - P/E is 6.6 times. The 24-month forward P/E is at the 77th percentile of five years; the cohort is at the 95th percentile. - The shares are +40% over 12 months, at the 97th percentile for relative strength. - The mean target price (PLN 15.56) is below the share price. - The share of brokers rating it a buy is 29%, down 21 points in one month, while FY2027 EPS estimates rose 64% in three months.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: PGE Polska Grupa Energetyczna S.A. (PGE-PL, Warsaw), Poland's largest state-controlled integrated power group; segments: Distribution (regulated grid), District Heating, Coal Energy (lignite and hard-coal generation plus own lignite mines), Gas-fired Generation, Renewables (onshore, solar, storage, and offshore wind including the Baltica 2 joint venture with Ørsted), Supply (retail), and Railway Energy Services.

Request: T-P1

Peers:

  • TAURON Polska Energia: Poland's largest distributor, with a coal fleet and the same regulator.
  • Enea: integrated, with hard-coal mining and a falling distribution return.
  • Energa (Orlen subsidiary): distribution-heavy, with delayed gas plants.
  • ZE PAK: a private lignite generator closing its fleet, a precedent for lignite cost run-down.
  • CEZ: state-controlled Czech integrated utility with coal and grid.
  • Public Power Corporation (Greece): a former state lignite utility that exited coal by 2026 and grew regulated grids.
  • RWE: separated lignite and conventional assets from growth businesses.

  • LEAG (private, Germany): lignite mine-and-plant operator with phased closures. Inputs:

  • The split of fixed and variable costs in lignite mining and lignite and hard-coal generation (Bełchatów, Turów, Opole, Rybnik, Dolna Odra): mine operating costs, plant maintenance, staff, CO2, fuel. How do unit costs per MWh change as load factors fall?

  • Which coal and lignite costs disappear when a unit or mine closes, and which remain? Remaining costs include reclamation and decommissioning provisions (PGE's reclamation provisions rose by PLN 435m in 2025, company 2025 results), staff obligations, take-or-pay coal contracts (the Polska Grupa Górnicza contract signed 13 December 2023, PLN 13.5bn over 2023-2028, PGE annual report), and the Price Difference Fund settlements.
  • How did PGE's conventional segment EBITDA move with generation volumes and capacity payments in 2019-2026? This covers the 2022 price spike, the 2024-2025 weakness, and the 2026 rebound when generation rose 18% in Q2 2026 (company presentation, 16 September 2026).
  • How do Polish distribution tariffs turn asset-base growth into EBITDA? Cover the WACC formula (the 18-month average of 10-year government bond yields), the reinvestment premium, and any volume or true-up mechanism. Why did the 2026 tariff WACC fall? Tauron reported a 1.34-percentage-point reduction (Tauron 2026 reports). What do brokers expect for the 2027 and 2028 tariff WACC?
  • Workforce reduction programmes at PGE, Tauron, Enea and ZE PAK (voluntary leave programmes, natural retirement, union agreements): what did each promise and deliver, at what cost and over what period? PGE's headcount was 41,690 at end-2025 against 42,898 at end-2024; employee turnover was 10.2% (PGE 2025 annual report).
  • The size and timing of costs and cash outflows when coal units close, as seen in precedents: Public Power Corporation's lignite exit (2019-2026), RWE's and LEAG's German lignite closures, and CEZ's coal reductions.
  • Did depreciation of the coal fleet fall after the 2025 impairment? The coal fleet was largely written down in 2025 (company 2025 accounts). How large is the effect on segment EBIT from 2026, and how do brokers treat it?
  • Whether PGE's cost base outside coal (supply, overheads, digital customer systems) has shown any measurable savings since 2024, against management's stated retail margin improvement target (Strategy 2035 update, 20 October 2026). Focus: How much of PGE's coal and lignite cost base actually falls away as units and mines retire between 2026 and 2030, and how much stays? And how strongly does growth in the distribution asset base translate into distribution EBITDA under the current tariff formula?

Request: T-P2

Peers:

  • TAURON Polska Energia: the same household tariff regime and largest distributor.
  • Enea: the same regime, with retail and distribution exposure.
  • Energa: the same regime; booked onerous household-tariff provisions.
  • CEZ: a state-controlled utility in a liberalised retail market.
  • Public Power Corporation: had household price caps lifted in 2025 and lost retail share under legislation.
  • EVN (Austria): regulated grid returns under a windfall levy regime.
  • Endesa: Spanish regulated networks and retail under political price pressure.

  • Polenergia (private): Polish offshore and onshore contracts for difference and commercial supply. Operating conditions:

  • A gas and power price shock (2022 and 2026).

  • Falling wholesale prices with negative-price hours from solar.
  • Household price freezes and below-cost regulated household tariffs (2023-2026).
  • High inflation.
  • Fiscal stress and election years, when governments impose levies or revenue caps.

  • Rising government bond yields that feed the regulated distribution return formula. Inputs:

  • How the Energy Regulatory Office sets the household G tariff, and what is expected for 2027. The 2026 approved average was PLN 495.16/MWh, which management said does not cover costs, with an expected loss of about PLN 560m (company Q2 2026 presentation). How did the tariff process work in 2023-2026?

  • The Price Difference Fund and revenue caps of 2022-2025: how much did PGE and its peers contribute or provision (PGE provisioned PLN 605m, 2025 accounts)? Is any similar levy or cap on generators proposed in 2026-2027? Poland already taxes some 2026 fuel revenues above average at 60% (reported October 2026).
  • Distribution tariff increases approved for 2025-2027 for each Polish distributor, and the effect of WACC changes on each. PGE's 2026 distribution rate rose by PLN 3.5/MWh (company, early 2026).
  • Capacity-market clearing prices and contract terms in recent auctions, including the 2027 supplementary auction (PLN 325.56 to 372.04 per kW-year; PGE won 1,859 MW; Bernstein, 4 September 2026). What is known about capacity support for coal units after 2028 and the European Commission's position?
  • The inflation indexation of offshore contracts for difference: Baltica 2 at PLN 319.6/MWh in 2021 indexed to PLN 443.49/MWh in 2025 (Business Foundations sources); Baltica 9 at PLN 489/MWh (December 2025 auction). How do the euro indexation and the embedded derivative work?
  • Business customer migration: PGE's Tariff A sales fell 25% and Tariff B 15% in Q1 2026 (company Q1 2026 presentation). What prices do Polish industrial buyers pay under corporate power purchase agreements and self-generation, against grid supply?
  • District heating tariff approval lags in Poland when gas and CO2 costs rise, and how quickly costs were recovered in 2022-2026.
  • Precedents in central and southern Europe where governments clawed back utility profits (Austria's 95% levy on excess generation profits, Hungary, the Czech Republic, Romania, Greece). What did utilities recover afterwards? Focus: Can PGE recover its costs through the regulated tariffs it depends on (household, distribution, heat) in 2027-2028? Or will the state cap or claw back returns after the 2026 energy shock and ahead of the 2027 parliamentary election, as it did in 2022-2024?

Request: T-P3

Why requested: PGE's earnings to 2030 depend on several interacting loops. Renewables displace coal. Network costs push industry to self-generate. The state's fiscal needs drive both dividends and levies. Bond yields feed the regulated return. Together these loops could amplify or offset each other. Peers:

  • TAURON Polska Energia: same market and loops, weaker balance sheet.
  • Enea: coal mining exposure.
  • Energa (Orlen): high renewables share on its grid.
  • CEZ: state-controlled coal and grid utility.
  • Public Power Corporation: curtailment and price cannibalisation in Greece.
  • Iberdrola: grid growth with regulated returns linked to bond yields.

  • Fortum: power-price exposure. Inputs:

  • Coal displacement: solar and battery build lowers daytime and negative prices and raises curtailment. Coal running hours fall and unit costs rise, leading to earlier retirements and more reliance on capacity payments. Poland had more than 450 negative-price hours in 2025 and more than 1 TWh of renewable curtailment (industry sources, 2026).

  • Network costs and self-generation: network investment raises network charges per kWh, so industrial customers self-generate or sign power purchase agreements. Grid-supplied volume then falls and charges rise further. The offsetting effect: self-generators still need grid connections and backup.
  • The state's fiscal position and its companies: a general government deficit near 7% of GDP (Bank Pekao, October 2026). The State Assets minister expects higher dividends from state companies (Polish Press Agency, 3 September 2026). What is the likely effect on levies, dividends, utility leverage and credit ratings?
  • Bond yields and regulated returns: higher Polish government bond yields feed, with a lag, into the regulated distribution WACC formula. This is a balancing loop that could offset fiscal stress.
  • Energy-price windfalls and political clawback: a gas-price shock raises generator profits, which raises the political pressure for levies or caps.
  • Batteries in capacity and balancing markets: more batteries lower clearing prices, which weakens the economics of new gas peakers and late battery entrants. Poland has about 5 GW of batteries that won availability auctions in three years (industry sources, 2026).
  • Data-centre connection requests (PGE reports 2 GW, Strategy 2035 update, 20 October 2026) and whether they convert into connected load, raising network investment and revenue. Focus: Which of these loops are measurably operating in Poland now? Are the network-cost-to-self-generation loop and the fiscal-stress-to-levy loop strong enough to change PGE's earnings over 2027-2030?

Request: T-P4

Why requested: PGE's bridge from EBITDA to earnings per share is unusually complex, and reported, company-adjusted and broker-adjusted EPS differ widely. The complexity comes from post-impairment depreciation, contract-for-difference derivative revaluations, preferential loans, capitalised interest, CO2 obligations counted in "economic net debt", and a new dividend policy. Peers:

  • TAURON Polska Energia: similar accounting and funding.
  • Enea: preferential state loans for grids.
  • CEZ: state-controlled dividend policy.
  • Public Power Corporation: an adjusted-earnings definition with recurring adjustments.
  • Ørsted: Baltica 2 partner, with project accounting and contract-for-difference treatment.

  • RWE: depreciation path after conventional write-downs. Inputs:

  • Depreciation and amortisation by segment, after the 2025 coal impairment and as Baltica 2, the Rybnik CCGT, the Żarnowiec battery and new grid assets enter service. What do brokers assume?

  • The accounting for the Baltica 2 joint venture (joint operation or equity method): where its EBITDA, debt and capital spending appear in PGE's accounts.
  • The Baltica 2 embedded derivative revaluations and how brokers treat them in adjusted EPS. Q1 2026: minus PLN 249m in finance costs. Q2 2026: plus PLN 267m in EBITDA and minus PLN 300m in finance costs (company presentations, 27 May and 16 September 2026).
  • Net interest: the fixed-rate share of debt (77% in 2025, PGE annual report), the 0.5% recovery-plan loans, capitalised borrowing costs on projects under construction, and the unwinding of provisions within financial costs. How will these change as net debt rises?
  • The tax rate (23.02% effective in Q1 2026, company) and any proposed change to Polish corporate income tax.
  • Minority interests.
  • The dividend policy. The October 2026 strategy targets a yield of up to 5% from FY2026 earnings, subject to leverage and coal market conditions, calculated on net profit excluding non-cash impairments. How is the base defined, how does the state shareholder vote, and what is the 2016 precedent?
  • Capital spending phasing against the PLN 117bn target to 2030 (Strategy 2035 update, 20 October 2026).
  • CO2 settlement timing and economic net debt.
  • Reverse factoring (PLN 1,574m at end-2025, PGE annual report).
  • Share count and any risk of equity issuance, given the 2022 capital increase.
  • How each named broker defines adjusted EPS (Bernstein, Trigon, Erste, PKO BP, BOS, Wood & Company, Noble), and why their FY2026-FY2028 estimates differ. Focus: Build a reliable bridge from PGE's recurring EBITDA to adjusted EPS and dividend per share for 2026-2029. It should explain how depreciation, net interest, contract-for-difference revaluations, tax and minorities will move, and how each broker's adjusted EPS definition differs.

Layer 2: Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

Written 10 November 2026, 18:03 to 18:19 UK, Opus, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's title
PGE Polska Grupa Energetyczna (PGE-PL): answers to the second set of questions (financial forecasts)

Written 10 November 2026 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets. Money in PLN; "bn" is billion, "m" is million. Price PLN 15.755 (close 6 November 2026); 2,243.7m shares. Fiscal year = calendar year.

Bases used throughout (read before using any figure)

  • EBITDA is the company's recurring EBITDA unless stated. It is the measure closest to the brokers' basis (FY2025: recurring 12,892m; brokers' basis 12,410m; reported 11,427m; FactSet operating 11,385m).
  • EPS is my adjusted EPS: recurring EBITDA, less depreciation, less net financial items excluding the Baltica 2 contract-for-difference (CfD) derivative revaluations, less tax, less about PLN 100m of minorities, over 2,243.7m shares. It is comparable with the brokers' adjusted EPS. Reported EPS will be lower every year by PLN 0.10 to 0.27 (the recurring "one-off" items).
  • Free cash flow (FCF) is operating cash flow less capital spending less lease payments, before dividends and acquisitions.
  • Economic net debt adds the forward CO2 obligations (about PLN 11.6bn at end-2025) to company net debt.
  • Dividend per share (DPS) is shown in the year it is paid (FactSet's convention): the dividend from FY2026 earnings is paid in 2027.

The six judgements that carry the model

  1. FY2026 is banked and slightly above the brokers. H1 2026 recurring EBITDA was PLN 8.10bn. My FY2026 of PLN 13.75bn needs H2 2026 of PLN 5.65bn, only +7% on H2 2025 (PLN 5.28bn), after Q2 2026 grew +21%. The brokers' PLN 13.37bn implies a flat H2; Trigon (14.44bn) and BOS (13.96bn) are already above it.
  2. EBITDA is flat for four years but EPS roughly halves, and most of the fall is mechanical. EPS falls from PLN 2.82 (FY2026) to PLN 1.50 (FY2029). About 72% of that fall comes from below EBITDA: depreciation (+1.5bn), net interest (+1.1bn) and the corporate income tax rise. Those three are the most predictable parts of the forecast. The coal cliff supplies the other 28%.
  3. The brokers' FY2027-FY2028 EPS is about 7% to 8% too high, and their FY2029 EBITDA about 10% too high. Three reasons: the corporate income tax rise to 22% from 1 January 2027 (passed by the Senate on 6 November 2026, after the latest broker estimates of 16-21 October); coal spreads normalising after the 2026 gas shock; and interest on debt-funded capital spending and dividends. The single broker visible for FY2029 (EBITDA 14.09bn) does not show the coal cliff; I have 12.65bn.
  4. Distribution is the main offset, and the brokers' flat line is explained, not an oversight. I disagree in part with the first set. The brokers' flat distribution EBITDA (PLN 5.17bn to 5.33bn) embeds a falling allowed return (Bernstein: 12.96% in 2025 to 8.0% by 2030; Noble: Enea 9.35% to 7.5% by 2028). I model +4% a year (PLN 5.70bn in FY2026 to 6.70bn in FY2030), because the asset base grows 7% to 8% a year and the 2026 bond sell-off (ten-year yield about 6.4% in October 2026) feeds the return formula from the 2027-2028 tariffs.
  5. Cash is negative every year and the dividend is borrowed; the leverage ceiling binds in 2029-2030. Cumulative FCF FY2026-FY2030 is about minus PLN 22bn; dividends add PLN 5.3bn. Economic leverage reaches about 2.9 times in 2029 and 3.2 times in 2030, against the company's 3.5-times ceiling and Fitch's 3.7-times trigger. Dividend and capital spending are the release valves from 2029; equity issuance becomes a live risk only then.
  6. Policy is the scenario axis, and its skew is negative for 18 months. Downside (a PLN 1bn levy in 2027 plus a PLN 0.3bn wider household tariff gap) takes FY2027 EPS from 2.43 to about 2.00. Upside after 2028 (about PLN 1.5bn of coal support plus one point more of distribution return) adds about PLN 0.60 to FY2029-FY2030 EPS.

Where I differ from the first set's answers

  • Tax: the first set used about 23%. The corporate income tax rate rises from 19% to 22% from 1 January 2027 (KPMG, 10 November 2026; the President's signature is still needed). I use 23% for FY2026 and 25.5% from FY2027. A draft with up to 30% for energy companies (Garrigues, 28 October 2026) is a downside scenario.
  • Leverage: the first set had economic leverage of 2.0 to 2.5 times by 2029. With the coal cliff in 2029 EBITDA, the dividend and the tax rise, I find about 2.9 times, even with capital spending held at PLN 14bn in 2029.
  • FY2029 EPS: the first set had a trough of PLN 1.8 to 2.5. I have PLN 1.50 (higher tax and interest than the first set assumed).
  • FY2026 capital spending: the first set and the brokers have about PLN 15bn. H1 2026 was PLN 4.3bn; I use PLN 13.0bn.
  • The size of the coal capacity cliff is less certain than the first set implied. Bernstein: conventional units earned PLN 2.25bn of capacity revenue in 2024 (this may include gas units such as Dolna Odra's PLN 350m). Fitch (28 September 2026): coal capacity revenues average PLN 914m a year in 2026-2027 and PLN 534m a year in 2028-2030. The two cannot both describe the same perimeter. My 2028-to-2029 coal step-down is PLN 2.1bn; the defensible range is PLN 1.0bn to 2.5bn.
  • Cost programme: the first set found no structural cost programme. The October 2026 strategy has an "Efficiency Improvement Programme" that reduces forecast EBITDA losses by 35% in 2030 and 53% in 2035 against previous forecasts. It most plausibly refers to the coal losses and is inside management's minus PLN 3.3bn 2030 coal figure (see P-03).

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

PGE reports recurring EBITDA for eight segments. Distribution is the only one with a stable margin (about 48% of segment revenue: PLN 5.3bn of EBITDA on PLN 11.0bn of revenue in 2025). Segment revenue is distorted by intra-group sales and wholesale pass-through, so the inputs are built from segment EBITDA.

FY2025 by segment is partly unavailable in the sources. Distribution (41% of the group) and renewables are from company data. Heat and railway are approximate.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

Revenue

  • Revenue is mostly pass-through of wholesale power, fuel and CO2.
  • FY2026 (+6.7%): H1 +5.6% actual (Q1 +1.7%, Q2 +10.6%). H2 lifted by the gas-price shock and higher wholesale prices.
  • FY2027 (+3%): the January 2027 household tariff rise; 2027 base forward PLN 590/MWh; Baltica 2 first power. Partly offset by business customer losses (-5% to -10% a year) and lower coal output.
  • FY2028-FY2029 (-0.5%, -3%): wholesale prices normalise; coal output falls (Dolna Odra coal units close 2026; Rybnik 2027-2028; solar 25.6 GW); Baltica 2 adds about PLN 1.4bn a year of revenue at full output.
  • FY2030 (+1.5%): indexation and new assets.

EBITDA: two clocks

  • Regulated and contracted, rising:
  • distribution grows about 4% a year: asset base +7% to 8% a year; allowed return falls only gently from 12.96% in 2025 because the 2026 rise in Polish ten-year yields (about 6.4% in October 2026) feeds the 18-month formula;
  • Baltica 2 from 2027: first power H1 2027, full operation end-2027, about 3 TWh a year PGE share at about PLN 470 to 490/MWh indexed;
  • Rybnik CCGT capacity payments of about PLN 300m a year from 2027;
  • Żarnowiec battery from Q2 2027;
  • gas peakers of about PLN 0.5bn a year from around 2030.
  • Coal, falling:
  • 2026 is lifted by the gas shock;
  • the 2027 supplementary capacity payment (1,859 MW, about PLN 600m) lapses after 2027;
  • the five-year coal capacity contracts end in 2028;
  • from 2029 coal is negative. I assume about PLN 0.5bn a year of replacement support. Management assumes minus PLN 3.3bn in 2030, Bernstein minus PLN 1.8bn.
  • Lignite costs are largely fixed, so they do not fall with volume.
  • Supply: improves slowly as the household tariff moves towards cost, but stays below cost through the 2027 election.
  • Policy scenarios are not in the base: a windfall levy of PLN 0.5bn to 1.5bn a year in 2027-2028; a wider household tariff gap; a higher energy-sector tax.

Below EBITDA

  • Depreciation: rises with grid spending of about PLN 6bn to 7bn a year, Rybnik, storage and Baltica 2. Coal depreciation is already near PLN 60m a quarter after the 2025 write-down.
  • Net financial items: about 5% on average company net debt, plus about PLN 450m of provision unwinding. 77% of debt is fixed; about PLN 14bn of recovery-plan loans are at 0.5%; Baltica 2 capitalised interest ends at completion.
  • Tax: 23%, then 25.5% (22% statutory plus about four points of leakage).
  • Minorities: about PLN 100m a year.

Cash and capital

  • Capital spending: below the strategy's pace (PLN 117bn for 2025-2030) because H1 2026 was only PLN 4.3bn, and because the 3.5-times leverage ceiling binds by 2029-2030. Above the brokers' PLN 10.7bn for FY2028.
  • Dividend: resumes at PLN 0.70 paid in 2027 (25% of FY2026 adjusted EPS; "up to 5%" is about PLN 0.79). It falls as leverage rises and coal turns negative.
  • No buybacks, no new shares in the base. Equity issuance is a 2029-2030 risk.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

PGE gives no annual guidance. Its October 2026 Strategy 2035 update gives 2030 targets, a capital plan, a dividend policy and a leverage ceiling.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

EBITDA (FY2025, PLN m)

  • Four figures: reported 11,427 (company); FactSet operating 11,385 (the model's history line, about equal to reported); company recurring 12,892; brokers' basis (FactSet actual on the estimates basis) 12,410.
  • Reported to recurring: recurring excludes onerous-contract provisions (2025: coal PLN 766m, retail PLN 316m), Price Difference Fund provisions (PLN 605m), reclamation provision changes, settlements (Turów release +PLN 135m in Q2 2026) and the Baltica 2 CfD revaluation booked in EBITDA (+PLN 267m in Q2 2026).
  • Recurring to brokers: the brokers' basis was about PLN 480m below recurring in FY2025. The brokers do not all use the same definition; the gap is not explained in the sources.
  • My EBITDA inputs are recurring. Compare them with consensus EBITDA (13,370 / 13,344 / 14,048) directly, and expect reported EBITDA PLN 0.3bn to 0.8bn lower.

Revenue

  • FactSet's FY2025 brokers'-basis revenue (PLN 42,942m) does not reconcile with reported PLN 61,434m. Some brokers net out trading or pass-through revenue: Erste has PLN 55bn for FY2026, BOS PLN 61bn to 76bn.
  • Use reported revenue as the base. Read the consensus median (64,648 / 66,547 / 66,500) as roughly reported-basis.
  • Never compute consensus growth from the brokers'-basis actuals for this stock.

EPS

  • Four bases: reported basic (FY2025 -1.56); FactSet adjusted diluted (1.60); Viewforth definition (3.00); brokers' adjusted (consensus 2.90 / 2.60 / 2.47 for FY2026-FY2028, three brokers: Trigon, Erste, PKO).
  • Brokers' adjusted EPS excludes impairments and, at least for Bernstein, the CfD derivative revaluations and provisions.
  • Bernstein's own figures (EPS 2.19 and 1.89; EBITDA 12.1bn and 11.9bn) are on a lower EBITDA basis and are not in the FactSet median.
  • My EPS is adjusted: recurring EBITDA less depreciation, less net financial items excluding CfD revaluations, less tax, less PLN 100m of minorities. It is comparable with the brokers' adjusted EPS. Reported EPS will be lower by about PLN 0.10 to 0.27 a year (the one-off allowance) and will swing with the CfD revaluations (±PLN 0.3bn a quarter).

Net debt

  • FactSet net debt includes leases and peaks and troughs with CO2 timing: cash peaks in June before the September surrender.
  • The company's economic net debt adds forward CO2 obligations (end-2025: company PLN 4.2bn; economic PLN 15.8bn).
  • Reverse factoring (PLN 1,574m at end-2025) is a debt-like item outside both measures.
  • Use economic net debt for the 3.5-times test.

Dividend timing

  • FactSet records DPS in the year paid: consensus 0.55 in FY2027 is the dividend from FY2026 earnings. My inputs follow the same convention.
